   Trading Literature Atlas
For Semi-Automated Systematic Trading


       Sohhom Bandyopadhyay

          October 18, 2026
                                            Abstract

Research that matters for systematic trading is scattered across communities that rarely read
one another. Time-series classification, deep learning, empirical finance, market microstructure,
derivatives, rough-path mathematics, statistics, decision theory, language models and practitioner
writing each study objects that a trading desk needs, such as regimes, labels, liquidity, cross-
sections and search over signals, but they name them differently and often study them in isolation.
This manuscript is a contribution in bridge-building. It builds a common vocabulary so that
traders can find better alphas and so that mathematicians and machine-learning researchers can
see where their skills apply in a hard, noisy, adversarial domain. The setting throughout is a
semi-automated systematic fund.
    The evidence base is a Zotero collection of 1065 papers. An automated classification of titles
and abstracts assigned each paper to one of 11 research silos, to one or more of 11 stages of a
fund’s pipeline, and to concept tags. Fourteen topical chapters, one per cluster of the collection,
review the papers on their own terms and close with bridge notes that translate jargon between
silos. Desk notes turn ideas into inputs, outputs and failure modes for a trading desk. A shared
vocabulary, a set of cross-silo bridges, a blueprint of the fund and a list of open gaps tie the
chapters together.
    The reader gets a map of the literature indexed by the stages of a real pipeline, a glossary
that translates between communities, a set of concrete cross-silo proposals, and an honest account
of what the method can and cannot claim. Silo and stage labels rest on abstracts, not full texts,
and they are produced by an automated classifier. Synthesis and proposals are ours, not the
cited authors’.

                                                 5
   Part I

Orientation




     6
Chapter 1

Introduction: A Library in Shards

1.1     A library in shards
A systematic fund is a pipeline. Data come in, features are built, signals and forecasts are
produced, the market regime and the risk budget are assessed, a portfolio is sized, orders are
executed, and the whole chain is validated and, increasingly, automated. Almost every link in
that chain has a research literature. The difficulty is that the literatures do not form one body of
knowledge. They form shards, each held together by a shared training, a shared set of benchmark
data and a shared dialect.
    The collection behind this manuscript makes the point plainly. It holds 1065 papers on trading,
gathered over time in a Zotero collection called trading. They span 1975 to 2027, but the mass
is recent: 597 carry a year of 2024 or later and 500 a year of 2025 or later (counted from the
year field of our records, whose latest value is 2027). When the papers are sorted by the research
community that they appear to belong to, eleven silos emerge, from time-series machine learning
(180 papers) and deep learning (176) through empirical finance and econometrics (136), market
microstructure (115), decision theory and reinforcement learning (104), statistics (95), language
models and agents (88), practitioner writing (55), rough-path and topological mathematics (49),
derivatives (43) and work imported from outside finance (24). Chapter 2 defines these silos. For
now the relevant fact is that the silos differ in what they publish, how they validate and which
words they use, and that a desk needs the output of all of them.

1.1.1    One object, many names
The clearest symptom of a silo is a single underlying object that carries a different name, a
different mathematical form and a different evaluation protocol in each community. Five examples
from the collection show the pattern. In each we point to representative papers. We did not
trace citation links between them, so we claim only what the titles and abstracts show: that
each community describes the object in its own terms.

The market that changes. Statisticians treat the problem as change-point detection. Habibi
(2022) introduce Bayesian online change-point detection to finance, with its focus on abrupt
changes in the generative parameters of a series. Market-microstructure researchers speak of
structural breaks and regimes in the order flow, and ask how to identify them in real time for
trading and risk systems (Jebali, 2026). Time-series data miners speak of discovering multiple
phases of dynamics in multivariate series (Wang and Hsieh, 2021). A mathematics-side paper
embeds persistent homology in a Markov regime-switching filter to detect volatility regimes
earlier (Faris, 2026). Four silos, four vocabularies (change point, structural break, phase, regime),
one operational question: when should the model, the risk limit or the execution schedule be
re-estimated?


                                                 7
The target. Supervised learning needs a label, and the label is rarely a given. A time-series
group shows that, holding the model fixed, the choice of target changes the results of stock
selection (Guida and Coqueret, 2026). A derivatives group labels intraday bars by the repriced
profit of an option position, in place of price direction (Vaghela, 2026). A practitioner study
documents a failure mode in which success at predicting triple-barrier labels does not carry over
to portfolio performance (Lima, 2026). Decision theorists have a name for the underlying issue,
which is to train the predictor for the decision that it feeds (Caunhye et al., 2026; Zhang and Li,
2026). These are one problem under four headings: label design, target engineering, label-to-P&L
gap, and decision-focused learning.

Who moves with whom. An econometrician tests whether one sectoral index Granger-causes
another (Sahoo and Kumar, 2025), in the line of the classic collected econometrics papers (Granger
et al., 2001). A time-series data-mining paper builds lead–lag relationships into the selection of
pairs (Gupta and Chatterjee, 2020). A deep-learning paper models delayed dependence between
stocks with a graph neural stochastic differential equation (You et al., 2026). A microstructure
study finds that the aggressive trades of high-frequency traders lead those of other investors
(Hirschey, 2021). Lead–lag, Granger causality, delayed graph dependence and order anticipation
are four views of directed information flow.

The correlation graph. A time-series paper finds that a graph built from stock correlations
carries an economic taxonomy (Mantegna, 1998). Another asks how well correlation-filtering
methods perform on noisy estimates (Millington and Sánchez Granero, 2022). An empirical-
finance paper examines partial correlation as an alternative to the Pearson coefficient for equity
co-movement (Kenett et al., 2014). A deep-learning paper learns the financial network instead of
taking it as given, in an asset-pricing model (Uddin et al., 2021). A mathematical paper allocates
a portfolio from a network built from path signatures (Gregnanin et al., 2024). A desk could use
any of these, and could not easily tell that they are answers to the same question.

The search over signals. Large-language-model agents explore a space of formulaic alphas
with memory of the search history (Yu et al., 2026e). Evolutionary algorithms do the same
without language models (Zhang et al., 2020). Reinforcement-learning researchers recast alpha
discovery as sequential decision-making (Zhu and Zhu, 2025). Scientific-computing researchers
call the procedure symbolic regression (Cranmer, 2023). A statistician bounds the false discovery
rate among published cross-sectional predictors (Chen, 2022). Every one of these is a search over
a combinatorial space with noisy feedback. Only one of them sets the multiplicity of the search
at the centre of its measure of success.
Bridge. Why this is a bridge problem and not a survey problem. A conventional
review would take each of these five objects, pick the community that looks most natural, and
review it. The shards would survive. Our aim is to place the five stories next to one another, to
give each object one shared term, and to say for each community what it could take from the
others.


1.2     Who this is for
Two readers motivate this manuscript. They need different things and should enter at different
places.

1.2.1    Portfolio managers and quantitative researchers
You know the pipeline and the cost of a mistake in it. You may not know that a method you need
exists under an unfamiliar name, in a venue you do not read. Use the manuscript as a lookup.

                                                8
Start from the stage of your pipeline that is giving you trouble, find it in the silo-by-stage table
of Chapter 2, read the topical chapter that covers it, and look at the desk notes. Each desk note
gives a stage, an input, an output and a list of things that can go wrong, so that the idea can be
tried in a notebook before anyone is convinced by it. Then read the bridge notes at the end of
the chapter, which say which other chapter may hold a tool you are missing. If you manage a
team, Chapter 18 assembles the pieces into a blueprint, and Chapter 19 lists what the literature
does not yet cover.

1.2.2    Mathematicians and machine-learning researchers
You know the tools: signatures, topology, change-point theory, conformal prediction, foundation
models, decision-focused learning. You may not know which problem in trading is shaped to fit
them, or which of the problem’s difficulties (non-stationarity, tiny signal-to-noise, adversarial
counterparties, selection bias over many trials) will break a method that works well on a standard
benchmark. Start with the vocabulary chapter and its translation table, which maps your terms
onto the trader’s. Then read the chapters of the parts on Representation and Pattern and
on Research Operations, which are written for the reader who wants to know what a correct
evaluation looks like. The key lesson of the Research Operations part is that a result in this
domain is only as good as its validation: look first at the backtesting and leakage material in
Chapter 14 before you invest in an architecture.


1.3     How the atlas was built
The method has four steps, and each of them has a limit that we state here and will repeat when
it matters.

  1. Collection. The starting point is a Zotero collection called trading, with sub-collections
     that group papers by topic. It holds 1065 papers. The collection is a convenience sample
     of what the owner found or was sent. It is not a systematic search, and its coverage is
     uneven. We make no claim to completeness, and the counts in this manuscript describe
     the collection, not the field.

  2. Automated classification. Every paper was read by a language-model classifier, in
     batches, from its title and abstract. For each paper the classifier assigned one of 11 silos,
     one or more of 11 pipeline stages (775 papers received one stage, 290 received two or
     three), a set of concept tags (122 distinct tags in all), a relevance score, a one-line desk use
     and a one-line cross-silo bridge. The classifier read abstracts only. Seven abstracts in the
     collection are shorter than 200 characters, six of them empty, so the labels for those papers
     lean on the title. A paper’s silo is a judgement about its dialect, and borderline papers
     could be assigned either way. We did not manually audit every label.

  3. Chapters. Papers were grouped by topic, using the sub-collections of the library as a
     guide, into 14 topical chapters, from 29 papers (market structure) to 134 (microstructure).
     Each chapter was written from the abstracts of its papers. Within a chapter, we report
     what a paper’s abstract says and attribute only that; the synthesis, the proposals and the
     gap-filling are our own and are worded as such.

  4. Synthesis. The final part draws the chapters together: a catalogue of cross-silo bridges, a
     blueprint for a semi-automated fund and an inventory of gaps.




                                                 9
Desk note. Limits of the method. (1) Grounded in titles and abstracts, not full texts: a
method that is described briefly in an abstract may be treated more generously or more harshly
than the paper deserves, and details such as costs, universes and test periods are often missing
from abstracts. (2) The classification is automated and the labels are noisy, so a count by silo or
stage is an estimate and not a measurement. (3) Authors are recorded by surname only, and
the reference list carries surnames only, so we make no claim about who works in which silo,
and we do not trace author networks or citation links. (4) Many abstracts report in-sample or
gross results. We repeat what the abstract states and flag when the validation is unknown. (5)
Sections that propose a design are proposals. They are not findings of the cited papers.


1.4     A roadmap
The manuscript has six parts. The structure follows the pipeline of a fund, from its inputs to its
governance, and ends with a synthesis.

Orientation. This chapter and Chapter 2, which defines the silos and stages, tabulates where
the papers sit, and offers a translation table of shared concepts across silos.

Data and Substrate. Before any signal there is data, and before a market exists in the
lab there is a simulator. Chapter 3 reviews synthetic data, generative models and agent-based
simulators as a training and testing ground. Chapter 4 describes the participants, rules and
reforms that produce the data in the first place, and how they create natural experiments.

Representation and Pattern. Chapter 5 covers time-series forecasting, classification and
mining, with similarity search and motifs as a shared engine. Chapter 6 covers the representations
(hand-built features, signatures, topology, self-supervised encoders and foundation models) that
turn a path into something a model can use. Chapter 7 covers regimes, change points, networks
and systemic risk.

Alpha. Four chapters deal with the sources of expected return. Chapter 8 reviews return
forecasting with deep sequence and graph models. Chapter 9 reviews factors, anomalies and
relative value, including the mining of formulaic alphas. Chapter 10 reviews trading strategies
and signals, from technical rules to reinforcement-learning traders. Chapter 11 reviews volatility
and derivatives, where option-implied information feeds back into equity signals.

Implementation. Chapter 12 deals with order books, market impact and execution, and
Chapter 13 with portfolio construction and decisions under uncertainty. This is where a forecast
becomes a position and a position becomes a fill.

Research Operations. Chapter 14 deals with labels, backtesting, overfitting and the statistics
of selection. Chapter 15 deals with language models, text and agentic research. Chapter 16 deals
with general methods imported from other domains: anomaly detection, change-point methods,
symbolic regression, validation tools and the automation of the research loop.

Synthesis. Chapter 17 collects the cross-silo bridges that the topical chapters propose and
develops twelve of them into research proposals. Chapter 18 assembles them into a blueprint
of a semi-automated systematic fund. Chapter 19 lists the gaps and open problems that the
collection leaves, and concludes.
    Two conventions run through the topical chapters. Each ends with a section on bridges out
of the chapter, and each contains desk notes. Chapter 2 explains the boxes before the reader


                                                10
meets them. Citations use author–year form, and every attributed statement is limited to what
the cited abstract supports.




                                             11
Chapter 2

A Shared Vocabulary

A bridge needs two banks. This chapter fixes the shared terms that every later chapter uses:
the eleven research communities (silos) that the collection falls into, the eleven stages of a
semi-automated systematic fund against which each paper is placed, and a translation table, a
Rosetta stone, between the dialects in which the same object is described. It also explains the
two kinds of box that run through the topical chapters.


2.1     Eleven silos
A silo, in this manuscript, is a research dialect: a community with its own training, benchmark
problems, venues and words. It is not a verdict on quality. The eleven labels below were assigned
to each paper by an automated classification of its title and abstract (one label per paper), so
each is a judgement about which dialect the paper speaks. Counts in parentheses are the number
of papers in our collection of 1065.

tsml (180): time-series machine learning and data mining.
  Similarity search, motifs and discords, shapelets, clustering and classification of series, labelling
  schemes and feature libraries. The default question is how to find structure in a sequence.

dl (176): deep learning and foundation models.
   Sequence, attention and graph networks, self-supervised encoders, generative models, pre-
   trained forecasters. The default question is how to learn the mapping from history to a
   target.

fin-econ (136): empirical finance and econometrics.
   Anomalies, factor models, event studies, volatility models, flows and behavioural effects, often
   on a single national market. The default question is whether an effect is priced and why.

microstructure (115): market microstructure, order books and execution.
  Spreads, depth, order flow, price discovery, impact, auctions and the behaviour of high-
  frequency participants. The default question is how trades form prices.

decision (104): decision theory, control and reinforcement learning.
  Optimal execution, stochastic control, portfolio allocation, predict-then-optimise, reinforcement-
  learning traders. The default question is what to do given a belief.

stats (95): statistical testing, change-point and Bayesian methods.
   Hypothesis tests, forecast comparison, change-point detection, uncertainty sets and point
   processes. The default question is how sure we are.

nlp-llm (88): text, language models and agents.
  News and filings as features, language-model reasoning, alpha-mining agents, multi-agent

                                                  12
  trading systems, security of agents. The default question is what a language model can read
  or do.

practice (55): practitioner and research-methodology writing.
  Backtesting practice, platforms, walk-forward protocols, overfitting diagnostics, discretionary-
  rule write-ups. The default question is what survives contact with a real desk.

math (49): rough paths, topology, algebra and geometry.
 Path signatures, persistent homology, geometric and information-theoretic constructions. The
 default question is what the right invariant of a path is.

quant-deriv (43): derivatives, volatility modelling and mathematical finance.
  Implied-volatility surfaces, risk premia, option pricing and hedging, scenario P&L. The default
  question is what the option market implies.

other-domain (24): work imported from outside finance.
  Root-cause analysis in software systems, anomaly detection in medicine, control charts,
  symbolic regression in physics. The default question is whether a tool built elsewhere transfers.


2.2     Eleven pipeline stages
The second axis follows a semi-automated systematic fund from its inputs to its governance.
Each paper was assigned one or more stages (a paper may serve two, for instance a labelling
scheme that is both a data and a forecast tool). The assignment is again automated and rests on
the abstract.

data.
  Acquisition, cleaning, sampling and labelling of market data, including simulated and synthetic
  data.

features.
   Transformations and representations that turn raw series into model inputs: indicators,
   embeddings, signatures, topological summaries.

signal.
   A rule or score that proposes a trade or ranks assets: technical rules, factor scores, mined
   formulaic alphas, event signals.

forecast.
  A model that predicts a return, a direction, a volatility or a distribution.

regime.
  Detecting that the market state has changed: change points, latent states, early-warning
  indicators.

portfolio.
  Turning forecasts and signals into positions: allocation, sizing, constraints, decision-focused
  learning.

execution.
  Turning positions into fills: order placement, schedules, market impact and transaction-cost
  analysis.

risk.
   Measuring and limiting loss: volatility, tails, drawdown, liquidity stress and systemic contagion.

Table 2.1: Silo by pipeline stage: number of papers (a paper with several stages is counted once
per stage). Silo and stage labels come from an automated classification of titles and abstracts. A
blank cell is zero.

validation.
  Testing whether a result is real: backtests, labels, out-of-sample protocols, multiple-testing
  and leakage controls.

automation.
  Making the research loop run itself: agents, platforms, harnesses and tooling.

theory.
  Mathematical or conceptual foundations that do not yet sit in one operational stage.

    The topical chapters often abbreviate this list to data, features, signal, regime and risk,
portfolio, execution, validation and research automation. That shorthand folds forecast into
signal, pairs regime with risk and leaves theory implicit; it is the same pipeline. Chapter 18 uses
a third, operational cut of ten stages, which it maps back onto these.


2.3     Where the literature sits
Table 2.1 cross-tabulates the two axes. Because a paper can carry up to three stages, the cells
add to 1357 stage assignments over 1065 papers (775 papers have one stage, 290 have two or
three).

Where the mass sits. The signal stage is the largest (288 assignments), followed by forecast
(206) and validation (167); portfolio (68), regime (59) and automation (46) are the thinnest, with
execution (76) close behind. The silos pile up in different places. Deep learning concentrates on
forecasting: 113 of its 235 stage assignments, and 113 of the 206 forecast assignments overall, are
its own. Time-series machine learning is the main supplier of data-stage work (66 assignments,
much of it labelling and sampling) and also contributes to signal and feature construction.
Microstructure is the home of execution (39) and a major supplier of risk work (24). Statistics
and practitioner writing hold the validation stage (39 and 30). Language-model papers cluster
in signal (47), where alpha-mining agents sit. Mathematics is the one silo whose largest cell is
theory (22 of 60).

Where it is empty, and why that matters. Fourteen of the 121 cells are zero, and 23 more
hold one or two papers. The zeros are informative because each marks a hand-off that no one

                                                               14
in the collection is working on, at least in a way that the classifier could see. Some examples:
no derivatives paper is placed in execution, portfolio, features or automation, so option-implied
information is largely not carried through to sizing and order placement; no microstructure
paper is placed in portfolio, so cost curves reach allocators through other silos if at all; no
language-model paper is placed in regime or execution, so agents that trade are rarely paired
with change-point detectors; decision-theory papers have no data or feature stage, so control
formulations assume their inputs; and practitioner writing has no feature or regime stage. Only
two silos (microstructure and decision) place five or more papers in execution. Some of these
gaps may be artefacts of the classifier, which gave each paper a single dominant silo and at most
three stages. We treat the table as a prompt for the bridge-building of Chapter 17 and for the
gap analysis of Chapter 19, not as a measurement of the field.


2.4      A Rosetta stone
The table that follows is the core of this chapter. Each row is a shared concept, taken from the
concept index of the collection (122 distinct tags, assigned by the same automated classifier; the
table keeps those that span silos or recur most). For each we give the term we will use, how the
relevant silos name or frame it, the chapters in which it is most prominent, and one to three
representative papers. The silo framings are our translations. They are meant to help a reader
recognise an object in an unfamiliar dialect. They are not attributions to the cited papers, whose
abstracts we checked only for fit with the row. Cited papers were chosen from the concept index
and checked against their abstracts; they are examples, not a complete census.

 bias          trading. Microstructure: attention and price                 Agarwal et al. (2025)
               pressure. Trader: crowding.


    Two cautions apply to the table. First, the shared term is a convenience, and in places the
silos mean different things by the same word: “anomaly” is the sharpest case, and “regime” is a
close second. Second, a row lists only the silos that appear in the concept index for that tag, so
an empty framing does not mean that a community lacks the idea. It means that we saw no
paper in the collection that frames it.


2.5     How to read the boxes
Two kinds of box recur in every topical chapter. They have different purposes and different
standards of evidence.

Desk notes.
  A desk note converts a cluster of papers into a design that a desk could try. It states a pipeline
  stage, an input, an output and what can go wrong, and it is written as a proposal: “a natural
  use is” or “a possible procedure” marks our design, not a result of the cited work. The cited
  papers support only what their abstracts state, such as a method, a data set or a reported
  finding. The “what can go wrong” list matters as much as the design, since many of the failure
  modes (look-ahead in labels, costs omitted from the reported edge, a detector that fires after
  the loss, thresholds fitted on the window that the strategy research already touched) are those
  that the validation chapters warn about. Treat a desk note as a hypothesis to be tested with
  the protocol of Chapter 14, never as a recommendation to deploy.

Bridge notes.
  A bridge note sits in the closing section of a chapter, “Bridges out of this chapter”. It takes
  an idea from one silo and says, in both directions, what another silo calls it and what each
  side could borrow. A typical sentence is of the form “what microstructure calls X is what
  time-series mining calls Y”. These translations are our synthesis, informed by the abstracts,
  and several are also candidates for the catalogue of Chapter 17. A bridge note is a claim
  about vocabulary and a suggestion for work, not a finding that the two communities have
  shown the equivalence.

    Across the 14 topical chapters there are 66 desk notes and 56 bridge notes. A reader short of
time can read the opening of a chapter for its dialects, the bridge section for the connections,
and the desk notes for the stage of the pipeline that interests them. The Rosetta stone above is
the index to all of this: find the shared term, read the framings, and follow the chapters column
to the place where the papers are discussed.




                                                  18
      Part II

Data and Substrate




        19
Chapter 3

Synthetic Data, Simulation and
Market Generators

Three communities meet in this chapter and rarely read one another. Deep-learning researchers
speak of generative models, foundation models, tokenisation and diffusion; they want realistic
samples. Market-microstructure and agent-based-modelling researchers speak of simulators,
zero-intelligence agents, calibration and stylised facts; they want a mechanism that explains why
prices behave as they do. A third, smaller group (financial mathematics, time-series mining,
behavioural finance) contributes scoring rules, anonymisation and equilibrium arguments. In the
shared vocabulary of the atlas these are all instances of one object: a synthetic-data engine that
produces counterfactual paths, order flow or scenarios, because history gives only one realised
trajectory.
    The translation is mostly a matter of purpose. What the deep-learning silo calls a “sample”
or a “rollout” is a scenario; what the agent-based silo calls “calibration” is fitting; “stylised facts”
(heavy tails, volatility clustering, absent return autocorrelation, square-root impact) are the
common validation checklist. The two main families differ in what they give a desk. Data-driven
generators reproduce statistics without explaining them. Agent-based simulators offer causal
levers (add a seller, remove a market maker) but fit statistics less tightly.
    In the fund pipeline the chapter sits at the data stage, as augmentation and scenario generation,
and at the validation stage, as the sandbox against which execution code, risk limits and learned
policies are tested before capital is committed. It feeds risk and execution indirectly, through
stress scenarios and cost assumptions. Cross-references: Chapter 12 for real order-book data,
Chapter 14 for validation practice.


3.1     Generative models for return paths
The oldest question is whether a neural network can emit price paths that look like markets.
Wiese et al. (2020) build Quant GANs from temporal convolutional networks, with a generator
constructed so that a transition to a risk-neutral distribution is possible; they report volatility
clusters, leverage effects and serial autocorrelations. Kwon and Lee (2024) ask the sceptical
version: can GANs learn random-walk, mean-reverting and jump behaviour in univariate and
multivariate series? Caulfield and Gleeson (2024) compare deep generative models with parametric
alternatives on multivariate series using distribution-moment distances, then test the best on
an implied-volatility trading task. Their message, together with the previous two, is that the
generator must be benchmarked against classical models, not assumed superior.
    Two papers attack the failure mode that matters to traders. Zhang et al. (2026d) argue
that GAN output can look realistic yet collapse under trading backtests, and add differentiable
stylised-fact constraints for asymmetry and tail events. Cliff (2006) is unrelated in method but
relevant in spirit: a 60-parameter search finds parameters that beat the original, and the paper

                                                  20
casts doubt on earlier conclusions. Any simulator-tuned result inherits this sensitivity.
    Extensions add structure. Gregnanin et al. (2026) combine path signatures, LSTMs and
graph networks on visibility graphs; Masi et al. (2026) pair a conditional GAN for correlated
mid-price and volume with a diffusion model whose critic guides sampling; Xia et al. (2026)
condition a GAN on macroeconomic indicators and train robust policies against it. Crisan
et al. (2026) address small data with a rough-path perspective and a parsimonious variational
autoencoder. A fuller account of representations such as signatures is in Chapter 6.
Desk note. Stage: validation, with data augmentation. Input: daily or intraday returns
for a universe. Output: a generator-gated stress set. A natural use is to train a generator on
the history, sample many paths, and run the unchanged strategy code on them, reading the
dispersion of drawdown and turnover rather than any single P&L. Require that a stylised-fact
checklist passes before a path set is used. Risks: the generator is fitted on the same history
as the strategy, so leakage and look-ahead are easy; GAN mode collapse produces implausible
extremes; and synthetic paths inherit the regime mix of the training window, so they cannot
supply a regime the data never showed.
    Between the two sections sits a practical point about data volume. Daily return histories are
short, so a generator trained on one asset memorises more than it generalises; the tick-level models
below are trained on far larger event sets (billions of trade events in the case of Kawawa-Beaudan
et al. (2026)), which is why cross-asset transfer is plausible there and doubtful here.


3.2     Diffusion, wavelets and foundation models
Diffusion has become the default generative family. Wang and Ventre (2026) survey it by data
type (time series, order books, tabular), and Bayer et al. (2026b) survey neural market generators
more broadly, including GPT-style models. Takahashi and Mizuno (2025) convert multivariate
price, volume and spread series to wavelet images and denoise them with a DDPM; Wang
and Milenkovic (2026) instead diffuse directly on wavelet coefficients with level-specific energy
constraints. Tang et al. (2026a) extend Tweedie’s formula to geometric Brownian motion, squared
Bessel and Cox–Ingersoll–Ross processes, so state-dependent volatility enters the denoiser. Waller
et al. (2026) replace transformer feed-forward blocks with quantum layers, evaluated on two
stocks. Choi et al. (2026a) move the idea to firm-level annual statements in latent space. Read
together, they also show the field’s trade-off: more expressive denoisers fit multi-scale structure,
while the parametric route of Tang et al. (2026a) keeps interpretable volatility dynamics that
a risk team can audit. Across these, the idea is the same: match multi-scale structure, not
just marginal distributions. Their claims are made on their own datasets, and nothing in the
abstracts shows out-of-sample trading gains.


3.3     Limit-order-book and order-flow generators
At tick level the object is an event stream. Coletta et al. (2022) learn a single “world agent” from
data, avoiding hand-set agent populations. Berti et al. (2025) generate order flow conditioned on
market state with a transformer diffusion model, and note that metrics for such simulators are
scarce, so they adapt a predictive score. Wang et al. (2026a) use conditional flow matching for
LOB trajectories across Hong Kong symbols, and report that it needs far fewer solver steps than
diffusion. Zhang et al. (2026e) model order events and book state jointly as a foundation model,
motivated by counterfactual questions. Kawawa-Beaudan et al. (2026) tokenise trade events
with scale-invariant features for a 524M-parameter transformer that works across thousands of
equities. Noble et al. (2026) are the practical counterweight: a simulator for large-tick stocks
projected on spread and volume imbalance, with a power-law impact kernel, aimed at realistic
fills and costs.


                                                21
Desk note. Stage: execution and validation. Input: message-level or top-of-book data
for the instruments traded. Output: a slippage and fill-rate estimate with its dispersion for an
execution algorithm. A natural use is to replay the same parent order through many generated
books, conditioned on a spread or volatility scenario, and compare schedules. Risks: generators
trained on passive history do not react to our own orders, so impact is understated unless a
feedback kernel is added; costs and queue position are often the first things a generator gets
wrong; and capacity conclusions do not transfer from large-tick to small-tick names.


3.4     Agent-based simulators
The agent-based line reproduces mechanisms. Byrd et al. (2020) provide ABIDES, an open-source
message-level simulator with configurable latency, used for market-impact experiments. Gao
et al. (2024) calibrate a high-frequency model with machine-learning surrogates and recreate the
2010 flash crash by adding an institutional seller; Leal et al. (2014) reach a related conclusion
in a model of low- and high-frequency traders, linking flash crashes to spreads and sell-side
synchronisation. Vytelingum et al. (2025) model liquidation cost by letting traders update beliefs
about fundamental value from order flow. Lussange et al. (2019) replace zero-intelligence agents
with reinforcement-learning agents calibrated to London data. Nakagawa et al. (2025) derive
microfoundations for GARCH from noise, fundamental and AI traders. Weng (2026) generate
momentum and reversal from herding and delayed information diffusion. Zeng et al. (2012)
supply a reputation game between institutions and individuals, and Davidovic et al. (2019) a
network model where uncertainty about exposures induces liquidity hoarding.
    Several papers study instability. Cliff (2022) shows that competing adaptive traders converge
on suboptimal outcomes; Rocha et al. (2021) shows that reaction speed can reverse rankings
of strategies; Zhou et al. (2026b) find that square-root impact is necessary for self-sustained
manipulation cycles in a learning-agent market; Novotny (2026) treat fundamental anchoring
as the stabiliser of liquidity, with no stress transmission between coupled books. Maitrier et al.
(2025) generate synthetic flow from superposed metaorders to test the square-root law. Calvez
and Cliff (2018) clone a trader’s behaviour from quotes with deep networks, and Inverso et al.
(2026) outline how formal methods might check emergent outcomes.
Desk note. Stage: risk and portfolio limits. Input: the fund’s position and liquidity
profile. Output: a crowding or fire-sale limit. A proposed use is to run an agent-based market
with a large seller whose size equals a plausible unwind, vary the strength of the fundamental
anchor, and set position caps at the size where one-sided book stress becomes self-sustaining.
Risks: results depend on hand-picked agent populations; mechanisms found in a toy model are
hypotheses, not estimates; and regime dependence is unavoidable, since the anchor strength that
matters is exactly what is unobserved.


3.5     Evaluation, benchmarks and infrastructure
Sun et al. (2026) supply thirty controlled environments across six mechanism families, so forecast
failures can be attributed to volatility clustering, heavy tails, regime switching or jumps. Galas
(2014) describe a simulation environment for testing algorithm stability and parameter sensitivity.
Der et al. (2023) synthesise series that preserve matrix-profile similarity joins while masking raw
values, a route to sharing data with outsiders.
    A common thread is that evaluation of generators lags their construction. Berti et al. (2025)
and Kwon and Lee (2024) both note the lack of agreed metrics, which suggests a desk should
score a generator by what it will be used for: a predictive score for forecasting models, fill and
cost error for execution, tail coverage for risk. The same logic explains why a harness such as



                                                22
that of Galas (2014) matters more than any one generator, since it fixes the protocol under which
every generator is compared.


3.6     Bridges out of this chapter

Bridge. Microstructure and deep learning. What the agent-based literature calls a
“calibrated population” is what the generative literature calls a “learned world agent” (Coletta
et al., 2022) or a “foundation model” (Zhang et al., 2026e); both answer “what would the book
have done?” Execution teams in Chapter 12 can use the same stylised-fact checklist to judge
either.

Bridge. Time-series mining and backtesting. The mechanism-labelled environments of
Sun et al. (2026) give TS-mining groups a labelled benchmark in which what they call a “regime
change” or “discord” is known by construction. Chapters 5 and 14 can use that as a positive
control for detectors and for overfitting tests.

Bridge. Market structure and regimes. Herding, anchoring and liquidity-hoarding models
turn the behavioural vocabulary of Chapter 4 (“herding”, “confidence”) into simulator parameters,
and offer regime-switch triggers for the models of Chapter 7.




                                               23
Chapter 4

Market Structure and Participants

Four communities meet in this chapter and rarely read one another. Financial economists
write about participants, flows, anomalies and behavioural biases. Microstructure researchers
write about venues, auctions, liquidity and market impact. Time-series and deep-learning
researchers, who reach this topic through surveillance, write about anomaly detection and
imbalanced classification. A fourth group, drawn from law, science and technology studies, control
theory and ecology, writes about regulation, stacks, feedback and fragility.
    In the shared vocabulary of the atlas these become a small set of terms. A regulatory reform
is a candidate regime-shift. A fund flow or crowd of retail traders is an order-flow or sentiment
feature. A manipulation alert is an anomaly score. A “fragile” market is one whose market-impact
and network-contagion behaviour changes sharply in the tails. The chapter is therefore less about
alpha than about the ground the alpha stands on: who trades, under which rules, and how the
rules break.
    In the fund pipeline the chapter sits mostly at the regime and risk stages, with side entrances
into data (which volume can be trusted), signal (flow and attention features), execution (auctions,
expiry days) and validation (simulation, falsification). Most examples come from India, the
market this atlas keeps returning to.


4.1     Rules as regimes: reforms and natural experiments
A backtest that spans a rule change spans two markets. Potharla (2025) chart the 2010–2025
growth and reform of Indian capital markets, and Gunnam et al. (2024) review SEBI’s derivatives
measures: larger lot sizes, fewer weekly expiries, higher expiry-day margins, upfront premium
collection and tighter intraday monitoring. A natural use is a dated table of regime flags, joined
to every Indian backtest as structural-break dummies.
    Three papers show how to measure what a rule change did. Wang et al. (2026b) treat
China’s 2024 program-trading provisions as a quasi-natural experiment. They build a stock-level
HFT-intensity index and use difference-in-differences, finding larger spread reductions where
prior HFT exposure was higher, and larger effects after negative-return periods. Hussain and
Olekar (2026) exploit the 2025 swap of weekly expiry days between two Indian exchanges and
subject each estimate to a four-part falsification battery, because only two exchanges are involved.
The pairing is useful: one paper supplies the exposure-based design, the other a discipline for
distrusting it. Khatwani (2026) describe the Closing Auction Session that replaced VWAP-based
closing prices for F&O stocks, and report early sessions only; their observations are anecdotal until
more data accumulate. Older and cross-market evidence enters through Hillion and Suominen
(2004), whose agency model of closing-price manipulation explains why the last minute at the
Paris Bourse was so active before a closing call auction, so the closing window deserves both
execution care and surveillance. Babu and Dsouza (2021) fit the same event-study mould for
52 Indian IPOs in 2018–2020, examining abnormal returns and their link to over-subscription,


                                                 24
profit after tax, promoter holdings and issue price.
Desk note. Regime calendar for Indian backtests (regime stage; validation). Input:
dated SEBI circulars, lot-size, margin and expiry changes, auction-regime start dates (Gunnam
et al., 2024; Potharla, 2025; Khatwani, 2026). Output: a categorical regime feature plus a hard
rule that no model is fit across a flagged break without a break dummy, and that cost models
are re-estimated per era. Failure modes: look-ahead if announcement and effective dates are
confused; too many flags for too few observations; and, as Hussain and Olekar (2026) warn,
assuming a rule change moves volatility before placebo tests agree.


4.2     Participants and flows
Several papers describe who is on the other side of a trade. Agarwal et al. (2025) study Indian
retail options traders, who concentrate in short-maturity index options, hold briefly and lose
money; supply-side interventions raised trading, and participation costs pushed investors toward
cheaper, shorter-dated out-of-the-money options. Saw and Santosh (2026) survey 320 retail
investors and report that herd behaviour shapes perception and participation, partly mediated
by perception and moderated by financial literacy. Questionnaire evidence is weaker than trade
data, and a desk should treat it as hypothesis generation. Bunkar and Ramaiah (2024) use a
similar survey design for intention to use algorithmic platforms, a rough guide to how much
retail flow may become automated.
    Flows can also be measured. Madhumathi et al. (2013) find that Indian equity-fund flows
respond to lagged returns and that outflows follow negative market returns without a spiralling
effect. Sicherman et al. (2016) use daily account logins: logins fall by 9.5% after market declines
and when the VIX is high. Gabaix and Koijen (2021) close the loop with the inelastic markets
hypothesis: because institutions are constrained, flows move aggregate prices, and a dollar
invested raises market value by about five dollars in their estimates.
Desk note. Flow and attention overlay (signal and risk stages). Inputs: mutual-
fund flow data, broker login or search-activity indices, and short-dated retail option volume
(Madhumathi et al., 2013; Sicherman et al., 2016; Agarwal et al., 2025). Output: a slow crowding
or flow-pressure feature, or an exposure cap in short-dated index products, and a candidate
contrarian overlay (Saw and Santosh, 2026). Risks: flow data arrive with a lag, so the feature
may be stale on arrival; attention proxies are a consequence of returns as much as a cause; and
elasticity estimates (Gabaix and Koijen, 2021) are macro-level and not a capacity model for one
book.
    Taken together, these papers suggest a layered picture: slow, measurable flows set the
background pressure on prices, while attention and herding explain why retail participation
clusters in particular products and moments. None is a trading rule by itself. Each could serve
as a conditioning variable, telling a model when its usual relationships are likely to be distorted
by who is active.


4.3     Manipulation and surveillance
Surveillance is where classification methods meet market rules. Zulkifley et al. (2023) survey AI
detectors of stock manipulation and give a baseline taxonomy. The newer papers differ by data
and idea. Chen and Hybinette (2026) detect pump-and-crash patterns in the velocity of option
Delta and price at minute frequency on BANKNIFTY, with a time-partitioned test and SHAP
attribution; conditioning on hidden-Markov regimes trades recall for precision, a useful negative
result. Abdulrazaq and Musa (2026) address class imbalance in cryptocurrency pump-and-dump
data with SmoteGAN augmentation and a TabNet classifier, reporting precision and recall on


                                                25
25-second intervals. Zwydak et al. (2026) instead use complexity measures (multifractality,
entropy, detrended cross-correlation) to flag exchanges whose trade counts rise without matching
volume. Y et al. (2026) fuse disclosure text, online sentiment and trading features at firm-month
level against CSRC sanctions. Tsaknaki et al. (2026) flip the viewpoint: in an Almgren–Chriss
setting an RL agent learns manipulative strategies, a red-team generator for detectors.


4.4     Algorithmic fragility, opacity and oversight
The last group asks what happens when many fast agents interact. Jain et al. (2016) study
Tokyo’s Arrowhead platform and argue for CoVaR-style correlation measures in tail states. Ng
(2026) classify AI-driven flash crashes as herding, model-error cascades or GenAI disinformation.
Dou et al. (2025) model coordinated AI speculators who create and then unwind bubbles. Bajpai
(2025a) recast markets as adversarial control systems with a proposed Control Fragility Index,
which is unvalidated. Wallace and Wallace (2025) offer a biological analogy for limits on HFT-AI
systems, and Farmer and Skouras (2013), known to us by title only, take an ecological view of
computer trading. Aldridge (2026) surveys how trading rules evolved through electronic books,
auctions and AI agents.
    On the oversight side, Raschner (2021) analyses MiFID II’s testing and simulation require-
ments, Gulhane (2026) argues that undisclosed strategy patterns across broker APIs form
functional dark liquidity in India, and Hansen (2025) draw on 182 practitioner interviews to
argue that analysis should shift from algorithm logic to the layered stack and its dependencies.
Desk note. Pre-deployment simulation and stress set (validation and risk). Input: a
simulated market or replayed tick data, scenarios from the flash-crash taxonomy (Ng, 2026; Dou
et al., 2025), and a stack dependency map (Hansen, 2025). Output: documented pass criteria, in
the spirit of Raschner (2021), and tail-state position limits (Jain et al., 2016). Risks: simulators
embed their builders’ assumptions, so passing proves little about unseen regimes; and scenario
sets tend to fit last year’s crash.
    Read together, the group offers a vocabulary of failure modes rather than forecasts. A fund
can use it to ask which of its own strategies would be crowded, correlated with others in a stress,
or exposed to a venue or broker dependency, and to write those questions into its risk review
before an incident does.


4.5     Bridges out of this chapter

Bridge. To microstructure and execution (Chapter 12). What this chapter calls
a “closing-window manipulation signature” (Hillion and Suominen, 2004) is what order-book
work calls an end-of-day liquidity and impact regime. Expiry and auction reforms are natural
experiments for the impact and cost models there.

Bridge. To time-series mining and regimes (Chapters 5 and 7). A manipulation
episode is, in mining language, a discord or anomalous subsequence; velocity features (Chen
and Hybinette, 2026) and complexity diagnostics (Zwydak et al., 2026) are representations that
anomaly search could reuse. Regulatory dates are the change-point candidates that regime
methods usually have to discover blindly.

Bridge. To synthetic data and validation (Chapters 3 and 14). Simulation-based
certification and rare-event augmentation are the same need seen from law and from deep
learning. The falsification battery of Hussain and Olekar (2026) is a multiple-testing safeguard
that backtesting chapters can borrow.


                                                26
         Part III

Representation and Pattern




            27
Chapter 5

Time-Series Methods: Forecasting,
Classification and Mining

Time-series mining is the least “financial” chapter of this atlas, and that is exactly why it is
here. Most of the papers below were written by people who look at water-treatment plants,
accelerometers, servers, hospital wards and seismometers, and who have never been asked to
produce a Sharpe ratio. Three communities meet in this chapter. The data-mining school
(the tsml silo, centred on the matrix-profile programme) speaks of primitives, motifs, discords,
shapelets and similarity joins. The statistics school speaks of dynamics, long memory, spectra
and system identification. The finance and econometrics school speaks of clusters of stocks,
volatility regimes and “mechanisms”. A small group of deep-learning and LLM papers appears at
the edges.
    The atlas’s shared vocabulary lets us translate. A motif is a recurring pattern, which a trader
would call a setup or a template. A discord is the subsequence least like anything else, which is an
anomaly, an outlier bar sequence or a dislocation. Similarity search is nearest-neighbour matching
of a query shape against history, the ancestor of every “find days like today” study. Clustering is
grouping series by shape, dynamics or features, and the trader’s version is a peer group, a sector
proxy or a hedge basket. Representation and feature extraction turn a window of raw prices into
a vector that a model can consume. The recurring concepts here are similarity-search, motif,
anomaly, representation-learning, regime-shift and cross-section.
    In the fund pipeline (data → features → signal → regime/risk → portfolio → execution →
validation → research automation) this chapter sits mostly at the first two stages. It supplies
distance functions, indexes, descriptors and unsupervised groupings that later stages consume. It
supplies few finished alphas. This half of the chapter covers the mining primitives, clustering
and feature libraries. The second half turns to forecasting and classification models proper.
Chapter 6 covers learned representations in more depth, and Chapter 7 picks up the regime and
change-point uses of the same tools.


5.1     Similarity and the matrix profile as a shared engine
Nearly everything in the mining silo reduces to one operation: compare a subsequence with other
subsequences. Berndt and Clifford (1994) is the historical anchor, an early experiment applying
dynamic time warping (DTW) to find patterns in time series, with stock prices named among
the example data. DTW tolerates stretching along the time axis, so two similar shapes that
unfold at different speeds still match. Rakthanmanon et al. (2012) then attack the practical
objection, that similarity search is the bottleneck of mining algorithms, and show how to search
and mine trillions of subsequences under DTW. For a desk this is the difference between a
research notebook and a screen that runs across a whole universe.
    The matrix profile generalises the idea. Yeh et al. (2016b) frame all-pairs similarity joins

                                                28
as a unifying view that includes motifs, discords and shapelets: for each subsequence, store
the distance to its nearest neighbour elsewhere in the series. Low values mark motifs and high
values mark discords. The follow-ups are mostly about scale and convenience. Zhu et al. (2016)
push exact motifs and joins past one hundred million points using GPUs, Zhu et al. (2018)
give an interactive-speed algorithm (SCRIMP++), and Zimmerman et al. (2019) scale GPU
computation to a quintillion pairwise comparisons a day. Yeh et al. (2016a) use the profile
to visualise salient subsequences in massive series. Shahcheraghi et al. (2024) bring the same
scaling ideas to recurrence plots and similarity matrices, from which features can be derived for
downstream analytics.
    Several papers relax the assumptions that make the basic profile brittle. Furtado Silva and E.
A. P. A. Batista (2018) extend it beyond Euclidean distance and fixed subsequence length to
elastic measures, and Alaee et al. (2020) give exact motif discovery under DTW. Zhu et al. (2021)
handle missing data, a daily reality for tick archives with gaps and halted names. Der et al.
(2022) propose a distance for comparing long series with cyclical behaviour, where Euclidean
distance and DTW are less meaningful, and Gharghabi et al. (2018) argue that the common
distances are less robust than believed and propose MPdist. On the symbolic side, Fuad and
Marteau (2013) speed up the symbolic aggregate approximation (SAX), a dimension-reduction
representation for similarity search. Approximation is a theme too: Yeh et al. (2021) give
error-bounded approximate joins using compact dictionary representations.
    The anomaly side is where this half of the chapter first touches risk. Yeh et al. (2024)
apply the matrix profile to anomaly detection in multidimensional series, and Yeh et al. (2023b)
sketch multidimensional series so that discord mining has runtime independent of the number
of dimensions, with reported speedups of at least an order of magnitude on water-treatment
and transportation data. Their examples are not financial, so the finance reading is ours: a
multi-asset panel is just a multidimensional series.
Desk note. Streaming dislocation screen (data → regime/risk). Input: intraday returns
or normalised mid-prices for a few hundred instruments, windowed to a fixed length. Output:
a per-instrument discord score, built on multidimensional matrix-profile ideas such as those of
Yeh et al. (2024) and the sketching of Yeh et al. (2023b), that serves as a risk filter (reduce
size when the current window is far from everything seen in the last months) or as a flag for
manual review. What can go wrong: the profile is distance-based, so volatility scaling and
z-normalisation choices decide what counts as unusual; a discord is not a direction, so it is a
filter, not a signal; and in-sample thresholds tuned on a single crisis will not generalise. Always
compute the nearest-neighbour search using only data available at the decision time.


5.2     Motifs, chains, shapelets and the question of length
If the profile is the engine, motifs are the most-used output. Yeh et al. (2017b) ask what a
meaningful multidimensional motif is, and Dau and Keogh (2017) propose a generic way to
incorporate domain knowledge so that discovered motifs match the user’s intent. The recurring
practical problem is length. Linardi et al. (2018) introduce VALMOD for variable-length motifs,
Linardi et al. (2020b) package it as a suite for exact detection, and Linardi et al. (2020a) extend
the idea to variable-length motifs and discords together. Wesenbeeck et al. (2023) (LoCoMotif)
find time-warped motifs, removing the limitations of fixed length, of only the two most similar
occurrences, and of univariate input. For traders, a “pattern” that must have exactly 30 bars is
arbitrary, so the length question is the real one.
    Three papers bring this to markets. Rodrigues et al. (2019) study motif discovery and analysis
in financial time series with VALMOD, and flag the evaluation and significance of motifs as
the issue. Cremona et al. (2023) embed asset prices in a functional-data framework to find
functional motifs in stock prices, motivated by recurrent shapes such as the surge of a bubble.


                                                29
Cartwright et al. (2022) combine SAX, minimum description length and the matrix profile in a
side-length-independent method, and link motif discovery to volatility analysis. All three are
research results. None should be read as evidence that a motif earns excess return.
Bridge. Motif vs. technical pattern. What the mining silo calls a “motif” is what
technical analysts call a chart pattern, and what the strategy literature (Chapter 10) tests as
a rule. The mining view adds two things the chart view lacks: a distance threshold, so that
pattern membership is not a matter of taste, and a significance question, which Rodrigues
et al. (2019) raise explicitly. Conversely, mining people can borrow the backtesting literature’s
warning (Chapter 14) that a pattern found by searching many lengths and many assets needs a
multiple-testing correction before it counts.
    Other primitives answer different questions. Zhu et al. (2017) introduce time-series chains,
ordered sets of subsequences in which each resembles its predecessor while the first and last may
be arbitrarily dissimilar, which is a formal way of describing gradual drift and so connects to
regime-shift ideas. Imani et al. (2018) propose snippets as a representative summary of a long
series, arguing that motifs, shapelets, cluster centres and random samples are poor answers to
“show me typical data”. Lee et al. (2024) formalise the smallest set of representative series for a
similarity threshold. Ye and Keogh (2009) introduced shapelets, discriminative subsequences
for classification that can be read by a human. Mercer et al. (2022) define the contrast profile,
a primitive that contrasts two classes of subsequences for real-world classification, and Yeh
et al. (2017a) use weakly labelled series to predict outcomes without needing precisely extracted,
correctly labelled subsequences. That last assumption is close to the trader’s situation, where
events are fuzzily dated.
    Two papers look at patterns on a coarser footing. Wu et al. (2023) mine top-k contrast
order-preserving patterns, i.e. frequent relative orders that differ between two classes of series.
Aghamohammadi et al. (2014) use a permutation approach on tick-to-tick exchange rates to
examine how the variety of micro patterns changed as high-frequency trading emerged. Rank-order
patterns are robust to scale, which suits prices.


5.3     Clustering series: shape, dynamics and mechanism
Clustering asks which series behave alike. The survey of Warren Liao (2005) remains the entry
point. Choices are the representation (raw, features, model parameters), the dissimilarity and
the algorithm. Papers in this section differ mainly in the dissimilarity.
    Shape-based methods compare waveforms. Fahiman et al. (2016) extend c-shapes to fuzzy
clustering of finite waveforms, and Begum et al. (2016) give a density-based framework with an
admissible pruning strategy so that DTW clustering stays tractable. In finance, D’Urso et al.
(2021) use trimmed fuzzy clustering with DTW on financial series, with the trimming meant to
cope with outlying series, and Xiao (2025) use DTW-based K-means within a causal-inference
framework (additive noise model mixtures) to cluster return mechanisms. The causal claim is
the author’s to defend, and a desk should treat inferred directions as hypotheses.
    Dynamics-based methods compare the generating process. Yue et al. (2025) cluster vector
autoregressive series through system identification, Cerqueti and Mattera (2023) cluster by a
time-varying Hurst exponent, so that long memory is allowed to change, and Cerqueti et al. (2024)
cluster multivariate series with time-varying parameters in a multiway framework. Frequency-
domain variants include Caiado et al. (2020), who cluster big collections using a fragmented
periodogram around the driving cycles, and Ghosh (2025), who apply k-means to polyspectral
means of 49 Indian stocks, bringing bispectral information that captures nonlinearity beyond
the power spectrum. Caiado and Crato (2010) combine threshold-GARCH parameters with the
periodogram of squared returns to cluster stock returns, a volatility-aware dissimilarity.
    Feature-based methods are the bridge to the next section. Wang et al. (2007) represent each
series by a fixed-length vector of statistical features and cluster the concatenation, which also

                                                30
reduces dimension, and Bonifati et al. (2022) (Time2Feat) learn interpretable feature subsets for
multivariate clustering. Machado et al. (2015), from accelerometer-based activity recognition,
shows how sensitive unsupervised results can be to feature-extraction parametrisation, a useful
warning for any feature-based grouping of assets. Oriona et al. (2023) treat ordinal-valued series,
with economic applications, which resembles discretised ratings or state labels. Kim and Hwang
(2024) use sparse functional PCA to find hidden dependence structure in sparse longitudinal
data.
Desk note. Dynamics-aware peer groups (features → portfolio). Input: daily returns
and realised volatility for the tradable universe. Output: cluster labels, from a volatility-and-
spectrum dissimilarity such as that of Caiado and Crato (2010) or a trimmed fuzzy DTW scheme
such as that of D’Urso et al. (2021), used as (i) hedge baskets for relative-value trades, (ii) a
risk-concentration limit per cluster, or (iii) a neutralisation group for cross-sectional signals.
Fuzzy memberships give soft limits instead of hard sector walls. What can go wrong: clusters
estimated on the full sample leak the future into any backtest, so re-estimate on rolling windows
and track label stability; fuzzy and trimmed variants still need a choice of cluster count; and
clusters formed in calm periods can merge in a crisis. Treat cluster stability as a monitored
quantity.


5.4     Feature libraries and the interrogation of series
A different answer to “how do I describe a series?” is to compute everything. Fulcher et al. (2013)
organise annotated collections of over 35 000 real-world and model-generated series against over
9000 analysis methods, and study the empirical structure of both. Fulcher and Jones (2017) turn
this into hctsa, a framework for automated phenotyping through massive feature extraction, and
the same group’s Fulcher et al. (2019) (CompEngine) offers a self-organising, living library of
series intended to connect fields that rarely talk to each other. Fulcher (2026) supply a controlled
testbed: 1000 simulated series from 133 mechanisms, including long-memory, multifractal, chaotic
and noise-driven processes. In atlas terms this is alpha-mining at the feature level: generate
thousands of candidate descriptors, then select. The multiple-testing concept attached to Fulcher
and Jones (2017) and Fulcher et al. (2013) is therefore not a footnote. With thousands of features
and a few years of data, some feature will correlate with next-week returns by luck.
    Classification papers show how features and shape complement each other. Alaee et al. (2019)
argue that it is a false dichotomy: some classes are best separated by features and others by
shape, so a classifier should be able to use both. Baldán and Benítez (2020) study complexity
measures as features for classification, in the setting where a hierarchical-vote ensemble dominates
the state of the art. For anomaly detection at scale, Hyndman et al. (2015) compute a vector
of features on each of thousands of monitored series and look for unusual ones in feature space.
This is a design a desk could use to monitor thousands of strategy P&L streams or data feeds.
Hudson et al. (2021) use subgroup discovery to find interpretable local exceptional behaviour in
time series, outside finance, and the interpretability is the attraction.
    Finance-flavoured descriptors appear in a few places. Hosking (1981) generalise ARIMA
models by letting the degree of differencing take fractional values, which yields processes with
long-term persistence. Practitioners have since used fractional differencing as a route to features
that keep memory while removing a unit root; that use is our gloss, not a claim of the 1981
paper, and Chapter 14 returns to such window-safe transforms. Lacasa and Flanagan (2016) use
visibility graphs to quantify time irreversibility of 35 financial indices, and show that the measure
complements volatility-based ones, which they exploit for classification. The graph viewpoint is
itself a bridge: a series becomes a network and network tools apply (Chapter 7).




                                                 31
Desk note. Feature-bank screen with a hard multiple-testing budget (features →
validation). Input: a rolling window of returns, volume and realised volatility per instrument.
Output: a few hundred descriptors in the style of Fulcher and Jones (2017), with fractionally
differenced inputs per Hosking (1981) and graph-based irreversibility per Lacasa and Flanagan
(2016) as candidate additions, scored for stability across assets and sub-periods. What can go
wrong: selection on the full sample leaks the future; descriptors that are highly collinear inflate
the apparent number of independent discoveries; features that need long windows have few
independent observations; and a descriptor tied to a single regime (a volatility regime, say) will
decay. Log the number of features tried, and deflate performance accordingly (see Chapter 14).


5.5     Labels and the modern edge
Two recent papers show where the tradition is heading, and each carries a warning. Yang
et al. (2025) argue, for forecasting, that not all data are good labels. They re-label series
by self-supervised reconstruction, using intermediate outputs as pseudo-labels that improve
generalisation for any predictor. For finance, where the target is a noisy return, the idea of
denoising the label is attractive but unproven. We suggest it as an experiment, and a leak-free
implementation would fit the reconstruction network only on the training fold. Gao et al. (2026b)
bridge clinical time series into a pretrained vision-language model with a compact adapter. It is
a medical paper and peripheral here, but it shows the direction of travel: irregular numerical
series are being fed to foundation models, a theme of Chapter 6 and the LLM chapter.
Bridge. Mining vocabulary for the fund. A “discord” of the matrix-profile school is what
a risk manager would call an out-of-distribution day, and a “snippet” (Imani et al., 2018) is a
representative regime exemplar. “Time-series phenotyping” (Fulcher and Jones, 2017) is feature
engineering, and “weak labels” (Yeh et al., 2017a) are the fuzzy event dates and triple-barrier-style
targets that the backtesting chapter discusses. In the other direction, finance contributes what
the mining silo mostly lacks: costs, capacity and a hostile non-stationary adversary. A discord
detector that is 50 times faster is only useful if the flagged window is actionable before the market
reprices it.
    Two cautions run through the chapter. First, nearly every method here is unsupervised
or exploratory, so its output is a hypothesis, to be tested out of sample with realistic costs.
Second, evaluation conventions come from domains where the data are stationary and labelled,
which makes benchmark gains on those archives a weak guide to performance in markets. The
distinction between a better distance measure and a better trading signal should be kept explicit
throughout.


5.6     Who leads whom: causal structure and lead–lag
A forecasting desk rarely asks only “what will this series do?”. It also asks which other series carry
information about it, and whether that information is stable enough to trade. The papers in this
group attack that question with tools that come from climate science, cardiology, econometrics
and machine learning, and they use one shared idea: replace a symmetric correlation by a
directed, conditional statement about dependence.
    The econometric root is the testable definition of causality and feedback between two series,
collected with the rest of Granger’s work in Granger et al. (2001). The modern, high-dimensional
descendant is the causal-discovery pipeline of Runge et al. (2019), which combines linear or
nonlinear conditional-independence tests with a graph-discovery algorithm and is validated on
climate and heart-rate series. Löwe et al. (2022) ask a different question: when many samples
share the same dynamics but have different underlying graphs, can one amortised model infer
the graph for each sample rather than refitting every time? The broader review in Runge et al.

                                                 32
(2023) places these tools within a causal-inference framework for time series, and is useful mainly
for its vocabulary of assumptions (what must hold before an arrow can be read as a cause).
    Finance has its own, more modest versions. Wilinski et al. (2018) build a Hilbert-transform
complex correlation that works on unevenly spaced intraday quotes and picks up lead–lag
relations at several scales without knowing the lag in advance; on Tokyo Stock Exchange data
they report that sectors and subsectors form market components that follow each other with
small delays. Zhang et al. (2023a) detect lead–lag relations in lagged multi-factor models by
clustering sliding-window subsequences. Kenett et al. (2014) strip out the influence of a third
stock with partial correlations, which is the static, non-directed cousin of the same conditioning
idea. Koopman et al. (2015) model dependence between tick-level price changes with time-varying
discrete copulas, and Patton (2012) survey copula models for economic series, whose appeal is
that marginals and dependence can be specified separately. Two further papers apply clustering
or classification to market structure itself: Dehao (2022) combine a graph-attention autoencoder
with a self-organising map to cluster multidimensional market series, and Yeh et al. (2023a)
classify foreground subsequences hidden inside background noise using nearest-neighbour graphs,
a setting that resembles finding a pattern in a long price history.
Desk note. Lead–lag screen for input selection (signal stage). A natural use is a nightly
job that takes returns, or intraday mid-quote changes, for a universe of related instruments, runs
a conditional-independence or partial-correlation screen, and outputs a sparse directed candidate
graph. The graph is a feature-selection filter: only instruments with a stable incoming edge enter
the signal model for a target. What can go wrong: with realistic sample sizes and heavy tails,
nonlinear tests have low power and many edges are false; a common driver (the market factor)
can masquerade as lead–lag; and an edge found on quotes may vanish after costs and latency.
Treat every edge as a hypothesis to be validated out of sample, and track edge stability across
rolling windows as a diagnostic in its own right.


5.7     Small, fast and frequency-aware forecasters
A large part of the recent forecasting literature, written for traffic, energy and weather benchmarks,
asks how little model is enough. For a systematic fund this is the relevant question, because
the signal-to-noise ratio in returns is low and large networks mostly fit noise. Before any of
these, the classical baseline deserves a sentence: Scrivano (2025) review ARMA models as a
computationally cheap and statistically principled tool for stationary series. A desk should be
able to beat that baseline net of costs before it spends engineering time on anything below.
    The frequency-domain family starts from the observation that a series can be manipulated
spectrally. Xu et al. (2023) interpolate in the complex frequency domain, discard high-frequency
components, and reach performance comparable to larger models on forecasting and anomaly
detection with roughly ten thousand parameters. Eefsen et al. (2024) replicate that result and
then show, on a wide range of datasets, that the tiny model especially excels at capturing
periodic structure while missing trends. That is an honest and useful caveat: a model that
captures seasonality will look strong on intraday volume or volatility seasonality and weak on
drift. Yi et al. (2023) apply MLPs in the frequency domain to overcome point-wise mappings
and information bottlenecks, Fei et al. (2025) amplify neglected low-energy spectral components,
and Huang et al. (2025) decompose mixed-frequency series and model each component with
Kolmogorov–Arnold blocks. Zhang et al. (2025a) do the multi-resolution version with wavelets
inside an MLP-Mixer, and Ferdaus et al. (2026) review twenty-six KAN-based architectures,
noting practical deployment challenges.
    A second sub-family mixes scales or removes decomposable parts before learning. Wang
et al. (2024) treat the series as a mixture of patterns that differ by sampling scale. Choi et al.
(2026b) make the dominant cycle depend on the input window rather than the dataset, Wang


                                                 33
et al. (2026c) separate period-aware components from trend and noise, and Liu (2026) combine
decomposition with a lightweight state-space design to mitigate non-stationary distribution shift.
Wang et al. (2025d) push the same multi-scale, multi-resolution idea into one backbone meant to
serve forecasting, classification, anomaly detection and imputation.
    Two papers argue about the architecture itself. Naghashi et al. (2025) reconsider the GRU as
a cheaper alternative that still captures cross-channel dependence, and Oreshkin et al. (2020)
remain the reference for the interpretable, fully connected, residual N-BEATS stack; Binte Habib
et al. (2025) extend it with multi-head self-attention, but report their results mainly on a weather
dataset, with brief generalisation checks in finance and healthcare; the abstract does not say
what financial target was forecast, so any trading relevance is ours to test, not theirs.
Desk note. Cheap-forecaster ladder (forecast stage). Input: a panel of daily or intraday
features (returns, volume, realised-volatility proxies). Output: a point forecast or, more usefully,
a seasonality and trend decomposition used as a feature. A sensible workflow is a ladder: ARMA,
then a frequency-domain linear model, then a multi-scale mixer, with each rung required to beat
the previous one after costs on purged, walk-forward folds. Lightweight models suit latency-
limited intraday signal generation and are easy to retrain. Risks: benchmark datasets here are
periodic by construction, whereas returns are close to unpredictable in the mean, so the gains
seen on electricity or traffic will shrink sharply; a model that captures seasonality may simply
restate a known intraday volume curve; and normalisation or decomposition steps fitted on the
whole sample leak the future.


5.8     Transformers, foundation models and exogenous information
The Transformer branch of forecasting has gone through its own swing of fashion. Liu et al.
(2024) invert the usual tokenisation so that attention runs over variates rather than time stamps,
and Cheng et al. (2024) pursue a related two-dimensional view with vanilla Transformers. Wang
et al. (2026i) make the scale of the multivariate model adaptive, and Shen et al. (2025) ask which
of encoder-only, encoder-decoder or decoder-only designs works best once time-series-specific
tricks are stripped away. Lim et al. (2020) remain the practical reference for multi-horizon
problems that mix static covariates, known future inputs and observed exogenous series, with
interpretability built into the architecture. Liu et al. (2025b) is the one most relevant to markets
because it treats non-stationarity as something to be separated into short-term and long-term
effects rather than simply removed, an idea we return to in the next section.
    The growing exogenous-information theme is where these ideas touch finance most directly.
Dong et al. (2026) design a financial multi-scale Transformer that fuses exogenous drivers, on
the premise that a target asset is coupled to other series. Outside finance, Wang et al. (2025b)
fuse text, time series and satellite imagery for demand forecasting and H et al. (2026) align
multilingual text with numerical streams for risk monitoring; their data differ from ours, but the
fusion problem (aligning slow, sparse, textual evidence to fast numerical series without leaking
future timestamps) is exactly the one a news-aware signal faces.
    Foundation-model work changes the question from “which architecture?” to “can we skip
training?”. Hoo et al. (2026) treat forecasting as tabular regression with temporal featurisation
on top of a pretrained tabular model, reporting strong zero-shot results on covariate-informed
forecasting. Wang et al. (2026h) route series to experts guided by a regime predictor, which in a
multi-instrument setting is a way to let one model specialise by forecastability. Fechete et al.
(2025) let the forecaster re-weight its loss toward an application-specific range at inference time,
a natural fit when only tail moves matter for stop placement. Marusov et al. (2026) supply a
contrastive generative model for probabilistic forecasting, closer to what risk needs than a point
forecast. Two other papers show how far the same machinery travels: Allam and McEwen (2021)
classify astronomical transients with a Transformer that is agnostic to channel structure, and


                                                34
