Dong et al. (2024) forecast wind power with a dynamic hypergraph over linked producers. Both
are peripheral to equity trading, but the first resembles tagging irregular event bursts across
instruments, and the second resembles energy-market trading, where physical spatial coupling
exists and the hypergraph has something real to encode.
Desk note. Zero-shot and exogenous forecasters as challengers (forecast and signal
stages). A tabular or time-series foundation model could serve as a challenger that runs beside
the house models on the same covariates (term-structure points, factor returns, lagged flow). Its
output is a forecast distribution and a disagreement score against the incumbent. Strengths:
no training pipeline to leak, easy to rerun daily. Risks: pretraining corpora may include the
very market series being evaluated, so a clean test needs data dated after the model’s release;
exogenous covariates must be lagged to what was knowable at the decision time; and capacity is
small, since a signal that looks good only in tails may be untradeable at size.


5.9     Non-stationarity, regimes and anomalies
If one fact separates finance from the benchmarks above, it is that the process changes. Several
papers here attack this directly. Liu et al. (2023a) use Koopman theory with a Fourier filter to
disentangle time-invariant from time-variant dynamics, and Alkharashi et al. (2025) propose a
self-learning type-2 fuzzy system that prunes its rule base as forecast accuracy deteriorates after
adaptation, an online, interpretable response to shifting dynamics. He et al. (2026) is explicitly
financial: it argues that log-returns are non-stationary with very low signal-to-noise, and that a
fixed context window is mismatched to regime-dependent dependence, so it builds a regime-aware
expert network with variable context. A parallel in a different domain is Dong et al. (2025a),
which clusters load and price jointly into market states before adaptive forecasting; the same
joint-state idea transfers to a price–volume state for equities. In the pattern-mining tradition,
Cartwright et al. (2021) apply matrix profiles to financial series and propose several ways to find
similar behaviour patterns, linking this chapter to the motif material earlier in it.
    Regime labels are only useful if one can tell a regime change from an outlier, and the anomaly
papers address that. Li et al. (2021) propose a clustering-based anomaly detector for multivariate
series in which scores account for both temporal and variable relationships. Der et al. (2024b) go
one step further and ask for an explanation after an anomaly is flagged, so that a human can
triage it. The same concern drives the explainable-clustering papers: Fragkathoulas et al. (2026)
use counterfactual explanations to show what minimal temporal change would move an instance
across a cluster boundary, Schlegel et al. (2025) survey explainable deep clustering, and Boniol
et al. (2025) present a visual system for graph-based clustering. For a regime classifier, the
counterfactual question (what would have to change for the label to flip?) is the right monitoring
question.
Desk note. Regime tags with explanations (regime and risk stages). Input: multivariate
daily or intraday features. Output: a regime label plus a nearest counterfactual (“how far is
today from the boundary?”) and an anomaly score, consumed by the risk layer as a leverage
or signal-weight filter. Failure modes: labels are fitted in-sample and flip with a refit, so the
regime history is not the history the desk would have seen live; clusters that look stable may be
a volatility proxy in disguise; and a filter that turns off signals after a drawdown has already
begun costs return in exactly the rebound.


5.10     Evaluation, hygiene and tooling
The last group is about whether we should believe any of the above. Its common idea is that
evaluation machinery deserves scrutiny as much as the models do. Wu and Keogh (2020a)


                                                35
argue that popular anomaly-detection benchmarks are flawed and create an illusion of progress.
Spiliotis et al. (2020) ask whether forecasting-competition datasets are representative of reality,
and Goktas et al. (2026) propose a framework for time-series foundation models because the field
lacks an accepted evaluation protocol. Bagnall et al. (2017) is the classification equivalent: a
bake-off of recent algorithms on a standard archive, which shows what honest comparison across
many datasets looks like. Wu et al. (2021) ask when early classification, deciding from a prefix of
a pattern, is meaningful at all, which is the right question for any trader who wants to act before
a pattern completes. Wu and Keogh (2020b) is a small but instructive case of the same attitude:
an approximate speed-up for dynamic time warping that turns out to be generally slower than
the exact algorithm.
    Other papers give tools for specific validation questions. Zhang et al. (2025b) propose
influence-function data contribution for time series, which could help find which stretches of
history drive a model; Hounyo and Lahiri (2023) show that the standard pairwise bootstrap
is invalid for model averaging and propose a fixed-design residual-based bootstrap, a result
relevant when combining forecasts; and Chatrath et al. (2002) test for low-dimensional chaos in
commodity futures, finding strong nonlinear dependence but no long-lasting chaotic structure,
a useful reminder that nonlinearity is not the same as predictability. Finally, process and
tooling: Goodwin et al. (2023) document a lag between forecasting advances and organisational
implementation, and Bacry et al. (2017) provide an open-source Python library for point processes
and time-dependent models, the kind of infrastructure that makes research automation cheap.
Desk note. Validation checklist for time-series models (validation stage). Before
any model from this chapter is promoted: (i) test on purged, walk-forward splits; (ii) include
a trivial baseline and report the gap net of costs; (iii) log the number of architectures and
hyper-parameters tried, so the multiple-testing burden is explicit; (iv) use a bootstrap suited to
the forecast combination in question; (v) keep a held-out period untouched. A flawed benchmark
gives a false sense of progress in a lab; a flawed backtest gives the same in production, only with
money attached.


5.11     Bridges out of this chapter

Bridge. Lead–lag and the order book (Chapter 12). What TS mining calls “time-warped,
multi-scale lead–lag” is, in the microstructure literature, the way information propagates from a
liquid instrument to a slower one across venues. Complex correlation on uneven timestamps and
time-varying discrete copulas on tick data speak to the same object as the order-flow papers in
that chapter: dependence that changes within the day. A natural joint project is to test whether
a causal-discovery screen finds the same edges that order-flow-based lead–lag models find, and
whether either survives costs.

Bridge. Non-stationarity and regimes (Chapter 7). What the forecasting community
calls a “distribution shift” or “non-stationary window”, and the regime chapter calls a “change
point” or “state”, are the same phenomenon viewed from two ends. The mixture-of-experts
and regime-aware designs here supply forecasters; the regime chapter supplies the labelling and
detection tools that decide which expert to trust. Likewise, what finance calls a “dependence
breakdown” is what anomaly detection calls a “discord”.




                                                36
Bridge. Validation and research practice (Chapter 14). The benchmark-critique papers
translate directly into backtest language: “flawed benchmark” means “selection bias in the test
set”, “leaky normalisation” means “look-ahead”, and “number of architectures tried” means
“trials” in the multiple-testing sense. Researchers from the time-series community will find a
ready-made vocabulary there; traders will find that the classification bake-off is a worked example
of the discipline they are asked to apply.

Bridge. Text, features and foundation models (Chapters 15 and 6). The multimodal
fusion papers pose the alignment problem that news-driven signals face, while the pre-trained
and self-supervised forecasters connect to learned features. Whenever a vendor says “embedding”,
the TS-mining word is usually “feature vector”; whenever TS-mining says “phenotyping”, the
trader’s word is “factor”.




                                                37
Chapter 6

Representation Learning and Feature
Engineering

Four communities meet in this chapter and rarely read one another. Deep-learning researchers
speak of embeddings, self-supervised pretraining, contrastive views and foundation models. Rough-
path mathematicians speak of signatures and iterated integrals. Topologists speak of persistent
homology, point clouds and landscapes. Time-series miners speak of feature libraries and phase-
space reconstruction. Smaller groups add wavelets, complex networks and computer-vision
imports.
    In the shared vocabulary of the atlas, all of them answer one question: what fixed-size
description of a window of data should downstream models see? A learned embedding, a
signature, a persistence diagram and a hand-built indicator vector are all features. Which of
them sits upstream of a signal or a regime label is a design choice. The vocabulary translates
as follows. A “pretrained encoder” is a feature extractor fitted on data that is not the trading
target. A “foundation model” is such an encoder, or a forecaster, trained on very many series. A
“signature” is a systematic family of ordered cross-moments of a path. A “topological summary”
is a shape statistic of a sliding window.
    In the fund pipeline (data → features → signal → regime/risk → portfolio → execution →
validation) this chapter sits at the features stage. It touches data when synthetic series are used
for pretraining, and it touches forecasting and risk at its edges. Chapter 5 treats classification
and mining of series, and Chapter 8 treats the forecasters that consume these features.


6.1     Signatures: ordered moments of a path
The signature of a path is a graded sequence of iterated integrals. Chevyrev and Kormilitzin
(2026) give a primer on its definition, properties and machine-learning use, and Fermanian (2019)
treats embedding and learning with signatures. Király and Oberhauser (2016) use signature
features to build “sequentialized” versions of any static kernel. Their abstract describes signatures
as an ordered variant of sample cross-moments. That phrase is the most useful translation for a
quant: a signature records not only how far a series moved but in what order its components
moved together.
    Finance entered early. Levin et al. (2013) frame regression with a stream as input and use the
signature’s universality so that linear regression can be applied to streams. Lyons et al. (2014)
propose the signature as a feature set for streams, with an application to high-frequency tick
data. Gyurkó et al. (2014) apply it to market events such as order placement and cancellation.
Order flow then becomes a path, and its signature is a candidate summary of it.
    Newer work makes the transform trainable or compact. Bonnier et al. (2019) combine the
signature with deep-learning frameworks by learning an augmentation before the transform. Li
and Liu (2022) use signatures inside phase-space reconstruction, because ordinary reconstruction

                                                 38
needs a large embedding dimension to be useful. Yin et al. (2022) show the same machinery
on landmark-based human action recognition. We cite it as evidence of portability, not as a
financial result.
Desk note. Signature features for order-flow and trend models (features/signal). A
natural use is to compute truncated signatures over rolling windows of a multichannel path, for
example mid-price, signed volume and spread. The resulting vector feeds a linear or gradient-
boosted model. Choose the truncation depth by out-of-sample validation. The feature count
grows quickly with depth and channels, so regularise heavily. What can go wrong: window length
and time-parametrisation choices act as hidden hyperparameters and invite leakage if tuned on
the test span. Transaction costs at tick frequency can erase thin edges, and the features are hard
to explain to a risk committee without a worked example.


6.2     Topology: shape statistics for crashes, bubbles and co-
        movement
Topological data analysis builds a point cloud from a sliding window and summarises its shape.
Gholizadeh and Zadrozny (2018) survey TDA for time series and systems analysis, and Bailey
(2026) reviews topology as a language for multiscale structure and early-warning signals in
complex systems. In finance, Gidea and Katz (2017) track persistence landscapes of daily index
returns through the 2000 technology crash and the 2007–2009 crisis. Akingbade et al. (2023) offer
a heuristic argument, based on the log-periodic power law singularity model, for why TDA detects
bubbles. Mounir (2025) take a neighbouring route and build a stress measure, the Signature
Fragility Index, from signatures of daily returns in four equity markets, which links the first two
sections.
    Two papers use topology for prediction and grouping. Huang et al. (2024) study stock-index
movement classification and examine point-cloud construction, topological representation and
classifier choice. Jeneret and Diamantis (2025) compare clustering of currencies using TDA-
derived features against traditional statistics. The shared message is that these summaries are
most defensible as slow-moving stress and structure indicators, not as stand-alone alpha.
Desk note. Topological stress monitor (regime/risk). A natural use is a daily job that
computes persistence-based norms on a rolling window of index or cross-asset returns and raises
a flag when the norm rises above its own history. The flag could scale gross exposure down or
trigger human review. What can go wrong: crash episodes are few, so thresholds are fitted on
very small samples. The window and embedding parameters are free, and the series may mainly
restate realised volatility. Compare it against a plain volatility or correlation-eigenvalue baseline
before trusting it.


6.3     Hand-built and transform-based features
Not every representation is learned. Lubba et al. (2019) distil a large time-series feature library
into 22 canonical, interpretable characteristics selected by highly comparative analysis. Silva et al.
(2022) propose a complex-networks feature set, NetF, as an alternative to linear and nonlinear
measures. Razdan (2004) use wavelets on two strongly correlated financial series and show that
correlation can depend on scale. For a pairs desk that is a direct warning: a pair can co-move at
weekly scale and diverge at daily scale.
    Image encodings borrow from computer vision. Pandey et al. (2026) compare Markov
transition field and Gramian angular field images for seq2seq trend prediction, and Li et al.
(2025c) propose ChartNet, a unified chart representation meant to reduce the subjectivity of
technical chart reading. Their value is as a way to feed convolutional models. They should be
judged against the plain indicator vector they replace.

                                                 39
6.4     Self-supervised and contrastive encoders
Contrastive methods learn without labels by pulling together views of the same series. Eldele
et al. (2021) propose TS-TCC, which uses weak and strong augmentations with temporal and
contextual contrasting. Yue et al. (2021) propose TS2Vec, which contrasts hierarchically so
that any sub-sequence has a representation. Meng et al. (2023) review the unsupervised family.
Kusupati et al. (2022) contribute a general idea from outside time series: nested, flexible-capacity
embeddings, so one vector can be truncated to suit a downstream budget.
    Finance adaptations follow. Sun et al. (2022a) propose Self-FTS for intraday stock trading,
motivated by overfitting of ordinary deep learning on validation sets. Demirhat et al. (2026)
combine TS2Vec with 22 engineered features including technical indicators for BUY/HOLD/SELL
classification on Turkish BIST stocks. Hwang et al. (2024) learn temporal embeddings for stock
similarity with SimStock, motivated by the ambiguity of regional and sector classifications.
Dolphin et al. (2022) learn distributed representations of assets, in the spirit of word embeddings,
to model asset correlations. Feng et al. (2025) embed series in context using state-space
reconstruction and a Bayesian nonparametric method. Merchant et al. (2026) argue that financial
representation learning needs state representations useful for planning across tasks.
    Data scarcity is the obstacle. Der et al. (2024a) report that, in their experiments, most
self-supervised time-series models were beaten by simple supervised ones, and that pretraining
on a larger volume of generated samples gave a noticeable improvement. Chapter 3 treats the
generators.
Desk note. Asset embeddings as a universe and neighbour map (features/portfolio).
A natural use is to train a stock-similarity encoder on rolling return windows and use nearest
neighbours to build peer groups, replace stale sector labels, or seed pairs-trading candidates.
Retrain on a schedule and freeze the encoder inside each backtest fold. What can go wrong: an
encoder fitted on the full history leaks the future into every fold, and embeddings drift between
retrains, which breaks identity mapping. Neighbours found during one regime may not hold in
the next, and crowded pair trades have limited capacity.


6.5     Foundation models: pretrained forecasters and tokenisers
The largest group imports the language-model recipe. Ansari et al. (2024) (Chronos) tokenise
scaled and quantised values and train language-model architectures with cross-entropy. Zhou
et al. (2023) reuse a pretrained language model across time-series tasks. Shi et al. (2024b) scale to
billions of parameters with a mixture of experts. Feng et al. (2024) cast multivariate forecasting
as next curve shape prediction for zero-shot use. Liu et al. (2026b) move cross-variate modelling
out of the raw variate space. Gillman et al. (2025) propose a Fourier head for modelling complex
distributions over non-linguistic tokens, which matters for the heavy-tailed returns a tokeniser
must handle.
     Finance-specific models make the case that general models underperform on candlestick
data. Shi et al. (2025b) introduce Kronos for K-line data, covering tasks such as volatility
prediction and synthetic generation. Zhu et al. (2025a) build FinCast to address pattern shifts
from non-stationarity and multi-domain diversity. Ding et al. (2025) present DELPHYNE as a
pretrained model for general and financial series. One study, held as two records of the same
title (Rahimikia et al., 2025a,b), evaluates time-series foundation models in finance. We cite no
figures from any of them. A desk should treat all claims of zero-shot skill as hypotheses until
tested with its own costs and its own universe.




                                                 40
Desk note. Zero-shot forecaster as a benchmark and feature (forecast/validation).
A natural use is to run a pretrained model over daily or intraday bars to produce a forecast
distribution. Treat that output as one more feature, or as a challenger in the model-comparison
harness, never as the sole signal. Log each model version and its pretraining cut-off. What can
go wrong: pretraining corpora may contain the test period, which is silent leakage. Inference
cost scales with universe size, and gains may vanish after costs. Also check calibration of the
quantiles, not just point error.


6.6     Bridges out of this chapter

Bridge. Signatures and order flow (Chapter 12). What microstructure calls an “order-
flow path” or “event sequence” is what rough-path theory calls a multidimensional path whose
signature gives ordered cross-moments. Microstructure asks whether imbalance at one level
precedes the next trade. A depth-two signature term records exactly that kind of ordered
co-movement, so it could serve as a drop-in feature set for imbalance models.

Bridge. Topology and regimes (Chapter 7). A “topological early-warning signal” is, in
change-point language, a statistic whose level shift marks a candidate regime break. Regime
researchers could benchmark persistence-based norms against their usual detectors, and TDA
researchers could borrow detection-delay and false-alarm metrics from that literature in place of
case-study plots.

Bridge. Foundation models, generated data and validation (Chapters 3 and 14).
What the deep-learning silo calls “zero-shot transfer” or “pretraining on generated paths” is what
a systematic fund calls a model whose training data is not its trading data. That makes leakage
tests, purged cross-validation and deflated-performance checks from the backtesting literature
mandatory before any pretrained feature enters production.




                                               41
Chapter 7

Regimes, Change Points, Networks
and Systemic Risk

Five communities talk about the same object in this chapter: a market that behaves differently
from one period to the next. Econophysicists and random-matrix people speak of market states,
eigenvalue spectra and correlation clusters. Time-series machine-learning (TS-ML) people speak
of subsequence clustering, segmentation and state classification. Statisticians speak of change
points, control charts, particle filters and instabilities in forecast performance. Ecologists and
climate scientists speak of tipping points, critical slowing down and early-warning indicators.
Financial economists and network scientists speak of contagion, spillovers, systemic risk and
community structure. Pure mathematicians add path signatures and persistent homology as
feature maps.
    In the shared vocabulary of the atlas, almost all of this is regime-shift, change-point, graph-
network and network-contagion, with a supporting cast of cross-section (a correlation matrix is
a cross-sectional object), order-flow, realized-volatility, topology and path-signature. A “market
state” is a cluster label on a window of data. A “change point” is the boundary between two such
labels. A “tipping point” is a change point that a theory says can be anticipated. A “systemic
risk indicator” is a regime variable computed on a graph rather than on a single series.
    In the fund pipeline the chapter sits between signal and risk: its outputs are rarely alphas.
They are filters, gates, state labels and limits that tell the forecasting models of Chapter 8 and
the portfolio layer of Chapter 13 how much to trust the recent past. The same ideas also serve
validation, because a backtest is only informative about regimes it contains (Chapter 14).


7.1     Market states from correlation structure
The oldest strand treats the correlation matrix of many assets as the state of the market. Drożdż
et al. (2001) apply the correlation-matrix formalism to high-frequency DAX data, and report
that memory effects are shorter than the standard autocorrelation function suggests and that
short repeatable intraday structures exist. Tan et al. (2016) take the same idea to the minute
timescale, combining random matrix theory with complex-network methods to study stock and
sector interactions and a market state. Münnix et al. (2012) define a state of the market from the
similarity of correlation structure and use it to locate points of drastic change in S&P 500 data,
and Pharasi et al. (2020) cluster correlation matrices from sliding epochs for the S&P 500 and
Nikkei 225 into named market states. Domínguez-Monterroza et al. (2025) show the econometric
end of the same toolkit: the eigenvalue spectra of IBEX correlation matrices differ across periods
around COVID-19 and deviate from the random-matrix prediction.
    One study on NSE intraday data, held as two records in the library (Sharma et al., 2018a,b),
uses kernel and functional PCA to find stocks that move together beyond what regression and
correlation capture. It is a cluster tool rather than a regime tool, but the clusters are the building

                                                 42
block of a state definition for an Indian universe.
    The common recipe is: choose a window, compute a correlation matrix, reduce it by eigenvalues
or clustering, and label the window. The recipe has two well-known weaknesses. The window
length trades noise against lag, and the labels are assigned after the fact. Every state is therefore
visible only in hindsight unless it is assigned by a rule that uses past data only (see the online
classifiers below).
Desk note. Correlation-state gate (stage: regime/risk). Input: daily or intraday returns
of the tradable universe. Output: a state label, plus the largest eigenvalue and its share of total
variance, computed on a trailing window. A natural use is to feed the label as a conditioning
variable to the signal-combination layer and to scale gross exposure when the market-mode
eigenvalue is high, since diversification then fails. What can go wrong: states are fitted on
the whole sample (look-ahead leakage), the window is tuned on the same history that is later
backtested, and the state count is chosen post hoc. Fix the window and the clustering rule in a
pre-registered config and refit only on expanding data.


7.2     Latent states and online classification
A second strand fits an explicit state model to returns, volatility or order flow. Salvi (2025)
model order-flow imbalance with an asymmetric hidden Markov model whose transition structure
captures persistence and skewed liquidity, and Koukorinis et al. (2025) combine an HMM with a
kernel machine (SVM/MKL) in a generative-discriminative scheme to classify high-frequency
regimes and predict trade direction. The idea in both is that the generative model supplies
temporally coherent features while the discriminative layer does the labelling. For a desk, this
is the closest link to microstructure: the state is defined on flow, so its output can condition
execution as well as signals (Chapter 12).
    Nystrup et al. (2020) address the latency problem directly with a greedy online classifier,
built on clustering, that assigns a hidden state to each new observation without parsing the
whole history while keeping states persistent, using realized intraday volatility features. Plümer
(2026) take a particle-filter route calibrated to the shape of market stress rather than to specific
historical episodes, and Faris (2026) embed persistent homology in the Hamilton filter so that
volatility regimes are detected from phase-space geometry and not return magnitude alone,
aiming at earlier warning than a second-moment filter.
    The path-signature papers offer a nonparametric alternative. Bilokon et al. (2021) classify
regimes by encoding time series as path signatures, so that separation of regimes corresponds
to clustering, and Issa and Horvath (2023) turn this into an online detector with a path-wise
two-sample test based on a maximum-mean-discrepancy metric using signatures as the feature
map. Both belong to the representation toolbox of Chapter 6.
    Three more papers use ordinary supervised or data-mining machinery. Kurzman et al. (2023)
combine several models to predict the “state” of the market, and Ionescu and Dinsoreanu (2013)
mine markets through market-state classification with a design that gives the analyst control over
which market aspects are analysed. Their value is mainly to show how the problem is framed
as classification, with technical indicators as inputs. Doğan and Aytekin (2026) combine the
Bai–Perron structural-break test with multifractal detrended analysis of S&P 500 prices and
volume, reading the multifractality as evidence against market efficiency. A natural reading for
a desk is that breaks and scaling behaviour are properties to monitor, not tradable signals in
themselves.




                                                 43
7.3     Change points and segmentation
Where a state model asks “which regime are we in”, change-point detection asks “when did the
process change”. Jebali (2026) apply Bayesian online changepoint detection (BOCPD) and two
extensions to order flow, motivated by the need for real-time detection of structural breaks in
any high-frequency system. This is the bridge between this chapter and the order-flow literature:
a break in flow is a candidate trigger for a spread or sizing change.
    The statistical papers supply methods that a desk could borrow. Cribben (2019) detect
change points in the network structure of heavy-tailed multivariate series using precision matrices,
via the graphical lasso and the tlasso, with an unknown number and placement of change points.
Kim and Hwang (2026) build control charts and change-point detectors for multivariate functional
data, using a nonparametric conditional distribution and functional PCA to remove variance
inflation from cross-correlation. Boulton and Lenton (2019) detect abrupt shifts by searching
for gradient changes over a short span, a method conceived for climate series. Banerjee and
Guhathakurta (2020) apply change-point analysis directly to financial networks, linking market
crashes and contagion across countries.
    The TS-ML papers segment without an explicit probabilistic model. Gharghabi et al. (2017)
propose a domain-agnostic online semantic segmentation built on the matrix profile, which
needs few parameters. Köhne et al. (2023) use an autoencoder and iterative modelling to find
change points and cluster subsequences in transient multivariate data from industrial condition
monitoring, and Wang and Hsieh (2021) extend hierarchical factor segmentation to several
dimensions to dissect a multivariate series into phases. The common thread is that each segment
is a candidate regime label, obtained without a return forecast.
    A smaller group addresses non-stationarity as a parameter-inference problem. Owens and
Fulcher (2024) and its extension Owens et al. (2026) infer time-varying parameters of a non-
stationary process through statistical features and slow feature analysis, without learning a
generative model. Saggioro et al. (2020) reconstruct causal relationships that themselves differ
by regime. The proposal for a desk is to treat a lead–lag or causal link as regime-dependent
rather than global.
Desk note. Online break monitor on flow and volatility (stage: signal to risk). Input:
one-minute order-flow imbalance, realized volatility and spread series per instrument. Output: a
run-length posterior from a BOCPD-style detector, thresholded into a “new regime” flag that
shrinks position limits for a cooling-off period and widens execution limits. What can go wrong:
false alarms at the open and around announcements, hazard-rate and prior choices that quietly
encode the history being tested, costs from turnover induced by the flag, and capacity when many
instruments flip together. Validate by counting flags per day out of sample and by measuring
the P&L impact of the action taken, not the detector’s accuracy.


7.4     Adapting forecasts to shifts
If regimes cannot be detected reliably, the alternative is to let models adapt. Wong and Barahona
(2023) build deep incremental learning from decision trees for financial tabular data under
distribution shift, and Zhao et al. (2024a) use meta-learning (DoubleAdapt) to update stock
trend forecasters incrementally as new data arrive. Both fit the retrain-and-roll loop of a
production system; both inherit the usual risk that adaptation speed is tuned on the evaluation
period. Rossi (2021) supplies the discipline: a guide to evaluating and improving forecasts in the
presence of instabilities, including the 2007–08 crisis. The message, as we use it, is that average
out-of-sample loss hides time variation in relative performance, so a desk should report forecast
performance by sub-period and test for instability, not rely on a single pooled score (Chapter 14).




                                                44
7.5     Early warning and critical transitions
Ecology and climate science developed indicators for approaching tipping points: rising variance
and autocorrelation, termed critical slowing down. Scheffer et al. (2009) lay out generic early-
warning signals across systems that, as their abstract notes, include financial markets. Dakos
et al. (2012) compare detection methods on simulated ecological data, Lenton et al. (2012)
compare methods for climate tipping points to improve robustness, and Dakos et al. (2015)
discuss prospects and limitations of resilience indicators. Boettiger and Hastings (2012) quantify
the limits to detection, a cautionary result for any user. For trading, the lesson is that the
indicators are transferable but their false-alarm rate must be measured on the target series.
    The finance papers do exactly that. Guttal et al. (2016) report no critical slowing down
before financial meltdowns, so they are not behaving as critical transitions in the ecological
sense, yet rising variability could still signal systemic risk. This is a negative result with
a constructive use: variance, not autocorrelation, is the candidate indicator. Fabozzi and
Phoa (2026) make a related practitioner argument, that volatility changes character before
magnitude, with rising autocorrelation, volatility of volatility and unstable cross-asset correlations
as diagnostics. Bouchaud (2026) supplies the methodological stance behind the whole strand,
taking scaling, intermittency and extremes as stubborn features of the data to be modelled
instrumentally.
Desk note. Rolling variance-and-autocorrelation risk dial (stage: risk). Input: index
and sector returns, plus cross-asset correlation. Output: a composite of rolling variance, lag-one
autocorrelation and correlation instability, mapped into a risk-limit multiplier. Following the
finance evidence above we would weight variance above autocorrelation, and treat the dial as a
limit, not an alpha. What can go wrong: indicators rise in benign trending markets, windows
are tuned on the crisis they are meant to predict, and a limit tightened too late costs spread and
impact when the whole fund de-risks together. Report hit rate and false-alarm rate on episodes
withheld from design.


7.6     Networks, contagion and systemic risk
The last strand moves from one market to a system. Neela (2025b) model markets as multi-layer
graphs in a “Systemic Risk Radar” for early crash warning. Pawanesh et al. (2024) embed the
Indian stock market, treated as a heterogeneous scale-free network, in hyperbolic space and
cluster it there, while David et al. (2026) detect communities in financial hypergraphs, which
capture higher-order interactions that pairwise networks miss. Dahake and Chakraborty (2026)
model correlations as signed networks and study the loss of structural balance during systemic
stress, and Xiu et al. (2021) use anomalies in time-evolving correlation networks, together with
visibility graphs and a log-periodic power-law extension, to diagnose crashes and predict rebounds.
Rai et al. (2024) detect extreme events with topological data analysis across continental indices
in one pass. Caraiani and Lazarec (2021) measure how monetary policy shocks change Dow
Jones networks using entropy, and Konwar and Dutta (2026) study spillovers between Indian
stock markets and monetary policy.
    The economics papers address transmission. Rajwani and Kumar (2019) measure contagion
between the US and Asian equity markets with time-varying copulas, baghi et al. (2019) use
nonlinear Granger causality for Iranian financial sub-systems, and Rouz et al. (2025) study
spillover dynamics and causal structure in composite stress networks. Zhu et al. (2025b) examine
how bank liquidity hoarding affects the spillovers of Chinese nonfinancial firms. A last pair
concerns the fund’s own industry: Sabkha and Jbir (2025) investigate whether AI diffusion drives
herding and systemic stress, and Meng and Chen (2026b) model AI-driven systemic risk through
performative prediction, herding and cognitive dependency. These matter for a systematic fund
because crowding in similar models is itself a regime variable.

                                                 45
Desk note. Network concentration monitor (stage: risk). Input: rolling correlation or
partial-correlation graphs of positions and factor exposures. Output: a graph-based fragility score
(for example community concentration or balance loss) used as a cap on gross exposure within
a community. What can go wrong: graph estimates are noisy at short windows, thresholding
choices change the topology, and the fund’s own positions move the correlations. Treat the score
as an additional limit next to a conventional covariance stress test, not as a substitute.


7.7     Bridges out of this chapter

Bridge. To time-series mining (Chapter 5). What the matrix-profile community calls a
“semantic segmentation boundary” or a “regime change”, and what the statistics community calls
a “change point”, are the same event. A TS-ML person can supply scalable online algorithms; a
statistician supplies the false-alarm control they usually lack. The shared test is the same: how
many flags per year, and what did acting on them earn net of costs.

Bridge. To microstructure (Chapter 12). An “order-flow imbalance regime” in microstruc-
ture language is a hidden state in an HMM and, in the segmentation vocabulary, a stretch
between two change points. Chapter 12 describes the observable quantities that these detectors
consume; this chapter supplies the latent-state machinery that can sit on top of them.

Bridge. To volatility and portfolios (Chapters 11 and 13). Volatility regime indicators
here play the role that “state variables” play in conditional volatility models, and correlation-state
labels are inputs to covariance shrinkage and risk budgeting. A portfolio engineer can treat the
state label as a switch for the covariance estimator; a volatility modeller can test whether the
topological and signature features add information beyond realized variance.




                                                 46
Part IV

Alpha




  47
Chapter 8

Return Forecasting

Return forecasting is the stage of the fund pipeline where prepared data and features are turned
into a statement about what an asset, or a cross-section of assets, will do next. It sits after
data and features and before regime and risk controls and portfolio construction: its output is a
forecast, a class label, a rank or a conditional distribution that later stages must size, cost and
validate. The papers in this half of the chapter come from many communities that rarely cite
each other. Time-series machine learning (“tsml”) people think in terms of classifiers, kernels
and tree ensembles benchmarked on index or single-stock data. Deep-learning authors speak of
architectures, encoders and losses. Financial economists speak of risk premia, predictors, the
cross-section and out-of-sample R2 . Text and language researchers speak of sentiment and event
propagation. Derivatives researchers bring option-implied information. A few mathematicians
contribute path geometry and chaos theory.
    The atlas translates this vocabulary into a small set of shared terms. What deep learning
calls a “trend label” and tsml calls a “target definition” is label design (Chapter 14 treats it as a
validation matter). What economists call a “return predictor” and practitioners call a “feature”
is an input to a signal. What finance calls the “information coefficient” and learning-to-rank calls
a ranking metric is a forecast-evaluation measure. Where a paper reports accuracy or F1 and
another reports a Sharpe ratio, the atlas asks which stage of the pipeline each number actually
informs, and which costs and capacity limits stand between the number and a fund’s P&L.
    The sections below group the papers by the problem they attack. First come data preparation
and denoising, then the design of targets, then indicator and pattern models, then condition-
ing information from options, liquidity, volume and text, and finally cross-sectional ranking,
benchmarks and probabilistic forecasts. Throughout, a recurring caution applies: most abstracts
report accuracy, backtest returns or Sharpe ratios on a single market and period, and these are
claims by the authors, not evidence of tradable alpha.


8.1     Preparing the input: thinning, differencing and denoising
Many papers argue that the way raw prices are transformed matters more than the model
that follows. Zhang et al. (2023c) make this point for high-frequency data: they propose three
strategies for processing raw intraday data, including data thinning and feature engineering, and
report F1 gains for SVM mid-price classifiers on Dow Jones 30 stocks. Stempień and Gajda
(2025) compare ordinary log returns with fractional differencing and its tempered extension
as preparation for LSTM models on four stock indices, evaluating both forecast errors and
risk-adjusted trading metrics. The idea, standard in the field, is to remove non-stationarity while
preserving as much memory as possible. A natural use of both is as a pre-processing layer that a
research team can test independently of the forecaster.
    A second family decomposes the series before modelling. Li et al. (2026d) split prices into
trend and seasonal components with a wavelet transform and feed a BiLSTM, adding daily price


                                                 48
deviation as a feature. Gao (2026) decompose minute-level A-share prices with CEEMDAN into
intrinsic mode functions, forecast each with an Informer and recombine. Bashir et al. (2026)
likewise decompose with CEEMD before a bagged ensemble of tree models whose hyperparameters
are tuned evolutionarily. Akkas et al. (2026) take a related denoising route, with attention-based
autoencoders applied to indicator-augmented BIST 30 data before an XGBoost trader, and G
et al. (2026) use wavelet and multiscale entropy as interpretable descriptors of informational
complexity inside a cross-attention network. The shared bet is that noise lives at particular
scales. A desk should note the standing risk: if decomposition is fitted on a full sample, later
data leak into earlier components, so any such step must be refitted inside each training window.
    A third family re-represents the series. Wu et al. (2022) map price series to graphs and use
the graph embeddings and node weights as inputs to a temporal-attention predictor. Pandey and
Kumar (2026) reconstruct a phase space of the Nifty 50 using embedding dimension and time
delay before an LSTM, a chaos-theory idea whose abstract carries only an initial and cautious
evaluation. Berrada (2026) ask whether a price window can be summarised by its terminal return
and realized variance, and show with purged out-of-sample tests on 50 ETFs that path-shape
features reject that sufficiency. Da Costa and Gebbie (2020) learn low-frequency patterns on
JSE data with a stacked autoencoder and online updating, and they measure backtest overfitting
with combinatorially symmetric cross-validation and deflated Sharpe ratios. That last point is
unusually careful for this literature. Chapter 6 covers these representation ideas in more depth.
    Two papers address structure rather than transformation. Fu et al. (2026) decompose stock
data into market, sector and idiosyncratic layers, inspired by asset-pricing theory, to separate
systematic risk from idiosyncratic alpha. Xu et al. (2025) use causal representation learning and
domain generalization, with a bound linked to multi-factor models, to find invariant features
that generalise out of sample. Noseda et al. (2025) address a different obstacle, data that cannot
be pooled: desks train a shared LSTM trend classifier by exchanging model updates rather than
raw data, with differential privacy.
Desk note. Window-safe preprocessing (data and features stage). Inputs are raw
OHLCV or intraday bars. A research pipeline can wrap fractional differencing, wavelet or EMD
decomposition and autoencoder compression in a single transform that is fitted only on the
training window and then applied forward, so it can be swapped without touching the forecaster.
The output is a feature matrix plus a stationarity report. What can go wrong: full-sample
decomposition leaks future information; fractional-difference weights and EMD modes change
when the window rolls, which breaks feature stability; and thinning rules tuned on one set of
tickers may not transfer. Report any gain against the same model on plain returns.


8.2     Designing the target: labels and what a “hit” means
If the preceding papers change the input, the next group changes the question. Zhao et al. (2017)
give a formal trend definition based on financial practice and weight training samples by temporal
nearness before an LSTM. Akşehir and Kiliç (2022) propose a rule-based labelling algorithm
and feature selection to cure class imbalance in a CNN that predicts next-day trade actions for
Dow 30 stocks. Feng et al. (2026) use extrema labelling with oversampling and a focal loss for
buy, sell and hold classes on 63 A-share stocks, and Kakad and Shinde (2023) use an adaptive
labelling strategy and test how much training data an intraday LSTM needs. Zeng et al. (2021)
go further: their model selects potentially profitable samples by attention and iteratively refines
noisy labels. These papers all treat the label as a modelling choice rather than a given, which is
the central lesson of this section.
    The triple-barrier method, a standard device in which a position is labelled by whichever
of a profit barrier, a loss barrier or a time limit is hit first, appears in several papers. Kang
(2025) tune the window and barrier width to balance classes for an LSTM on raw OHLCV data


                                                49
in Korean markets. Bieganowski and Ślepaczuk (2025) and Bieganowski and Ślepaczuk (2024)
combine it with supervised autoencoders and noise augmentation, on indices and FX and on
cryptocurrencies respectively; the latter adds fractionally differenced features. MarekKašpar
(2026) apply it with volatility-index predictors on Czech stocks and report that, after transaction
costs, tree models are usually unable to beat buy-and-hold or momentum benchmarks. That
honest negative result is as useful to a desk as any of the positive ones. Gami and Jha (2026)
instead target a rare event, a 50% gain in 30 days on NSE stocks, and argue that hard binary
thresholds create instability near the boundary, which is the label design problem in its sharpest
form.
    Other targets concern timing. Guangliang Gao et al. (2015) cast buy-and-sell prediction as
a survival problem with a Cox hazard model on technical covariates. El-Yaniv and Faynburd
(2012) predict turning points of small swings with Fourier-enriched support vector regression, and
koosha et al. (2022) predict Bitcoin tops and bottoms with forest and recurrent models, scored on
precision. Merello et al. (2019) change the objective rather than the label: they regress returns,
weight samples by magnitude and select the best asset each period. Adriko Debo and Ciresi
(2024) add a Bayesian CNN-LSTM whose uncertainty feeds a Deep Q-learning meta-labeller that
chooses side and size. Meta-labelling, in the practitioner sense, means a second model decides
whether and how much to act on a primary signal.
Bridge. Time-series mining calls a “turning point” or “swing boundary” what statisticians
call a change point and what regime researchers call a regime switch. Chapter 7 treats these as
detection problems, scored by detection delay, while El-Yaniv and Faynburd (2012) and koosha
et al. (2022) treat them as prediction problems scored by trading performance or precision. A
fund could use one team’s labels to bootstrap the other’s benchmark.

Desk note. Label audit and meta-label layer (signal stage). Input: bar data and a
primary signal. Output: a per-bar label plus a sizing or filter score. Triple-barrier parameters
(width, horizon) become hyperparameters that must be chosen inside the training fold; the
Korean-market tuning to balance classes and the Czech cost result together show both sides of
this choice. A natural use of the Q-learning meta-labeller idea is to scale position by predictive
uncertainty. What can go wrong: overlapping barrier windows create serial label dependence that
inflates cross-validation scores, balanced classes do not imply balanced economic payoffs, and a
reported 111% annualised return on A-shares (Feng et al., 2026) says nothing about capacity.


8.3     Indicators, patterns and model zoos
Technical-indicator classifiers are the workhorse of this literature. Rai (2026) build an XGBoost
three-class (down, neutral, up) model on 36 indicators for three large-cap US stocks, stressing
interpretability and hyperparameter tuning. Sharang and Rao (2015) feed deep-belief-network
features of technical indicators into a weekly direction classifier for Treasury note futures. Friday
et al. (2026) add structural features, fair value gaps and rolling Hurst exponents, to a multi-
horizon gated-attention model. Jiang et al. (2021) are the most ambitious of the group: instead
of pre-specified patterns they let a CNN learn from chart images which patterns predict returns,
and report that the patterns differ from standard trend signals and carry across time scales
and countries. Wang and Xie (2024) compare SVM, logistic regression and random forest with
sentiment features on the CSI 300, and Leon et al. (2024) search model pipelines automatically
for a USD/COP limit-order-book trend classifier. Jabbar and Jalil (2024) screen 41 models
on Bitcoin with backtests and forward tests. Grice (2026) tune hyperparameters across three
market regimes and find that no tabular deep architecture beats gradient-boosted trees, though
a rank-aggregated ensemble does well.
    Read together, these papers show a division of labour. Indicator models answer whether a
cheap, auditable feature set carries any short-horizon information; learned-pattern models such

                                                 50
as the chart-image CNN ask whether a flexible learner finds structure that hand-built indicators
miss; and the model-zoo studies ask which learner to trust when the answer is unclear. For a
research-automation stage, the pipeline search and the zoo screen are the most reusable, because
they can be rerun on every new market. Their weakness is selection: screening 41 models, or
searching a genetic pipeline space, multiplies the number of trials, so any reported best model
needs a multiple-testing correction before it enters a signal stack. Chapter 14 covers this in
depth, and Chapter 10 collects the rule-based counterparts of these indicator signals.
    A recurring pattern is that headline accuracy depends on the horizon, the market and the cost
assumption. The tabular-versus-trees result and the Czech cost result are the most transferable
lessons, because they say how much complexity the evidence supports.


8.4     Conditioning information: options, liquidity, volume and
        text
A different strategy adds information that prices alone do not contain. Chinco et al. (2019)
apply the LASSO to the whole cross-section of lagged returns for one-minute-ahead forecasts
and find predictors that are unexpected, short-lived and sparse, often tied to news. Eksi and
Roy (2025) show that an index-inclusion shock raises implied-volatility skew, which then predicts
price reversal. Sharma et al. (2026) combine HMM regimes, a temporal convolutional network
and option-chain microstructure features for 15-minute index direction, reporting higher accuracy
under regime filtering. These connect to Chapter 11.
    Liquidity and volume enter next. Panigrahi (2026) relate a conditional liquidity-volatility
state to short-horizon NIFTY 50 returns and find the one-day association economically modest.
Yang and He (2026) use the ratio of opening to total prior volume as an uncertainty proxy with
a threshold regression for the last half-hour of Chinese stock trading. Sheikh et al. (2026) build a
composite sentiment index from implied volatility, volume, Bitcoin, crude oil and FEARS across
seven economies.
    Text-based forecasts form a distinct cluster, covered in Chapter 15. Yokoyama and Chen
(2026) fuse BERTweet sentiment with price indicators in a hierarchical cross-ticker transformer.
Chen and Kawashima (2025) weight sentiment from connected firms by learned correlation.
Li et al. (2024c) learn lag-dependent causal graphs between stocks alongside a denoised news
encoder. Wu et al. (2026c) observe that 57% of stocks lack posts on a given day on the ACL18
benchmark and that information takes several sessions to propagate, so they model event lags.
Lis et al. (2026) turn Twitter emotion scores into a three-class overreaction signal for intraday
AAPL trading.
Bridge. What the text silo calls an “event-lag distribution” is, in market microstructure,
the decay profile of price impact after a news shock; what economists call “slow information
diffusion” is the same object at daily frequency. Wu et al. (2026c) estimate it from data, while
the volume-regime work of Yang and He (2026) implies a similar time-of-day dependence. See
Chapter 12.

Desk note. Conditioning gates (signal and regime stages). Input: option-chain skew,
open interest, opening-volume ratios or a liquidity-volatility state. Output: a filter that switches
an existing intraday or reversal signal on or off. The opening-volume threshold of Yang and
He (2026) and the regime gating of Sharma et al. (2026) are examples of the form. Failure
modes: thresholds estimated on the full sample are look-ahead; option-derived features need
clean timestamps and are costly to store; filtered subsets of trading days shrink the sample and
capacity.




                                                51
8.5     Cross-sections, benchmarks and probabilistic forecasts
Financial economics asks a different question: which assets will outperform, not what a single
price does. Gu et al. (2018) compare machine-learning methods for measuring risk premia and
trace gains to nonlinear predictor interactions, with momentum, liquidity and volatility dominant.
Kelly et al. (2022) prove that models with more parameters than observations can capture more
return predictability than simple ones. Practical rankers follow: Nakagawa et al. (2020) train
a network with rank information coefficient as stopping criterion and transfer across regions,
and Lin et al. (2026b) optimise rank IC directly inside XGBoost. Duan et al. (2025a) learn
hypergraph factors that supplement human-designed ones. See Chapter 9 and Chapter 13.
    The benchmark studies temper the enthusiasm. Kim (2003) showed early that SVMs are a
promising alternative to neural networks for an index. Qian (2018) report that machine learning
beats ARIMA and GARCH on index data, while Reikard (2026) find only small improvements
over a random walk for monthly exchange rates, which are dominated by serial correlation. Pokou
et al. (2024) hybridise ARIMA with learning models using Student-t errors to respect heavy tails,
and Maung and Swanson (2025) survey forecasting methods around the M6 competition. Fiedor
(2015) take an information-theoretic view, using entropy-rate estimates as a model-free measure
of predictability. Nag (2025) apply forecasts to fundamental valuation, a different horizon from
the rest.
    Generative models give distributions rather than points. Zhou et al. (2018) train an adversarial
LSTM-CNN on high-frequency index data and study how often to update. Vuletić et al. (2024)
produce conditional return distributions with an economics-driven GAN loss, and Liu et al.
(2023b) classify volatility trends with an ordinal-regression GAN. Chapter 3 treats generators
more broadly.
    Taken together, the forecasting-method papers in this section suggest a simple discipline for
a desk. Every candidate model should be compared with a persistence or random-walk forecast
and a regularised linear model on the same data, with the same costs, before any architecture
is admitted; and probabilistic outputs, whether from a GAN or a Student-t hybrid, should be
scored on the quantity the next stage consumes, such as calibrated quantiles for position sizing,
not on point error alone.
Desk note. Rank-IC research loop (forecast stage). Input: a daily panel of features
for a liquid universe. Output: a cross-sectional score handed to the optimiser. A natural use
of the rank-IC objectives is to train and judge with the same metric, then compare against a
random-walk or linear baseline as the benchmark studies demand. What can go wrong: IC
decays quickly after costs, turnover and capacity limits bind, and gains concentrated in small
illiquid names disappear at size.


8.6     Learning who moves with whom: graph models of the cross-
        section
The largest cluster in this half of the chapter starts from one complaint: a per-stock sequence
model treats each name as an island, while a desk trades a cross-section. The remedy is a graph
whose nodes are stocks. The papers differ mainly in where the edges come from, and that is the
most useful way to read them.
    The first family takes edges from outside knowledge. Xu et al. (2022) mine shared information
through web-derived stock concepts and add “hidden concepts” beyond the manual labels. Ye
et al. (2025) build a corporate knowledge graph and fuse it with alpha-factor libraries and
sentiment factors in a Time2Vec transformer. Sawhney et al. (2021) motivate a scale-free relation
graph from the financial literature and embed it hyperbolically, framing stock selection as learning
to rank. Liu et al. (2026a) use industry, valuation and price-movement graphs, refreshed quarterly,


                                                52
to correct the residuals of a LightGBM factor model. That last design is a modest and auditable
way to add a graph to a tabular baseline.
    The second family learns the edges. Uddin et al. (2021) use attention to learn a dynamic firm
network and diffuse accounting fundamentals through it. Zhu et al. (2026a) learn a hypergraph,
with a causal convolutional encoder meant to prevent future information leakage. Yao et al. (2026)
smooth rolling-window correlations to stabilise a dynamic graph and fuse it with a structural
view. Wu et al. (2026a) couple multi-view dynamic graphs with a multi-period temporal encoder,
and Zhang et al. (2027) argue that the order of intra-stock and inter-stock modelling matters, so
they run two paths. Li and Zhang (2026) worry about “crosstalk”: trend, fluctuation and shock
components entangled in one representation, and heterogeneous relations fused indiscriminately.
    A third family asks what an edge should mean. Liu et al. (2026c) want directed, causal
influence from symbolic movement patterns rather than symmetric correlation. You et al. (2026)
model delayed lead–lag dependence in continuous time with a graph neural stochastic differential
equation, and Zhou et al. (2025b) find time-varying lead–lag pairs by sparse cross-attention
and use the leader’s lag-aligned features to forecast the laggard. Zhang et al. (2026g) put
game-theoretic interactions among heterogeneous investors into the graph. Two papers aim for
the opposite of heavy side information. Zhuang et al. (2024) extract inter-stock structure from
stock factors alone, and Zhang and Lu (2024) add a Laplacian correlation penalty to the loss of
a trend network.
Desk note. Graph overlay on a tabular alpha (forecast and portfolio stages). Inputs
are the existing daily factor panel plus one slowly refreshed relation set: industry codes, rolling
return correlations or supplier links. The output is a residual correction to the baseline score,
in the spirit of Liu et al. (2026a), or a rank for a long–short book as in Yao et al. (2026). A
natural use is to run the graph model as a challenger against the plain baseline and promote it
only if its out-of-sample gain survives costs. What can go wrong: correlation graphs estimated
on the same window as the labels leak the target; a learned graph can quietly reproduce sector
exposure, so check neutralised returns; and quarterly or concept data must be stamped with the
date it became public.


8.7     Sequence architectures: transformers, state-space models
        and decompositions
A second cluster is about the temporal encoder itself. Transformers lead. Li et al. (2023) guide
a stock transformer with market information that re-weights features and allows momentary
cross-time stock correlations. Yoo et al. (2021) learn asymmetric, time-varying correlations
along the data axis with multi-level market contexts, and Zu et al. (2023) use sparse static
and dynamic attention combined with predefined relations. Zhang et al. (2026b) transfer a
sentiment-trained transformer to numeric inputs to build factors for Chinese stocks. Surveys
help with orientation: Coelho E Silva et al. (2024) catalogue attention networks for trading
across asset classes, Bhuiyan et al. (2025) review recurrent, convolutional and hybrid deep models
together with their overfitting and interpretability problems, and Pardeshi and Deshmukh (2025)
compare deep, reinforcement and hybrid forecasters.
    Cost and stability form a quieter thread. Li et al. (2025a) replace self-attention with agent
attention, a mixture of experts and LSTM decoding to cut training time, and Mehrabian et al.
(2025) and Hu et al. (2026) pair Mamba blocks with graphs for near-linear cost. This matters
for a desk because a model that retrains cheaply can be walked forward often. Song et al.
(2026a) provide a useful caution: in 1600 runs with a purged chronological split, naively inserted
self-attention collapsed to single-class predictions, while residual attention did not. Architecture
choice is therefore a validation question as well as an accuracy question.
    Several papers decompose the input before modelling. Huang et al. (2026b) separate trend,


                                                53
cyclical and high-frequency streams for futures. Lu and Zhang (2026) split returns into low-,
intermediate- and high-frequency branches fused by a reliability gate, for horizons of one to
twenty days. Bohra and Vijay (2026) fuse penultimate-layer features of several LSTM variants
across global indices, while Gil et al. (2024) benchmark TCN, N-BEATS, TFT, N-HiTS, TiDE
and an xLSTM variant, with wavelet denoising, on S&P 500 and Brazilian ETF data. Noel (2023)
and Dixon et al. (2016) are earlier, simpler cases: a dynamic neural network for daily closes, and
deep classifiers of five-minute futures direction backtested with a simple rule. Pillay and Ezugwu
(2019) train a feedforward network with a metaheuristic against particle-swarm, genetic and
ARIMA competitors. These are best read as benchmarks and history, not as templates. Their
value for a modern pipeline is as baselines: a research team that cannot beat a small recurrent
or feedforward network on the same features and splits should not be adding graph or attention
machinery on top.
Desk note. Architecture bake-off harness (research-automation and validation
stages). Inputs are one fixed feature panel, purged chronological splits and several random seeds.
The output is a table in which every deep model must beat a naive forecast and a tuned linear
or boosted model before it is allowed near production. A natural use is to run the multi-seed
collapse check of Song et al. (2026a) as an automatic gate, rejecting models that emit one class.
What can go wrong: price-level error metrics such as MAPE flatter near-random-walk forecasts;
reported gains from one index, one test year or a few stocks may be regime luck; and compute
budget, not accuracy, is often the binding constraint on retraining frequency.


8.8     What to predict: labels, horizons and classification
Several papers change the target rather than the model. Friday et al. (2025) predict buy, hold
and sell classes at 1-, 3-, 7- and 10-day horizons jointly, so the signal can be matched to a holding
period. Jiang et al. (2025) recast index regression as classification of binary-encoded digits,
fusing constituent-stock embeddings. Bai and Pukthuanthong (2026) go furthest in portfolio
terms: classification into return deciles beats matched regression in long–short construction, and
the advantage persists after transaction costs, according to their abstract. Yang et al. (2026b)
predict direction, volatility and volume jointly through a liquidity-driven mixture of experts,
with a portfolio mechanism on top. Kim and Song (2026) rank stocks with vector-quantised
latent factors combined with expert priors.
     These ideas share a message: the label is a design variable, and a cross-sectional ranking
objective is often closer to what a book needs than a squared price error. The neighbouring
papers work with deciles, ranks and movement classes, not point price forecasts.
     The proposals below are our synthesis, not claims of the papers. A desk that trades a monthly
rebalanced book could try the decile framing of Bai and Pukthuanthong (2026) as a challenger to
its regression target, keeping features, universe and cost model fixed so that only the loss changes.
A desk that holds positions for several days could use the joint horizons of Friday et al. (2025)
to check whether the one-day and ten-day views agree before sizing up; disagreement between
horizons is itself a usable filter. The multi-task design of Yang et al. (2026b), which predicts
volatility and volume alongside direction, hints at a third use: the auxiliary outputs can feed
the sizing and cost stages directly, so the forecaster stops being a single number and becomes a
small bundle of inputs to the portfolio and execution layers. The caveat is class imbalance and
overlapping labels: multi-day labels overlap in time, so any validation scheme must purge and
embargo around them.




                                                 54
8.9     Non-stationarity and regimes inside the forecaster
Return models degrade when market behaviour changes, and a large group builds the response
into the model. Lin et al. (2021) add a temporal routing adaptor with optimal transport that
sends each sample to one of several pattern-specific predictors. Sarkar (2026) gate attention by a
latent market regime, group 95 features into 11 semantic categories and train with a Sharpe-aware
objective; the paper also reports correcting alignment and backtesting issues that can inflate
trading results. Ceci et al. (2027) limit how much stock-specific signal is written into hidden
states during market-wide moves. Wang and Lera (2026) condition a prior-data fitted network on
recent feature–return pairs to adapt without regime labels. Liu et al. (2025a) build meta-learning
tasks from Gaussian-mixture regime clusters for zero-shot forecasting, and Xu et al. (2026c)
select training samples from historical environments whose causal drivers resemble the present
one, with a quantile-focused regulariser against non-implementable alphas.
    Not every treatment is neural. Anshuman et al. (2022) choose among predictors of the Indian
equity risk premium according to the level of policy uncertainty, and Sharma (2025) map Nifty
valuation to horizon-specific return distributions over 34 years. Cherifi et al. (2017) forecast
inter-stock distances by tensor decomposition as an early signal of herding-driven correlation
spikes. H and P (2021) test crypto ensembles trained in turmoil and tested in a bear market.
Alaminos et al. (2026) combine ARMA–GARCH with neural and fractal components for the
GameStop short squeeze on intraday data.
    The sharpest statement of the premise is Farmer et al. (2018): predictability is local in time,
with short pockets of significant coefficients between long stretches of none. If true, a regime
gate is a way of switching a signal off, not only of improving it.
Desk note. Predictability gate (regime and signal-weighting stages). Inputs are a
signal’s recent out-of-sample errors and a few state variables such as volatility level or policy
uncertainty. The output is a multiplier on that signal’s weight, possibly zero. A natural use is to
estimate it as a rolling, time-varying-coefficient diagnostic in the spirit of Farmer et al. (2018)
and compare it against the learned routing in Lin et al. (2021). What can go wrong: gates tuned
on the same history that defines the regimes overfit badly; a gate that switches on after the
pocket has started adds lag; and few regime episodes means few effective observations.


8.10      Uncertainty, noise and probabilistic output
A forecast without a confidence is hard to size. Lin and Sun (2026) combine a CNN–Transformer
with a Gaussian process prior to obtain calibrated predictive intervals; their abstract states
identifiability, consistency and coverage results and an uncertainty-penalised Kelly rule with
a CVaR constraint. Yu et al. (2026c) address a bookkeeping problem in probabilistic candle
forecasts, where quantiles cross or the forecast low exceeds the open or close, and project forecasts
post hoc so that they are consistent without retraining. Together they suggest a forecast should
be passed on as a distribution that is internally coherent, not a bare number. For a desk the
practical consequence is a handoff contract between the forecast and portfolio stages. A forecaster
that emits a mean, a calibrated interval and a flag for coherence gives the optimiser something
to penalise, and gives the risk layer a quantity to cap. The same logic applies to stop and target
distances, which are distances in a predictive distribution and not arbitrary round numbers.
What should worry the reviewer is calibration drift: intervals fitted in a calm year are too narrow
in a stressed one, so coverage needs to be monitored live and not assumed from the training
sample. The frequency-decoupled forecaster of Lu and Zhang (2026) also includes quantile and
likelihood terms in its objective, which places it in the same family.




                                                 55
8.11     Pattern and shape-based approaches at the margin
The time-series-mining silo appears here in a small way. Kim et al. (2025) extract recurrent,
amplitude- and warping-invariant patterns and classify direction with interpretable shapelets on
Bitcoin and large equities. Karbasian et al. (2025) add a persistent-homology loss to a generative
adversarial forecaster so generated five-minute series keep their topological structure. Sen and
Chaudhuri (2021) combine technical-analysis concepts with statistical and machine-learning
classifiers on five-minute NSE data. Their neighbours in the graph and transformer sections
rarely cite them, and the reverse is equally true; see Chapter 5 for the mining view of the same
ideas. A natural use is to treat shapelet or motif libraries as interpretable feature generators
for a short-horizon classifier, with each retained pattern reviewed by a human before it enters
production. The topological loss is more exotic; its proposed role here is as a regulariser that
keeps synthetic or forecast paths realistic, which links to the generator work in Chapter 3. The
risk with all three is that a pattern found on one instrument over a short sample is easy to
over-read, so out-of-sample pattern stability across assets deserves its own test before any of
these features is trusted. Finally, the earlier machine-learning comparisons are mostly narrow in
universe and period, so they are better treated as hypotheses to replicate than as evidence.


8.12     Does the forecast beat a hard benchmark?
The most important papers for a skeptical desk are the benchmark papers. Guo et al. (2026b)
find that even ridgeless and ridge models with huge parameter counts fail to outperform a simple
historical average, consistent with asymptotic results under zero true slopes. Bandi et al. (2026)
argue that the zero forecast is too easy a benchmark (in their words, excessively conservative),
because beating it may reflect only market-level timing, and propose the more stringent panel
historical mean; relative to it, only flexible nonlinear models show consistent gains. Puoti et al.
(2024) find that crypto series resemble Brownian noise and that naive models beat complex
ones. ROY (2022) test randomness on intraday Nifty data with mixed verdicts, and Shah et al.
(2026b) compare classical, deep, reinforcement and quantum learners under a leakage-free pipeline.
The conceptual frame is offered by Alonso (2026a), whose bounded-learner notion of structure
penalises description length, and the historical backdrop by Cutler et al. (1989), a classic study
of what moves prices (no abstract is available, so nothing more is claimed).
Desk note. Benchmark-first model approval (validation stage). Inputs are candidate
forecasts, realised returns and the panel mean computed with information available at each date.
The output is a pass/fail check: a candidate must beat both the historical-mean benchmark of
Bandi et al. (2026) and a naive forecast, after costs, in a majority of sub-periods, before any
capital is assigned. A cheap pre-screen is a complexity or randomness test along the lines of
Puoti et al. (2024) and ROY (2022) to decide whether a market deserves modelling effort. What
can go wrong: a benchmark that is too weak passes level effects rather than cross-sectional skill;
the screens are low-power; and passing them once says little about the next regime.




                                                56
8.13      Bridges out of this chapter

Bridge. Graph learners and the risk model (Chapters 13 and 7). What the deep-
learning silo calls a “learned dynamic stock graph” (Uddin et al., 2021; Yao et al., 2026) is, for the
risk team, a time-varying covariance or dependence structure, and “rolling-window correlation
smoothing” is a shrinkage estimator by another name. In the network literature of Chapter 7
the same object is a contagion network, and the tensor-distance forecasts of Cherifi et al. (2017)
are an early-warning indicator on it. Conversely, risk people could ask the graph modellers for a
cheap out-of-sample test: does the learned graph predict next quarter’s covariance better than a
shrunk sample estimate?

Bridge. Lead–lag, order flow and execution (Chapter 12). The “lead–lag pair” of
Zhou et al. (2025b) and You et al. (2026) is what microstructure writers describe as information
diffusing from a liquid asset to an illiquid one, or as price discovery. The microstructure term
“latency arbitrage” covers the fastest end of the same spectrum. A shared vocabulary lets the
forecaster ask for the delay at which a pair stops being tradable after costs, and lets the execution
team see that the same lag estimates could schedule child orders in the laggard.

Bridge. Regime language across silos (Chapters 7 and 5). The forecasting papers speak
of “routers”, “gates”, “meta-tasks” and “domain discovery” (Lin et al., 2021; Liu et al., 2025a;
Xu et al., 2026c); the time-series-mining silo speaks of change-points and discords; and Farmer
et al. (2018) speak of pockets of predictability. These are three vocabularies for one question:
when has the data-generating process changed? A change-point detector from Chapter 7 could
supply the labels a router lacks, and a router’s assignment entropy could serve as a change-point
score. The second wish is a proposal and not a finding of these papers.

Bridge. Targets, costs and the backtest (Chapters 14 and 10). The classification-versus-
regression result (Bai and Pukthuanthong, 2026) and the multi-horizon labels of Friday et al.
(2025) connect to the labelling schemes (for example triple-barrier) of Chapter 14, where a label
is a trade outcome. The skeptics (Guo et al., 2026b; Bandi et al., 2026) supply a null hypothesis
that the approval checklists in that chapter should adopt by default.




                                                 57
Chapter 9

Factors and Relative Value

Three communities meet in this chapter and rarely read one another. Financial economists
(the fin-econ silo, nearly half of the papers here) speak of anomalies, risk premia, factor
models, alpha measured against a benchmark set of factors, and limits to arbitrage. Their unit of
evidence is the sorted portfolio, the Fama–MacBeth cross-sectional regression and the t-statistic.
Machine-learning and decision-theory researchers speak of alpha mining, formulaic alphas, search
spaces, rewards and Markov decision processes; their unit of evidence is the information coefficient
and the backtest of a pool of expressions. A smaller group from derivatives, time-series mining
and statistics speaks of implied-volatility spreads, lead–lag clustering, cointegration, copulas and
spreads.
    In the shared vocabulary of the atlas these dialects collapse to a few terms. An anomaly, a
formulaic alpha and a latent factor are all a cross-sectional feature: a number computed per asset
per date that is meant to rank assets by future return. A factor model is a risk and attribution
layer that says which part of a signal’s return is already available from known exposures. Pairs
trading, statistical arbitrage and relative value are spread signals: a feature defined on a group of
assets whose relative price is assumed to revert. Limits to arbitrage, crowding and alpha decay
are capacity and regime dependence of the signal.
    In the fund pipeline the chapter sits between features and signal (a feature is turned into
a ranked forecast), and it reaches forward into regime and risk (factor exposure, crowding),
portfolio construction (long–short legs, beta neutrality) and validation (is an anomaly real, or
data mining?). Chapters 8 and 10 cover forecasts and signals built on time-series dynamics; this
chapter covers the cross-sectional and relative-value side.


9.1     Mining formulaic alphas
A formulaic alpha is a short algebraic expression over price, volume and cross-sectional operators
that returns one score per stock. Kakushadze (2016) publish 101 such expressions as code,
report average holding periods of roughly 0.6 to 6.4 days and low average pairwise correlation
among them, and find returns tied to volatility rather than turnover. That paper is the natural
corpus and baseline for the rest of this section, which asks how to search the space of expressions
automatically.
   The first generation used evolutionary search. Zhang et al. (2020) build a hierarchical
evolutionary algorithm that exploits a pattern in alpha structure to locate promising regions of
the space. Cui et al. (2021) try to unite two classes: formulaic alphas generalise well and form
weakly correlated sets, while machine-learning alphas on vector and matrix features are more
predictive but harder to diversify. A related symbolic route is Zuo and Jiang (2025), who apply
symbolic regression to asset pricing, searching function forms rather than fixing a linear one.
   The second generation casts construction as sequential decision-making. Yu et al. (2023)
generate synergistic collections by reinforcement learning, rewarding an expression for what it


                                                 58
