adds to the set; Shin et al. (2024) extend the search space and seed it with a pretrained alpha
set. Xu et al. (2024) argue that earlier genetic-programming methods are sensitive to their
initial population and pursue logical formulaic alphas through deep reinforcement learning, while
Zhao et al. (2024b) bound the variance of REINFORCE to obtain steadier factors. The central
difficulty these papers name is a sparse reward, since feedback arrives only when a formula is
complete. Zhao et al. (2025b) attack it with trajectory-level reward shaping learned from expert
factors, Zhu and Zhu (2025) use distributional reinforcement learning against a non-stationary,
reward-sparse process, and Yang et al. (2026a) constrain the search with an alpha-oriented
context-free grammar so that every candidate is syntactically valid and interpretable.
    A third family replaces reinforcement learning with generative models. Chen et al. (2025a) use
generative flow networks for structure-aware exploration, and Jin et al. (2026) generate formulas
with pool-aware hierarchical discrete diffusion, valuing each formula by the complementary
information it adds to a pool. Shi et al. (2024a) stress that fixed-weight combinations perform
inconsistently and mine factors together with a dynamic combination. Zhao and Sha (2026)
move the problem to order-book and price–volume data with a quality-diversity multi-agent
search whose stated aim is robustness after execution costs and diversity of factor families. Yu
et al. (2026b) survey the field from an evolutionary-computation view, listing noisy fitness,
nonstationarity, costly backtests and semantic redundancy as constraints, and Ding et al. (2026)
propose an evaluation framework because backtesting is expensive and correlation measures are
narrow.
Desk note. Alpha-mining loop with a quarantine. Pipeline stage: signal generation
with research automation. Inputs: adjusted daily or intraday price–volume panels, an operator
grammar, and the desk’s existing live-alpha pool. Output: candidate expressions, each scored
by its marginal contribution to the pool rather than its stand-alone information coefficient,
as the synergy papers above suggest. A natural use is to run a mining method such as the
grammar-guided or pool-aware searches only on an early window, then pass survivors to a frozen,
cost-aware holdout evaluated by a framework like AlphaEval. What goes wrong: every candidate
evaluated is a trial, so the multiple-testing burden grows with search effort (Chapter 14); mined
formulas often chase high-turnover patterns that die after costs; and pools mined in one regime
inherit its dependence.


9.2     Factor models, learned and priced
Classical factor models explain returns by a few exposures. The learned versions replace the fixed
exposures. Duan et al. (2022) propose FactorVAE, a probabilistic dynamic factor model that uses
a variational autoencoder to predict cross-sectional returns under a low signal-to-noise ratio. Zhao
et al. (2026b) argue that such latent-factor models capture overall market conditions but neglect
the temporal patterns of individual stocks and the richness of multiple factors, and respond with
a dual vector-quantised autoencoder. Gopal (2024) go in the generative direction, enhancing
classical factor models with deep generative modelling of equities. In the atlas vocabulary these
are representation learners (Chapter 6) whose latent layer plays the role of a covariance and
attribution model.
     The finance-economics response is a warning about scale. Martin and Nagel (2022) model
investors facing thousands of potentially relevant characteristics and show how conventional
efficiency tests must be reassessed when prediction is high-dimensional, with Bayesian shrinkage
or sparsity governing what investors can learn. For a desk this is a statement that the same
dimensionality that makes machine-learned factors attractive also limits what any one of them
is worth. At the other end, Holcblat et al. (2022) give simple tests to separate a risk-based
explanation of high expected returns from frictions or behavioural bias, a question that decides
whether a factor is a premium to hold or an anomaly to harvest and expect to fade. Buraschi et al.


                                                59
(2010) show the same point for hedge funds: apparent market-neutral alpha is often associated
with significant exposure to correlation risk.


9.3     Option-implied information in the cross-section
Options data give a second set of cross-sectional features, and the derivatives silo supplies them.
An et al. (2012) find that stocks whose call implied volatilities rose over the prior month earn
high future returns, while those with rising put implied volatilities earn low ones, with spreads
of about one percent a month. Bali and Murray (2019) turn related quantities into factors,
namely implied minus realised volatility, the call–put implied-volatility spread and their changes,
to explain optionable-stock returns. Chordia et al. (2021) document a positive link between
risk-neutral skewness and later returns and tie it to informed trading around news. Shafaati
et al. (2026) move to the options themselves, using LASSO with post-selection inference over 130
characteristics of delta-hedged option returns. Zhou et al. (2025a) split implied skewness from
Chinese index options into upper and lower parts to test lottery preferences.
    The timeliness caveat comes from Hosseinkhani (2026), who find the implied-volatility spread
robust across 1996–2024 while the sign of the option-to-stock volume ratio reversed after 2020.
Options features are therefore usable signals, but their parameters belong to the regime layer
(Chapter 11).


9.4     Beta, lottery, volatility and liquidity anomalies
The best-documented anomaly cluster here is low beta. Frazzini and Pedersen (2014) explain it
with leverage constraints: constrained investors bid up high-beta assets, and a betting-against-
beta portfolio is tested across equities, bonds and futures. Replication and critique follow.
Agarwalla et al. (2014) find a BAB factor earning significant returns in India that dominates
the size, value and momentum factors, whereas Rakhyani (2021) find the beta anomaly present
but the premium small on raw returns. Novy-Marx and Velikov (2018) attribute much of the
performance to non-transparent construction that effectively equal-weights stock returns; Liu
et al. (2018) argue the anomaly stems from beta’s correlation with idiosyncratic volatility and is
significant only among overpriced stocks; and Buchner and Wagner (2015) suggest that linear
CAPM regressions are misspecified for levered firms. Huang et al. (2015) add a timing dimension:
arbitrage activity produces booms and busts in the strategy’s abnormal profits.
    Neighbouring effects share the lottery and risk interpretations. Bali et al. (2008) find that the
maximum daily return in the past month negatively predicts returns, and Aziz and Ansari (2018)
test the minimum daily return in India, finding an effect concentrated among smaller stocks.
Idiosyncratic volatility gets mixed treatment: Aziz et al. (2017) report a positive but fragile
relation in India, sensitive to weighting, size group and sample, while Kumari (2026) revisit it in
1,356 firms with a dual measurement framework. Liquidity is the fourth leg: Bekaert et al. (2007)
show that a zero-return measure predicts emerging-market returns where turnover does not,
and Sethy and Tripathy (2024) price systematic liquidity risk in India with a liquidity-adjusted
CAPM.
Desk note. Anomaly screen for a long–short book. Pipeline stage: feature and signal,
then validation. Inputs: monthly or weekly universe with betas, trailing maximum and minimum
returns, idiosyncratic volatility, a zero-return liquidity proxy and market capitalisation. Output:
ranked features and an exposure report against the desk’s factor set. A natural use is to build
each anomaly with transparent weights, then check whether its return is explained by BAB, size
and value after costs. What goes wrong: results depend on weighting scheme and size bucket
(the Indian volatility evidence is explicitly sensitive to both), short legs may be hard or costly to
borrow, and construction choices can carry hidden leverage.


                                                 60
    A practical reading of these four sections together is that a factor is a claim with three layers:
a construction (weights, universe, lookback), an explanation (risk, constraint or behaviour) and
a decay story. Papers in this chapter usually supply one or two. The mining literature supplies
constructions in bulk but little explanation; the anomaly literature supplies explanations but
rarely a deployable construction with costs; and the crowding literature supplies decay stories. A
desk that wants durable signals should require all three before capital is assigned, and should
record which layer each live signal is missing so that its review cadence matches its fragility. A
signal with a clean behavioural explanation but no capacity estimate deserves small size; one
with a strong backtest and no explanation deserves a short leash and a pre-agreed kill rule.
    Emerging-market replication also deserves its own place in that reading. Several papers
here test Western anomalies in India or China, where retail participation, settlement rules and
short-selling constraints differ. For a desk trading those markets the useful output is not the
sign of the effect but the conditions under which it appears: small stocks, particular weighting
schemes or a given sample period. Those conditions are the first things to carry into the feature
definition and the cost model.


9.5     Calendar effects and lead–lag structure
Calendar effects are the weakest evidence in the chapter. Nath and Dalvi (2008) study the
day-of-the-week effect in Indian equities with high-frequency and daily data and find that the
significant days changed after rolling settlement was introduced in 2002, a case in which a
market-structure rule change moved an anomaly. Brusa et al. (2003) test whether a reverse
weekend effect is spread across industries, Elangovan et al. (2022), Chawla et al. (2024) and
Tripathi (2026) examine month-of-the-year effects in Indian broad and sectoral indices, with
Chawla et al. (2024) adding volatility models. On their own they are descriptive checks and, for
a desk, mainly examples of what a validation stage must treat with suspicion: small samples,
many tested calendars and structural changes. Lead–lag evidence is less seasonal and more
structural. Sahoo and Kumar (2025) use Granger causality, impulse responses and variance
decomposition to find that oil and gas leads several other Indian sector indices, which could serve
as a sector-rotation input if it survives out of sample.


9.6     Crowding, decay and the limits to arbitrage
A cross-sectional signal does not stay alone. Barroso and Santa-Clara (2015) show that the risk
of momentum is predictable and that managing it nearly doubles the Sharpe ratio while virtually
eliminating crashes; this is the template for a volatility-scaled factor sleeve. Arnott et al. (2019)
list three blunders in factor investing: exaggerated expectations from data mining, crowding and
unrealistic trading-cost assumptions; downside shocks far larger than naive risk management
would suggest; and diversification that vanishes when factor returns become more correlated in
some economic conditions. Deng and Sammon (2026) show that passive inflows reduce realised
returns of long–short accounting anomalies through price impact, especially on short legs, and
Unterberg (2026) connect passive growth to a decline in active-fund alpha after 2010. Meng
and Chen (2026a) formalise the machine-driven case with an alpha half-life that shortens as AI
adoption rises.
Desk note. Crowding and decay monitor. Pipeline stage: regime and risk. Inputs: live
signal returns, rolling factor exposures, passive-flow or ownership proxies where available, and
realised factor volatility. Output: a position-scaling rule (volatility targeting in the style of
risk-managed momentum) plus a decay flag when a signal’s rolling information coefficient falls
below a threshold set at research time. What goes wrong: the monitor lags the damage, flow
proxies are noisy, and decay estimates from a short window are indistinguishable from an ordinary
drawdown.

                                                 61
9.7     Relative value and pairs trading
Pairs trading replaces the question of which stocks will rise with which relationship will revert.
The pipeline has two stages, choosing pairs and trading the spread, and the papers divide
accordingly.
    For selection, the clustering literature begins with Mantegna (1998), who derives a hierarchical
tree and an economic taxonomy from the correlation matrix of daily stock log-price differences.
Millington and Sánchez Granero (2022) compare filters (minimum spanning tree, planar and
triangulated maximally filtered graphs) on stock returns from five markets. Gupta and Chatterjee
(2020) add a lead–lag-aware distance to pair selection, and Deng and Zhang (2026) compare
clustering algorithms for lead–lag relationships, aiming to fix the cost and cluster-count sensitivity
of DTW-KMedoids. Korniejczuk and Ślepaczuk (2024) combine graph clustering with the Kelly
criterion and classifier ensembles in a multi-pair US strategy designed to withstand transaction
costs.
    For trading the spread, Mudchanatongsuk et al. (2008) model the log relationship as an
Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process and solve a closed-form stochastic-control problem. Stander et al.
(2013) replace linear correlation with bivariate copulas to identify mispricing. Guo et al. (2025) use
the path-signature transform for an interpretable high-frequency futures pair strategy. Because
cointegration can fail, Dai et al. (2025b) filter VECM-based trades using violations of asymptotic
properties, and JY et al. (2021) combine structural-break detection with deep reinforcement
learning. Wilkens (2025) supply the sober check by studying whether pairs trading still works in
Germany from 2000 to 2023.
Desk note. Pairs book with break filter. Pipeline stage: signal, with portfolio and risk.
Inputs: adjusted daily or intraday prices for a liquid universe and a sector map. Output: a pair
list from cluster-then-test selection, spread z-scores as entry and exit signals, and a stop that
flags suspected structural breaks. What goes wrong: selection and testing on the same window;
costs and borrow on both legs; and pairs that decouple in precisely the stressed periods when
the book is most levered.


9.8     Bridges out of this chapter

Bridge. Alpha mining and the backtest-overfitting literature (Chapter 14). The
machine-learning phrase “search space exploration” is what statisticians call a multiple-testing
problem, and a “reward” is a backtest metric. The mining papers here mostly state the overfitting
danger in their own terms, such as noisy fitness; the backtesting chapter’s tools could supply the
missing deflation.

Bridge. Correlation filtering and regime networks (Chapter 7). What time-series mining
calls a “filtered correlation graph” or “subdominant ultrametric”, econophysics and systemic-risk
work call a “network of contagion”. The same maximally filtered graphs that choose pairs could
track when clusters merge, a regime signal for the risk layer.

Bridge. Options-implied features and volatility (Chapter 11). A derivatives desk’s
“call–put implied-volatility spread” is an equity factor-builder’s “informed-trading characteristic”.
The factor-sorting framework gives volatility surfaces a standard test; the volatility chapter gives
the surface its dynamics.

Bridge. Crowding and market structure (Chapter 4). What fin-econ calls “inelastic
demand from passive flows” is, in microstructure vocabulary, price impact from non-informational
order flow. Cost and capacity estimates for the long–short legs belong with the execution chapter,
Chapter 12.

                                                 62
Chapter 10

Strategies and Signals

Strategy research is the loudest and least coordinated corner of the trading literature. Finance
economists write about “technical trading rules”, “time-series momentum” and “anomalies”;
evolutionary-computation people write about “rule discovery” and “fitness”; reinforcement-
learning (RL) groups write about “agents”, “rewards” and “policies”; time-series-mining authors
write about “pattern recognition” and “motifs”; practitioners write about “liquidity sweeps”,
“volume profile” and “support and resistance”. In the atlas’s shared vocabulary these are all
attempts at the same step: turning a stream of prices and volumes into a signal, meaning a
position or a forecast that a portfolio layer can size. A “technical indicator” is a hand-built
feature, a “trading rule” is a thresholded feature, and an RL “policy” is a learned mapping from
state features to positions.
    This half of the chapter covers the stage between features and portfolio: signal definition and
signal search. It sits after the data and feature stages (Chapters 5 and 6) and before regime,
risk and portfolio logic (Chapters 7 and 13). Many of the papers below report a backtest of a
complete “strategy”, so the line between signal, sizing and execution is blurred, and a reader
should expect to decompose each strategy into its parts.
    The second thing to hold in mind is validation. Almost every paper here searches over
indicators, windows, architectures or reward functions, which makes it a multiple-testing problem
in disguise (Chapter 14). We read each paper for the idea it contributes, and we treat reported
profitability as a claim to be re-tested on our own data with our own costs.


10.1      Trend and technical rules: the economics of simple signals
The oldest question is whether simple price rules carry information. Bessembinder and Chan
(1995) test simple technical rules on Asian stock indices and find them more successful in emerging
markets such as Malaysia, Thailand and Taiwan than in Hong Kong and Japan. That pattern,
that rules work where markets are less developed, is a reminder that signal value is tied to
market structure (Chapter 4). Han et al. (2013) apply a moving-average timing rule to portfolios
sorted by volatility and report abnormal returns relative to CAPM and the Fama–French three-
factor model, with the effect strongest for high-volatility portfolios. For a desk this reads as a
conditioning result: the same rule behaves differently depending on the risk of the instrument it
is applied to.
    Trend following has a more developed literature. Baltas and Kosowski (2013) show that
better volatility estimation and trend detection can cut time-series momentum turnover by more
than a third without a statistically significant performance penalty. This is the cleanest example
in the chapter of a paper whose contribution is cost engineering rather than alpha. Basanisi and
Torresetti (2023) test a four-moving-average trend strategy with a trailing stop on 205 European
stocks over 2015–2022 and propose a novel variant, and Dong et al. (2025b) combine technical
rules with a liquidity-premium strategy in the Chinese market and report that the combination


                                                63
beats either component alone. The last two are single-market, short-sample studies, so we treat
them as candidate designs, not as established premia.
    The cautionary side is a breakdown. Kurth et al. (2026) document that short-term trends in
about 100 liquid futures have stopped delivering reliable returns since roughly 2009, and offer a
microstructural account that depends on signal speed and asset class. T and T (2022) examine
market conditions and large downside risks in trend following, motivated by diminished equity
profitability, and suggest remedies for trend reversals. Read together, these two papers say that
a trend signal is a time-varying asset whose decay should be monitored (Chapter 7).
Desk note. Trend-signal monitor (features → signal → validation). A natural use
is to run a small bank of trend signals at several speeds on a futures universe, each with a
volatility-scaled position and a turnover estimate, and to track net-of-cost performance by speed
on a rolling basis, in the spirit of the speed and asset-class dependence described by Kurth et al.
(2026) and the turnover reduction of Baltas and Kosowski (2013). The output is a per-speed
on/off flag or a risk-limit multiplier for the portfolio layer. Failure modes: the monitoring window
is short relative to the decay it tries to detect, so switching rules chase noise; cost estimates
taken from daily data understate the cost of fast signals; and trend and carry exposures overlap
with the factor book (Chapter 9).
    A different tradition treats trading as a control problem. Abbracciavento et al. (2022) use
a feedback approach, in which price is an exogenous disturbance to a loop and a controller
is synthesised, avoiding an explicit model of non-stationary dynamics. A natural use is as a
benchmark for adaptive sizing against learned policies. Two papers on intraday and indicator
design fit here. Tian et al. (2012) apply the ACD rules and pivot-point system, a practitioner
method, to Chinese intraday data, and Li et al. (2019) learn stock-specific technical-indicator
settings using stock embeddings, a bridge between per-instrument parameter tuning and shared
representation learning.


10.2      Searching the rule space: evolutionary and swarm methods
If rules are hypotheses, evolutionary computation is a way to generate and test them at scale.
Hu et al. (2015) give the systematic review of evolutionary computation for rule discovery in
stock trading, screening 650 articles to 51; it is the right map of this area. Martín et al. (2019)
compare genetic programming and grammatical evolution configurations, both attractive because
their evolved rules remain interpretable and flexible in structure. Corazza et al. (2024) use
particle swarm optimisation to set indicator parameters, rule definitions and the weights used to
aggregate signals in one pass. Atiah and Helbig (2019) study how five decision models affect
dynamic multi-objective optimisation algorithms for foreign-exchange trading, which raises the
question of how to pick one rule from a Pareto front.
    A distinctive link comes from Kacprzyk et al. (2008), who ask whether genetic-programming
trading rules are sensitive to the entropy of the series, and first whether price series show temporal
dependencies measurable by entropy estimates. Entropy as a predictability gauge could serve as
a pre-screen for where a rule search is worth running at all.
Bridge. Rule search ↔ alpha mining ↔ multiple testing. What the evolutionary-
computation silo calls “fitness” and a “population of rules” is what the backtesting silo calls a
specification search, and the overfitting risk is identical (Chapter 14). Conversely, the factor
and LLM chapters (Chapters 9 and 15) describe “alpha mining” with formula generators; the
evolutionary papers here are the ancestors of that work, and their interpretable rule trees (Martín
et al. 2019) are a design pattern modern formula miners could reuse.
   A practical reading rule for the next sections: ask of every paper which stage it really improves.
Some improve the feature (denoising, state design, stock-specific indicator settings), some the


                                                 64
decision rule (thresholds, reward, policy class), and some only the evaluation. Papers that bundle
all three in one backtest are the hardest to reuse, because a desk cannot tell which component
carries the result.


10.3     Patterns, charts and levels
A large body of work tries to make chart reading computable. Neela (2025a) take the deterministic
route: extract structural monotonic price runs and attach public events in a window, producing
auditable explanations rather than forecasts. Y et al. (2021) apply machine learning to candlestick
patterns with several feature types and windows of one to ten days, and Agah et al. (2025)
combine deep-learning trend prediction with candlestick recognition. Arévalo et al. (2017) propose
a flag-pattern rule with dynamic stop-loss and take-profit windows and an EMA filter, and Pal
(2024) study LSTM recognition of the Wyckoff accumulation pattern in currency trading. In the
same family, Lee et al. (2022) combine an attention-based BiLSTM with technical indicators.
    Image-based methods treat the chart as a picture. Birogul et al. (2020) apply a YOLO
object detector to 2D candlestick charts to produce buy and sell decisions, and Jang et al. (2026)
turn 1-minute KOSPI200 futures data into candlestick and Bollinger-band images for model-free
visual pattern mining with similarity metrics. What this literature calls “pattern recognition”
is the time-series-mining silo’s “motif discovery” and “similarity search” (Chapter 5); the desk
question is always whether the pattern label adds information beyond the raw window, and
whether it survives a cost model. Kriuk et al. (2025) apply attention-based correlation analysis
to identify support and resistance levels dynamically, attacking a structural-level task rather
than price prediction. Jóźwicki and Trippner (2025) examine the volume profile as an alternative
to turnover-based indicators for investment decisions.
Desk note. Pattern labels as audited features (features → signal). A pattern detector
such as the run extraction of Neela (2025a) or a candlestick classifier as in Y et al. (2021) can be
run offline to emit a categorical feature (pattern type, age, run length) plus the raw window.
The output is a feature column consumed by a downstream model, never a direct order. Check
three things: whether the pattern is defined using only data available at bar close; whether the
detector’s label frequency drifts across years; and whether a null model on shuffled windows
yields comparable “hit rates”. Chart-image methods multiply the number of tunable choices, so
the capacity for leakage grows with them.


10.4     Liquidity-centred discretionary ideas
Practitioner concepts are entering the academic record. Gokarna Bhusal (2025) discuss liquidity-
based “stop hunt” traps in Forex and US indices using ICT and Elliott-wave concepts. Hammer
and Patel (2025) document a live intraday ES futures strategy built on Fair Value Gaps, liquidity
sweeps and breaker blocks. These are documentation of discretionary practice, and their evidence
should be graded accordingly. Mittal and Choudhary (2026) offer a more formal framing, recasting
breakouts as a question of liquidity structure and arguing that quiet-looking moves travel further
than visually impressive breakouts.
Bridge. “Liquidity sweep” ↔ order-book language. The ICT vocabulary of sweeps, gaps
and traps describes behaviour near resting orders. Microstructure would phrase this as clustered
limit and stop liquidity, depth thinning, and price impact (Chapter 12). A natural proposal is to
test the practitioner labels against order-book depth and trade data, so that a “sweep” becomes
a measurable event with a base rate, and the microstructure silo gains a catalogue of hypotheses
it has not yet formalised.
   At the other end of rigour, Amaral (2026) model the mid-price at second scale as a stationary
mean-reverting error around a latent efficient price and solve for the trading rule that maximises

                                                65
profit net of the bid-ask spread. Cheevirot et al. (2026) study Forex grid trading with martingale
lot scaling and propose strategy-embedded labelling for machine learning, with attention to the
drawdown that scaling creates. Grid and martingale schemes earn small gains with exposure to
rare large losses, so their risk limits matter more than their signal.


10.5     Reinforcement learning as strategy design
The largest group, with roughly twenty papers, casts trading as sequential decision making. Sun
et al. (2023b) give a survey of RL for quantitative trading, which is the best entry point. The
applications are mostly Q-network variants on single instruments. S and J (2024) use multi-level
deep Q-networks for Bitcoin, Huang et al. (2023) propose a dual-action, dual-environment DQN
for stocks, Saepudin and Rauf (2025) apply DRL to the Indonesia Stock Exchange, and Yu
et al. (2026a) augment DQN with an LSTM to handle partial observability. Indian-index studies
include Mishra et al. (2025b), who compare DQN, double DQN and dueling double DQN on
NIFTY 50, Borkar and Jadhav (2026), who compare Q-learning, DQN and DDQN intraday, and
Verma et al. (2027), who use volatility guidance for intraday index trading. Vishwakarma et al.
(2026) focus on state representation for day trading with technical indicators and chart patterns,
and Park et al. (2024) condition DRL “robots” on market conditions and US interest rates.
    Several papers add information to the state or change the learning target. Casares (2025)
apply wavelet denoising to DIX, GEX and VIX indicators before DRL on S&P 500 futures.
Novotny and Hajek (2026) combine support-vector-regression forecasts with PPO and sentiment
and volatility indices for precious-metal ETFs. Pillai et al. (2026) assemble a hybrid system of
EMA and MACD trend capture, machine learning and sentiment for regime-adaptive equity
strategies. Shen and Huang (2025) bring meta-RL and cognitive game theory to non-stationarity,
and Tan et al. (2024) learn options-trading positions end to end, directly from market data,
without an option pricing model, with transaction costs in the picture.
    The sharpest recurring problem is the reward. Orra et al. (2025) shape the reward with
expert feedback, Goluža et al. (2024) use an imitation reward for noisy environments, Orra et al.
(2026a) learn from several heterogeneous expert strategies by adversarial inverse RL, and Orra
et al. (2026b) discover auxiliary tasks automatically to stabilise training. These papers share a
diagnosis: with a low signal-to-noise ratio, the raw profit reward is a poor teacher. Transfer is
the other fix for scarce data. Poh et al. (2023) fuse a source-market encoder with a target-market
module to rank crypto assets for cross-sectional momentum, and Koshiyama et al. (2022) transfer
learning across trading strategies to build global rather than per-market models.
Desk note. RL as a research harness, not yet a production signal (signal → validation).
A cautious use is to run an RL agent on top of a frozen feature set, with the reward set to
net-of-cost, volatility-scaled return, and to compare it against the fixed rules of the previous
sections on the same walk-forward splits. The output is a comparison table and, if the agent
wins, a candidate position signal that enters the portfolio layer with a hard risk cap. Hazards:
many reported agents are tuned on one instrument and period, seed variance is rarely reported
in abstracts, simulators ignore impact and capacity, and a policy trained in one regime can fail
in the next (Chapter 7).


10.6     Do classical technical rules survive scrutiny?
The first half of this chapter met rule-based systems as engineering objects. A second family of
papers asks the older, blunter question: do such rules earn anything once data snooping and
costs are respected? The classical bootstrap evidence for simple moving-average and trading-
range-break rules on Dow Jones data from 1897 to 1986 (Brock et al., 1992) set the baseline,
and the later literature is largely a sequence of attempts to repeat that exercise with better


                                               66
multiple-testing control. Rink (2023) test 6406 rules across 23 developed and 18 emerging equity
indices with a superior-predictive-ability test. They report in-sample outperformance, much
of it in emerging markets, but also decaying predictability, sensitivity to moderate costs and
no persistence out of sample. Chuang et al. (2024) reach a similar verdict on two Chinese
indices with 38,456 rules and stepwise error-rate control: after the correction only a few complex
rules survive, and the lucrative ones are high-turnover rules whose out-of-sample returns turn
negative after costs. Yang et al. (2020b) add a bound on cumulative return and a bootstrap
across international markets, and conclude that rules are hard to beat against a random trading
benchmark.
    Not every author is so negative, and the dispersion is informative. Comparative indicator
studies on Indian equities (Pedrycz et al., 2024), on the volatility–return trade-off in NSE 500
portfolios (Bhama, 2024) and on multi-indicator combinations (Sukma and Namahoot, 2025)
report profitability for Bollinger bands, MACD, RSI and their blends. Such studies typically test a
handful of hand-picked parameterisations, which is exactly the setting where the multiple-testing
studies above warn that apparent edge is cheap to find. We treat these three papers as a catalogue
of rule definitions and a source of market-specific hypotheses, not as evidence of tradable alpha.
    Why then does technical analysis persist? Two papers offer behavioural answers. Ebert and
Hilpert (2019) model how the market timing of technical rules induces lottery-like, positively
skewed profits, which a skewness-loving investor would find attractive even without a premium.
Hoffmann and Shefrin (2014), using Dutch brokerage records matched to surveys, find that
individual investors who report using technical analysis hold more concentrated portfolios, turn
them over faster, trade more options and earn lower returns. For a desk, these results define the
counterparty side of the trade and warn that the popularity of a pattern is not evidence for its
profitability.
Desk note. Rule-zoo audit (validation stage). Input: a library of candidate rules (moving-
average crossovers, channel breakouts, RSI thresholds) with their parameter grids, plus daily
or intraday bars and a realistic cost model. Output: a pass/fail report per rule family, using a
data-snooping-aware test over the whole grid and not over the winner, followed by a walk-forward
check with costs. A natural use of Rink (2023) and Chuang et al. (2024) is to make the grid size
an explicit input to the test, so that adding more rules raises the bar. What goes wrong: the
audit is run after the grid was shaped by looking at the data (leakage by researcher), turnover is
underestimated, and a rule that worked in the early sample is promoted although the evidence
points to decay.


10.7     Analytical models of technical rules
A smaller group of papers replaces simulation by mathematics, which is where the maths and
control communities meet this chapter. Lorig et al. (2019) study trading with exponential moving
averages and obtain closed-form solutions to log-utility and growth-rate problems when the
drift is an Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process or a two-state Markov chain. Blanchet-Scalliet et al.
(2007) compare a moving-average style rule against model-based strategies that must detect a
change in drift at an unknown time, and find the technical rule beats an optimal allocation
under mis-specified parameters but not the Model and Detect strategies. The lesson for a desk is
conditional: a crude rule is a robust fallback when the model is wrong, not a substitute for a
model that is right.
    Support and resistance receive the same treatment. De Angelis and Peskir (2016) pose an
optimal-prediction problem for a stopping time under geometric Brownian motion with a hidden
aspiration level, and Henderson et al. (2026) analyse a three-state path-dependent price model
through optimal stopping, proving smoothness of the value function. Neither says that levels
exist in real data; both show under what dynamics such a rule would be optimal. The empirical


                                                67
counterpart is Chung and Bellotti (2021), whose heuristic discovery algorithm finds intraday
levels that reverse price statistically significantly, with more prior bounces predicting further
bounces and a decay of bounce probability over time.
    Control theory contributes a different language. Barmish and Primbs (2016) propose model-
free feedback controllers whose investment level is a function of the gain–loss process, aiming
at robustness certificates in place of backtests. Azhmyakov et al. (2023) apply a PID controller
to trading and tune it in a data-driven way, and Hsieh (2025) formulate robust gain selection
for a double linear policy so that expected profit is positive across a range of uncertain market
conditions. These are the nearest relatives of the stop-and-sizing logic in a risk layer, and we
return to them in the bridges below.


10.8      Levels, patterns and market structure as features
Many practitioner papers turn chart folklore into machine-readable events. Elliott-wave labels
feed a classifier for option entries in Bhattacharya et al. (2026); short-term fast–slow patterns in
Nasdaq-100 futures are identified in Montes-Gómez et al. (2024); León-Camacho et al. (2026)
correct Bollinger bands for skewness and kurtosis with an Edgeworth expansion and use them
as contrarian thresholds; and Lin et al. (2026a) augment MACD with volume, volatility and
intraday structure plus a sensitivity calibration. Salman et al. (2025) use a genetic algorithm to
combine several strategies in the directional-changes paradigm, an event-driven alternative to
fixed-interval bars, and Sharma et al. (2024) track open interest, spot price and delivery data in
Indian derivatives to infer the movement of large operators.
     The most recent cluster concerns Smart Money Concepts and Inner Circle Trader vocabulary.
Fumanal-Andres et al. (2026) automate detection of change-of-character, range boundaries and
liquidity voids, and Fumanal-Andres (2026) use generative AI to build an MQL4 expert advisor
that detects fair value gaps. Oukhouya et al. (2026) detect such events on OHLC data, learn
calibrated probabilities that they resolve favourably and size trades with linear programming
under a risk constraint, while Boutgajouft (2026) compare a formal liquidity theory against these
heuristic concepts. Levine (2026) report a paper-traded comparison of opening-range breakout
and ICT-style setups in E-mini futures; the sample is a single trader over a short period, so we
read it as a hypothesis generator.
     Andriushchenko et al. (2026) wrap polynomial moving regression bands in an economic-
security policy for proprietary cryptocurrency traders, a reminder that a band rule also needs
firm-level limits around it; we read the paper for that governance framing and for the idea of
curve-fitted bands as an adaptive channel, and not for any performance claim. Such bands are, in
effect, a local polynomial smoother with a residual envelope, so the usual caveats about end-point
revision apply: the fit at the latest bar is the least reliable one and is exactly the one a live rule
uses.
     Two papers are good models of discipline. Brown (2026) pre-register four predictions of Gann
angle theory, test them on 2016–2022 in sample and 2023–2025 held out, and apply false-discovery-
rate and block-bootstrap checks; they report significant first-revisit and near-touch effects but
negative net returns for fan crossings. Mesfin (2026a) build a day classifier from overnight gap,
first-30-minute return and relative first-bar volume with expanding-window thresholds; it finds
a recurring intraday profile, yet none of the derived strategies pass walk-forward, after-cost
validation. A negative result reported this carefully is worth more to a desk than a positive one
reported loosely.




                                                 68
Desk note. Structure events as features, not as trades (feature and filter stage).
Input: intraday OHLCV and open-interest bars. Output: a table of event flags (level touch, gap
fill, range break, day-type label) with causal timestamps, consumed by a downstream classifier
or used as a regime filter. The proposal, building on Mesfin (2026a), Oukhouya et al. (2026)
and Chung and Bellotti (2021), is to treat each folk concept as a candidate feature with a
pre-registered hypothesis and an expanding-window threshold. Failure modes: swing points and
levels that are only confirmed after the fact (lookahead), thresholds fitted on the full sample,
event definitions drifting between the research and live code paths, and thin capacity on micro
contracts once spread and slippage are charged.


10.9     Fusing rules, filtering signals and meta-labels
If single rules are weak, an obvious response is to combine or filter them. Evidential-reasoning
methods are the older tradition: Dymova et al. (2016) build a Forex expert system on rule-base
evidential reasoning, and Lepskiy and Suevalov (2019) compute belief and plausibility for buy,
sell and hold recommendations via fuzzy inference on Russian equities. Their contribution is a
formal way to represent disagreement between indicators instead of averaging it away.
     The modern descendants are secondary models that decide whether to act on a primary signal.
Hu and Zhou (2024) treat the signal as the start of an observation window and a profit target
as the event of interest, so that deep survival models filter false signals. Zhang et al. (2026a)
evaluate meta-labeling of moving-average crossovers with XGBoost on 15-minute Chinese futures
under purged walk-forward and triple-barrier labels, and report ROC-AUC near 0.5, mixed
contract-dependent outcomes and no significant paired confidence interval. Mikhailov (2026)
similarly apply a meta-labeling approach to daily Bitcoin. Read together, these papers say that
a filter helps only if the secondary features carry information the primary rule did not. Vancsura
et al. (2026) make the related point for forecasts: RNN, LSTM and GRU predictions feed a
rule-based trader with volatility-adjusted stops, a rolling-error confidence filter and error-based
sizing, tested across twelve instruments and three regimes. Forecast accuracy is an input to, not
a proxy for, strategy performance.


10.10      Reinforcement-learning traders
A different silo reaches the same decision problem from the other end: skip the explicit rule
and learn the policy. Kabbani and Duman (2022) argue that deep reinforcement learning can
merge the prediction and allocation steps, and Z et al. (2021) extend the action space to variable
position sizes with an LSTM-DDPG agent. Tsui et al. (2026) train an agent to sell or buy at
perceived peaks and valleys, and Kar et al. (2025) bypass reward design altogether by training
with profit-guided loss functions. Go et al. (2026) borrow model predictive control to add
inference-time planning with price forecasts to a static policy.
    At shorter horizons, Cartea et al. (2021) derive statistical-arbitrage policies in an FX triplet
with double deep Q-networks and a new reinforced deep Markov model. For cryptocurrency,
Qin et al. (2024) use hierarchical RL for second-level trading, Zong et al. (2024) add memory
and context awareness to the same setting, and Sun et al. (2022b) build risk-awareness into
an intraday framework. The shared premise is that the agent should respect risk rather than
only maximise return. Control-engineering papers above and RL here are cousins: both choose
an exposure as a function of a state, one with guarantees and few parameters, the other with
flexibility and weak guarantees.




                                                69
Desk note. RL agent as a candidate, never a deployer (signal and validation stages).
Input: bars or limit-order-book features, a cost and latency model, and a held-out regime set.
Output: a policy that is promoted only if it beats a transparent baseline such as a volatility-scaled
trend rule after costs. Our proposal is to run the agents of Sun et al. (2022b) or Zong et al.
(2024) as research challengers inside a shared simulator, and to demand regime-by-regime results
as in Vancsura et al. (2026). Risks: simulator fills that are more generous than real queues,
non-stationary reward, seed sensitivity, and policies that quietly rely on a lookahead feature.


10.11      Humans, platforms and automation
The last group concerns who builds and runs strategies. Chivu et al. (2024) compare human and
algorithmic trading with a Pine Script bot using learning vector quantisation; Guarino et al. (2022)
evaluate autonomous, adaptive automated traders during cryptocurrency bubbles, asking whether
learning helps at all. Bohrinovtseva et al. (2026) propose a human–AI co-creative framework in
which the weight of AI signals and human input varies with volatility, task complexity and trust,
which is a good description of the semi-automated desk this atlas targets. AlDhaen et al. (2026)
describe a cost-effective smart algorithmic trading system for small professionals, and Sun et al.
(2023a) offer TradeMaster, a platform for reproducible RL-based trading research. Together
with the generative-AI-built expert advisor of Fumanal-Andres (2026), they point at research
automation: the same pipeline that implements a strategy can implement its test harness.


10.12      Bridges out of this chapter

Bridge. Event-based time and “discords”. The directional-changes paradigm in Salman
et al. (2025) samples price when it has moved by a threshold, not when a clock ticks. Time-series
mining would call that a form of adaptive segmentation, and a recurring Elliott-wave or flag
shape a motif; the day-type outliers of Mesfin (2026a) resemble discords. Microstructure readers
will recognise “liquidity voids” and “stop-hunt zones” as regions of thin resting depth. A shared
vocabulary lets the pattern-similarity tools of Chapter 5 be tested on these labelled events, and
lets order-book data from Chapter 12 supply the depth evidence that chart traders only infer
from OHLC.

Bridge. Data snooping is a backtesting problem. The multiple-testing designs of Rink
(2023) and Chuang et al. (2024), and the pre-registration of Brown (2026), are the strategy-side
face of what Chapter 14 calls selection bias and multiple-hypothesis control. Translated: “the
number of rules tried” is the same quantity as the number of trials in a deflated-Sharpe style
correction, and a “rule zoo” is a hypothesis family. The same chapter’s purged walk-forward
machinery is what Zhang et al. (2026a) apply to meta-labels.

Bridge. Feedback controllers are position-sizing rules. What control engineers call gain
selection and robustness certificates (Barmish and Primbs, 2016; Hsieh, 2025) is, in portfolio
language, a sizing function of running profit and loss with a worst-case guarantee; Chapter 13
treats the stochastic-control and Kelly-style counterparts. Conversely, optimal stopping results
on levels (De Angelis and Peskir, 2016; Henderson et al., 2026) give the stopping-time view of
stop-loss and take-profit, which the regime machinery of Chapter 7 could condition on drift
states, the same two-state structure used by Lorig et al. (2019).




                                                 70
Chapter 11

Volatility and Derivatives

Four communities share this chapter and rarely read each other. The first is the derivatives-
pricing school (quant-deriv), whose native words are smile, skew, risk-neutral density, Greeks, risk
premium and rough volatility. The second is financial econometrics (fin-econ and stats), which
speaks of GARCH, realized variance, bipower variation, jumps, spillovers and superior predictive
ability. The third is a small market-microstructure group that treats volatility as a by-product of
discrete, noisy, event-driven trading. The fourth is the machine-learning group, who see volatility
as a regression target and ask about architectures, underspecification and deployment.
    The atlas vocabulary lets us translate between them. What derivatives people call an implied-
volatility surface or a risk-neutral density is, in the shared terms, a market-implied forecast
of a distribution, and it is also a set of features for a signal. What econometricians call a
volatility forecast evaluated by a loss function is a forecast judged by forecast evaluation, and the
superior-predictive-ability test is a multiple-testing control. A variance risk premium is the gap
between a market-implied and a realized quantity, so it is a factor with a sign and a price. A
spillover is an edge in a network. An expiry-day effect is a calendar anomaly in liquidity and
realized volatility.
    In the fund pipeline this chapter sits in the middle. Volatility and option data enter as raw
inputs (data), become features (implied volatility, skew, realized measures, jump indicators), feed
forecasts of risk and of returns, and then drive regime flags, position sizing, hedging and risk
limits. Its papers mostly serve the forecast and risk stages, but several matter for validation, and
a minority are direct signals. About a third of the sources (20 of 64 by a keyword scan of titles
and abstracts) study India (Nifty, Bank Nifty, single-stock options), which makes the chapter a
practical resource for a desk trading NSE derivatives.


11.1      Implied volatility as information and as fear gauge
The starting point is that option prices carry information that prices of the underlying do not.
Christoffersen et al. (2013) survey how implied volatilities, skewness, kurtosis and densities can
be extracted for forecasting, and note that any forecast target that is a smooth function of the
future price can use option-implied information in a principled way. They also stress adjusting
for risk premia first. The rest of this section asks who trades on that information and what it
says in an emerging market.
    On the Nifty, Pathak (2015) study whether option volume and open-interest changes respond
to expected volatility, with a common implied-volatility measure and a vector autoregression,
and report a feedback relationship between the two. Chakrabarti and Kumar (2020) use model-
free implied volatility on five-minute data to test leverage, volatility-feedback and behavioural
explanations of the short-run return–volatility link. Shaikh (2018) find an asymmetric, negative
relation between the India VIX and returns, and read the index as a gauge of fear and portfolio-
insurance cost rather than optimism. Blau and Brough (2015) look from the equity side and find


                                                 71
that put-call ratios are not a proxy for short-sale constraints, and that their return predictability
is driven by ratios following positive-return periods. Gupta (2018) test put-call parity on Nifty
options against moneyness, maturity and volume, treating violations as a measure of illiquidity
as much as of arbitrage.
    Structural studies of the Indian surface follow. Shaikh and Padhi (2014) document smile,
skew and term-structure patterns of Nifty implied volatility. Jain et al. (2019) find that a smile-
adjusted Black model fits well, that implied volatility adds predictive power for future volatility,
and that the premium in single-stock options is higher than in other markets. Agarwalla et al.
(2021) use implied measures from single-stock options to argue that the COVID-19 repricing was
gradual and rational. Jayapalan (2013) propose a simple Nifty call pricing model built on the
correlation with the market index, and Singhal and Bhattacharyya (2024) backtest short-theta
strategies on Indian index options, using delta moves as a parameter and noting that gamma
and delta can overpower theta.
    A second thread asks how far implied volatility can be predicted from the past. Wen et al.
(2024) show a clear pattern of past dependence up to twenty days, in both linear and nonlinear
models. Guyon and Lekeufack (2023) find that a simple, power-law-weighted function of past
returns explains most of the variance of equity-index implied volatility and a large share of
realized volatility. Together they say that a good part of the implied-volatility level is endogenous
to price history, so a feature built from option data may repackage what returns already contain.
    Taken together, these studies show a recurring pattern. Each starts from a clean theoretical
object, such as parity, a smile or a risk-neutral density, and then measures how far an emerging,
retail-heavy market departs from it. The departures are where a desk might look for edge, but
also where costs, thin strikes and settlement rules are most likely to erase it. A reader from
a machine-learning background should note that none of the Indian studies above is a trading
backtest in the modern sense. They are mostly descriptive tests of efficiency, so any signal derived
from them needs its own walk-forward evaluation.
Desk note. Implied-volatility feature block (features, then signal and risk filter).
Inputs are daily or intraday Nifty and single-stock option chains, the India VIX, and put-call
ratios. A natural pipeline computes model-free implied volatility, a smile-adjusted level and
slope, and the put-call ratio, and regresses each on a path-dependent baseline of past returns
in the spirit of Guyon and Lekeufack (2023) and Wen et al. (2024), keeping only the residual
as a candidate signal. The output is a volatility-regime filter and a position-size scaler. Failure
modes: stale or illiquid strikes that make parity and smile fits noisy (Gupta, 2018), confusing a
risk-premium effect with a forecast effect (Christoffersen et al., 2013), and trading costs on thin
single-stock options, which the cited studies do not net out.


11.2      Risk premia, surfaces and pricing models
If implied volatility is information, it is also a price, and prices include compensation. Duarte
and Jones (2008) estimate a volatility risk premium from a cross-section of equity-option returns
and find it rises with overall market volatility. Andersen et al. (2015) show from index option
surfaces that priced left-tail risk is not spanned by volatility and introduce a tail factor that
predicts equity and variance risk premia rather than future volatility. Christoffersen et al. (2009)
explain the shape and term structure of the index smirk with multifactor stochastic-volatility
models. For a desk, these are the factor-model view of option returns: selling volatility earns a
premium that is time-varying and tied to crises.
    Whether such premia exist locally is a testable question. Pillai (2026) apply a finite-sample
simulation test, originally for US index puts, to Nifty puts from 2015 to 2025 under Black–Scholes,
Heston and a jump model, and the abstract itself warns that the jump-model estimation did not
fully converge. That is a model for how a validation check on an option-selling idea should look:
a null model, many simulated paths, and a confidence interval, not a single backtest.

                                                 72
    The modelling literature supplies the engine room. Ahlip et al. (2018) give a semi-analytical
call price under a double-Heston jump-diffusion with stochastic rates. Lu (2026) compare
option models from Black–Scholes to fintech-enhanced approaches, and Black (1975) is an early,
plain-language account of what options do and do not offer. Chen and Kay (2026) review the
history of probability measures in asset pricing, from state prices to stochastic discount factors,
and Abedi (2026) derive jump-diffusion option pricing from information constraints. These are
reference works: they define the measures under which implied quantities are computed, and the
distinction between risk-neutral and real-world measures underlies every premium above.
    Newer papers bring machine learning to the surface. Shikhman et al. (2026) show that
accurate option prices do not imply an accurately recovered risk-neutral density, report that
many pricing directions are numerically null, and test on a chronological Nifty benchmark. Han
et al. (2026) generate dynamic implied-volatility surfaces with diffusion models and judge them
through data-driven hedging. Green (2026) describes deep hedging, in which a network chooses
hedging positions directly, and Bloch (2026b) propose a path-signature swap for path-dependent
risk. Daniluk et al. (2024) reduce Monte Carlo error in pricing and Greeks in local-stochastic-
volatility models. Abi Jaber and Li (2025) report that rough-volatility models are inconsistent
with the global shape of SPX smiles and underperform simple Markovian alternatives of the
same size. Finally, Sahu (2026) show that aggregate dealer gamma exposure, quoted by vendors,
is only bounded by exchange open-interest disclosure, and that its sign is undetermined for the
participants examined.
Desk note. Option-selling risk control (risk stage and validation). Suppose a desk runs
short-volatility structures on Nifty weeklies. Inputs are the surface, open interest, and a premium
estimate in the manner of Duarte and Jones (2008) and Andersen et al. (2015). The output is
a risk limit on short-gamma exposure plus a validation check that compares the realized P&L
distribution with a simulated null as in Pillai (2026). Gamma-flip levels from vendors should not
be used as a trigger without the caveat of Sahu (2026). What can go wrong: premia that are
positive on average but crash-concentrated, margin and cost drag, capacity limits in far strikes,
and a generated surface (Han et al., 2026) that has never seen a true stress regime.


11.3     Forecasting realized volatility
On the econometric side the benchmark is Bollerslev (1986), the GARCH model, and the question
of whether anything beats it. Reinhard Hansen and Lunde (2003) compare 330 GARCH-type
models with a superior-predictive-ability test and ask exactly that; on IBM realized volatility,
GARCH(1,1) is beaten, mainly by models with a leverage effect. Measurement improved with
high-frequency data. Aït-Sahalia et al. (2005) show that with microstructure noise there is a
finite optimal sampling frequency, and Hoffmann et al. (2010) estimate the volatility function
nonparametrically under such noise with wavelets. Vipul and Jacob (2007) compare extreme-value
volatility estimators for the Nifty against a two-scale realized-volatility benchmark. Andersen
et al. (2007) add jump components using bipower variation, and Qu et al. (2024) revisit the
puzzle of null jump effects by using jump intensity inside a heterogeneous autoregression.
    Latent-state methods give another view. Johannes et al. (2009) derive optimal filtering for
jump-diffusions to extract latent volatility and jump states, Virbickaitė and Lopes (2019) add
Markov-switching regimes and a flexible error distribution to stochastic volatility, and Woitschig
and West (2026) combine a dynamic gamma process for realized volatility with Bayesian dynamic
linear models for prices. Dai et al. (2025a) build a GARCH-type model for fuzzy-set-valued
returns. Johansson et al. (2023) propose a simple method for predicting covariance matrices,
which is the multivariate counterpart.
    The recent cross-currents link ML and rough volatility. Rosenbaum and Zhang (2024) train a
pooled LSTM on hundreds of stocks and find that rough-volatility parametric forecasters match


                                                73
it, which they read as universality in the volatility formation process. Mouti (2026) measure
roughness across equities, futures, rates, FX and commodities and report realized volatility rough
everywhere, with implied estimates informative only where the skew term structure is clean.
Wang et al. (2026f) give a Hawkes-process microstructural foundation for a rough Hawkes–Heston
model, which is the nearest this chapter comes to order-flow modelling. Cortesi et al. (2026) warn
that networks with identical test loss on volatility learn different functions and produce portfolios
with sharply different turnover. Ye and Wanjiku (2026) study post-training quantization of
forecasters, whose calibration data can matter for deployment. S et al. (2024) apply seven
machine-learning methods to VIX constant-maturity futures from term-structure information,
with a walk-forward design and a constrained mean-variance portfolio.
Desk note. Volatility forecast as a shared service (forecast stage, feeding sizing
and risk). A fund can maintain one volatility forecast per instrument: a HAR or GARCH
baseline from noise-robust realized measures (Aït-Sahalia et al., 2005; Andersen et al., 2007),
challenged by a pooled neural model (Rosenbaum and Zhang, 2024). The output is a variance
forecast for position sizing, stop distances and covariance inputs. Gate every challenger with
a superior-predictive-ability test (Reinhard Hansen and Lunde, 2003), and compare models on
decision metrics such as turnover, not loss alone (Cortesi et al., 2026). Risks: leakage through a
pooled training window, realized-volatility estimates that change with sampling frequency, and
VIX-futures roll costs, which the strategy study may not fully capture.


11.4      Expiry, calendar and session effects
Derivatives expiry is a scheduled event, so it is a calendar feature. Illueca and LaFuente (2006)
use realized volatility to study expiry effects in Spanish index futures. Xu (2014) exploit a change
in expiration day in Sweden. In India, Agarwalla and Pandey (2013) study cash settlement on
the final half-hour volume-weighted average price and how short trading breaks affect intraday
volatility, Mahalwala (2016) study volume, return, volatility and reversal around expiry, and
Suryanarayana et al. (2024) fit an EGARCH on Nifty futures volume and price. Chhimwal
et al. (2025) study the shift of Bank Nifty expiry day and its effect on volatility, volume and
connectedness. Ahluwalia et al. (2023) do the same for another scheduled event, index rebalancing,
with asymmetric GARCH models of volatility persistence. Day-of-week patterns in volatility and
volume appear in Kiymaz and Berument (2003). Zielkowski (2026) show that first-passage times
scale with volatility with an exponent far from the diffusion value, largely because of first-bar
overnight gaps, which matters for holding-period and stop design.
    These papers share an idea: treat the exchange calendar as an exogenous regressor. A
desk can encode time-to-expiry, expiry-day dummies, rebalance dates and session-open flags as
features, so that signals do not mistake a settlement mechanism for information.
    The three themes above also interact. Expiry days change realized volatility, which changes
the volatility forecast that feeds the risk limit, which in turn changes position size on the day
when liquidity is least reliable. A pipeline that treats the calendar, the forecast and the limit as
separate modules can therefore be wrong in a correlated way. For that reason the calendar flags
should enter the forecast model as regressors, rather than being patched on afterwards by a risk
overlay.


11.5      Spillovers and causality in variance
Volatility moves across assets and markets. Diebold and Yilmaz (2012) define total and directional
spillovers from a generalized vector autoregression with order-invariant variance decompositions.
Cerqueti et al. (2023) cluster stocks by directional spillovers with a weighted fuzzy method on the



                                                 74
Italian market, Chai et al. (2020) model G20 comovements with an ICA-based ARMA-APARCH-
M approach, and Blazsek et al. (2026) use score-driven models for return and volatility spillovers
between India, the US and USD/INR. Cheung and Ng (1996) supply the underlying statistical
test for causality in variance from residual cross-correlations. The shared lesson is that a spillover
table is a directed graph, and that its edges rest on forecast-error decompositions that should be
tested.


11.6      Drawdown and tail risk measures
Three papers concern risk summaries. Harding et al. (2003) discuss the strengths and weaknesses
of drawdown as a risk statistic in managed futures, Mendes and Lavrado (2017) implement and
test Maximum Drawdown at Risk, and Banerjee and Paul (2024) test a multiplicative-component
GARCH with extreme value theory for intraday VaR and expected shortfall in emerging and
developed markets. They connect the volatility forecasts above to the limits a risk committee
actually sets.
Desk note. Event-calendar and drawdown guardrails (risk stage). Combine the expiry
and rebalance flags above with a volatility forecast and a drawdown-at-risk limit (Mendes and
Lavrado, 2017; Banerjee and Paul, 2024), so that leverage falls into scheduled events. Test the
limits with a rolling out-of-sample coverage check, not only an in-sample fit. Cost and regime
dependence matter: rule changes such as moved expiry days (Chhimwal et al., 2025) invalidate
old calendar estimates.


11.7      Bridges out of this chapter

Bridge. To time-series methods (Chapter 5) and representation learning (Chapter 6).
Econometricians say that volatility is long-memory, rough, or path-dependent (Guyon and
Lekeufack, 2023; Abi Jaber and Li, 2025). A time-series-learning group would say that the series
has a kernel-weighted look-back with power-law weights, which is a learned or fixed convolution.
The power-law-weighted sums are interpretable features that a representation-learning pipeline
can use as a prior, and the pooled LSTM result (Rosenbaum and Zhang, 2024) suggests that the
cross-asset pooling familiar from foundation models applies here.

Bridge. To regimes and systemic risk (Chapter 7). What this chapter calls a Markov-
switching stochastic volatility state (Virbickaitė and Lopes, 2019) or a latent jump state (Johannes
et al., 2009) is what change-point and regime papers call a regime or segment. A directional
spillover matrix (Diebold and Yilmaz, 2012; Cerqueti et al., 2023) is a network whose weights
can be tracked for structural breaks, and the dealer-gamma bounds (Sahu, 2026) are a reminder
that any regime label built on an unobserved quantity needs an uncertainty interval.

Bridge. To microstructure and execution (Chapter 12) and market structure
(Chapter 4). Microstructure speaks of order-flow imbalance, liquidity and settlement mechanics;
the derivatives literature speaks of expiry effects and hedging pressure (Agarwalla and Pandey,
2013; Zielkowski, 2026). Hawkes-based foundations for roughness (Wang et al., 2026f) connect
self-exciting order flow with the Hurst exponent measured in Mouti (2026). Participants’ books
and their disclosure (Sahu, 2026) are where the market-structure chapter and this one meet.




                                                 75
Bridge. To backtesting (Chapter 14) and synthetic data (Chapter 3). A simulation-
based null, as in Pillai (2026), and a superior-predictive-ability test (Reinhard Hansen and Lunde,
2003) are what the backtesting chapter calls multiple-testing control. Generated surfaces (Han
et al., 2026) are what the synthetic-data chapter calls a market generator, and Shikhman et al.
(2026) supply the warning that a generator should be tested on quantities not pinned down by
the prices it was fitted to.




                                                76
    Part V

Implementation




      77
Chapter 12

Market Microstructure, Order Books
and Execution

Four research communities meet in this chapter and rarely read one another. Market-microstructure
economists, who supply most of the papers here, study how the rules of a venue turn orders
into prices: who demands liquidity, who supplies it, and what each trade reveals. Financial
econometricians and statisticians contribute estimators for noisy high-frequency data, lead–lag
tests and price-discovery decompositions. Deep-learning groups treat the limit order book as
an image or a sequence and try to forecast the next mid-price move. A decision-and-control
group, which mixes stochastic control, game theory and reinforcement learning, asks how to slice
a parent order. Smaller contributions come from physics, rough-path mathematics, time-series
mining and behavioural finance.
    The vocabularies translate directly into the atlas’s shared terms. “Order-flow” is the signed
stream of trades and order-book events, and “trade classification” is the labelling step that signs
it. A “limit-order-book” state is a set of price levels with queued volume. “Liquidity” covers
spreads, depth and resilience, and “market-impact” is the price response to one’s own or others’
flow. “Execution” is the schedule or policy that works a parent order, and “transaction-costs”
are what remain after fees, spread and impact. Microstructure people call a persistent signed
imbalance “informed flow”. Machine-learning people call the same object a feature, a label or a
latent state.
    In the fund pipeline (data, features, signal, regime and risk, portfolio, execution, validation)
this chapter sits at both ends. At the front it governs data and feature construction: signing
trades, measuring spreads and encoding the book. At the back it governs execution and cost,
which decide whether a signal from any other chapter survives contact with the market. Whatever
the holding period, the cost estimates here set the break-even for the strategies of Chapter 10,
and the return forecasts of Chapter 8 are only as good as the execution assumptions behind
them.
    The evidence has a regional flavour worth noting. A substantial share of the papers use
Indian exchange data, which suits a desk trading NSE instruments, but many findings come
from US or Australian equities and from cryptocurrency venues, and transfer to a new market
should be treated as a hypothesis.


12.1     Signing the tape and measuring liquidity
Almost every order-flow feature starts with a label that the raw data usually lack: was a trade
buyer- or seller-initiated? Lee and Ready (1991) document two problems with quote-based
classification. Quotes may be recorded ahead of the trades that triggered them, and trades inside
the spread are hard to classify. They propose simple procedures to improve the labels. Aitken
and Frino (1996) test the claim that the tick rule is accurate on the Australian exchange, a

                                                78
robustness check outside the US. Chakrabarty et al. (2012) compare bulk volume classification
with the tick rule and the Lee–Ready algorithm and find the conventional rules more accurate, a
caution for anyone computing VPIN-style indicators. Jurkatis (2020) argues that established
algorithms fail as quote changes accelerate and proposes an algorithm that searches for the
matching quote instead of assuming a fixed assignment. Bessembinder (2003) shows that the
time adjustment between trades and quotes and the signing procedure both affect estimated
execution costs. For a desk, the message is that the sign of a trade is an estimate with its own
error, and that error is a source of leakage-like bias when timestamps are misaligned.
    Once trades are signed, the next question is how liquid the instrument is. Several reviews
organise the answer. Le and Gregoriou (2020) sort low-frequency proxies into transaction-cost,
volume, price-impact and multidimensional families. Cobandag Guloglu and Ekinci (2022)
concentrate on high-frequency measures and conclude that these cluster around the spread
and the order book, the latter being richer. Shaik and Bairaria (2026) survey bid–ask spread
estimators and describe a shift from covariance-based metrics towards data-driven ones. These
three give a menu from which a data team can choose by data availability and computational
cost.
    The empirical papers on India show what such menus look like in practice. Thomas (2003)
characterise intraday NSE data and find behaviour similar to that of more mature markets.
Krishnan and Mishra (2013) find U-shaped intraday volume and spread patterns on the NSE,
together with the unusual feature of high volume and wide spreads at the same time, and they
check for commonality across stocks. Pan and Misra (2021) test whether the classic low-frequency
determinants of the spread still hold in high-frequency data. University of Hyderabad et al. (2016)
relate spreads, volume and volatility at five-minute resolution, within the mixture-of-distributions
and sequential-information-arrival frameworks. Taken together, they suggest that intraday
seasonality must be removed or conditioned on before a spread or volume feature is used.
    Two further papers refine the measurement itself. Bibinger et al. (2026) generalise a one-
sided noise model for limit-order-book quotes so that the tail of the noise is asset-specific,
and estimate the noise tail parameter. This matters for realised-volatility estimation at tick
frequency, which Chapter 11 treats. Ibikunle et al. (2026) train models on a dataset with observed
high-frequency-trading activity and apply them to public data to separate liquidity-supplying
from liquidity-demanding strategies. The reported result is that their measures outperform
conventional proxies. A natural use is as a participant-mix feature when public data are all a
desk has.
Desk note. Trade-signing and spread feature stage (data and features). Inputs are
timestamped trades and quotes. The output is a signed-flow series with a per-day misclassification
check. A possible procedure: sign trades with a quote-matching rule, compare it with the tick
rule on a subsample, and log the disagreement rate as a data-quality metric. Intraday-seasonally
adjusted spread and depth then become inputs to the cost model and to the execution scheduler.
What can go wrong: exchange timestamps and quote feeds that are not synchronised can leak
future information into the sign, spreads estimated from daily data will not match what the desk
actually pays, and a classifier validated on one market may fail on another.


12.2     Order-book features and short-horizon forecasts
The most visible strand of the literature turns the book into a predictor. The older and simpler
idea is order flow imbalance. Su et al. (2021) generalise it to allow for changes that are not
a minimum quotation unit and for a stationarised log version, tested on CSI 500 constituents.
Gupta and Kumar (2025) ask whether depth beyond the best prices carries information on the
NSE, and report that deeper levels contribute to short-term return predictability after controlling
for autocorrelation, volume and trade imbalance. Mishra (2026) combine a Hawkes model of
trade arrivals with imbalance and Kyle’s lambda across the NIFTY50 universe and backtest

                                                79
a strategy. Its headline Sharpe ratio is an in-paper backtest figure and should be discounted
accordingly until costs, latency and selection are examined (see Chapter 14). Guda et al. (2025)
extend the bivariate Hawkes process, which captures self- and cross-excitation of order arrivals,
with a liquidity imbalance factor and a rolling volatility term.
    A second family is statistical learning on the raw book. Sidogi et al. (2023) compute
rough-path signature features from book data and compare them with autoregressions and
with neural-network and random-forest learners; this links to the path-signature material of
Chapter 6. The deep-learning group is represented by Sirignano and Cont (2018), who report a
stationary, universal relation between the book and subsequent direction across many stocks,
and by Lucchese et al. (2022), who introduce a volume representation of the book and study how
far ahead predictability lasts. Y et al. (2025) apply a patch-based transformer to the book. A
et al. (2025) release an open-source pipeline and show that forecasting power depends on the
microstructural characteristics of each stock and does not automatically translate into trading
profit. Naviglio and Lillo (2026) use a neural network on returns and signed volume as a tool
for discovering structure, and contrast it with a linear VAR benchmark. Their aim is to recover
effective parametric models, not only to forecast.
    These papers make one point that desks should absorb. Predictability is real at very short
horizons, but the horizon is often shorter than the time needed to cross the spread. The gap
between forecasting accuracy and tradable profit is therefore the main lesson of A et al. (2025),
and it is why the same data need the execution models discussed below. A complementary use
of the book is anomaly detection: Kong et al. (2021) study micro-trading behaviour before price
jumps with multivariate time-series methods and select the attributes that are most informative.
H et al. (2023) apply thermodynamic ideas to the book of a Bitcoin spot market and derive
temperature and entropy measures that correlate with spread-based liquidity measures. Both
could serve as regime features, but neither is validated as a trading signal in the sources.
Bridge. What microstructure calls an “order-flow imbalance regime” is what time-series mining
calls a “discord” or an abnormal subsequence, and what the pre-jump study of Kong et al. (2021)
measures with attribute-wise abnormality scores. Conversely, what the deep-learning papers call
a “spatial–temporal patch” of the book is, to a microstructure economist, simply a depth profile
over time. A desk that borrows the mining toolkit (Chapter 5) gets similarity search over book
states, and one that borrows the economist’s view gets a reason to expect a pattern to persist.


12.3      Price discovery, lead–lag and information
A different question is where information enters first. In India the classic test is between index
futures and the cash index. Sinha and Sharma (2006) use cointegration and regression and
find that Nifty futures lead the cash index, and Debasish and Mishra (2008) examine the same
relation from an efficiency and arbitrage angle. More recent work quantifies the role of derivatives
directly: Aggarwal and Thomas (2019) report that single-stock futures hold a 55% information
share during news arrivals, higher for negative news, which they connect to short-sale restrictions
and a leverage–liquidity trade-off. Outside India, Frijns et al. (2015) study cross-listed shares
and find that lower relative spreads and higher relative trading activity raise an exchange’s share
of price discovery. Chordia et al. (2005) model liquidity, returns and volatility between small
and large firms in a vector autoregression and find liquidity spillovers across the two groups.
    Informed trading can also hide in options and in who is trading. Anand and Chakravarty
(2007) show that informed traders split orders into small or medium trades depending on contract
volume, with at-the-money calls carrying the most information. Muravyev (2016) decomposes
option price impact into inventory-risk and asymmetric-information parts and finds that the
inventory component is larger, which matters for option-based order-flow signals (Chapter 11).
Jang et al. (2023) find that foreign institutions earn large profits in index derivatives while


                                                80
domestic individuals lose, and argue that the evidence is consistent with superior intraday
market-wide information. Garg and Mitra (2015) look at the lead–lag relation between foreign
institutional herding and Indian returns using daily data. Chaboud et al. (2014) show in foreign
exchange that algorithmic trading improves two measures of price efficiency, triangular arbitrage
opportunities and high-frequency autocorrelation, mainly when computers take liquidity.
    For a desk, lead–lag relations are the textbook “signal” in this chapter, and also the most
