perishable. The estimates describe averages over multi-year samples; the half-life of an edge
is shorter, and the lag at which it is observable must be longer than the desk’s latency or it
cannot be traded. Where a lead–lag pair has a stable cointegrating relation, Chapter 9 treats
the relative-value use.


12.4     Who is trading: high-frequency and algorithmic partici-
         pants
A long series of papers asks what different kinds of participants do to the book. Brogaard et al.
(2015) find that price discovery happens predominantly through limit orders, most of which come
from high-frequency traders, and that this falls in volatile periods. Hirschey (2021) reports that
aggressive high-frequency trades lead those of other investors, and that a subset of high-frequency
traders predicts the order flow of others the best. The model of Aït-Sahalia and Saglam (2013)
has a strategic fast trader with an imperfect signal about future flow and predicts reduced
liquidity provision when volatility rises. Easley et al. (2019) argue that the defining feature of
high-frequency trading is decision-making on a volume clock, not speed, and offer advice for
slower traders; time sampling by volume instead of by clock is also a feature-engineering idea in
its own right.
     On the NSE, where algorithmic participation is flagged, a set of papers distinguishes pro-
prietary from buy-side algorithms. Arumugam and Prasanna (2021) find that the two groups
affect quoted spreads and price impact differently, and Arumugam and Krishna Prasanna (2021)
report within-group commonality and between-group contrarian behaviour. Arumugam et al.
(2023) use jump-robust volatility estimates and find that heavy directional and non-directional
trading by buy-side and high-frequency algorithms increases volatility. Syamala and Wadhwa
(2020) show that algorithmic traders earn intraday market-timing profits, particularly when
providing liquidity. The identity of the trader can matter even on a decentralised exchange:
Zhai (2026) reconstruct a full-depth book where counterparties are visible by wallet address and
report that informativeness is a persistent trader attribute. Anshuman et al. (2025) find that
intraday proprietary traders are contrarian and correct temporary mispricing through informed
trading rather than liquidity provision. Kim and Hansen (2026) document periodic bursts at one-,
five- and fifteen-minute marks in cryptocurrency futures, tie them to algorithmic participation
through trade-size roundness, and report out-of-sample predictability of opening returns.
     Taken as a group, these studies suggest participant labels as features: the share of volume
from aggressive fast traders, or the periodic clock phase, can condition other signals. They also
warn about crowding. If many algorithms commonly trade the same direction, the resulting flow
is itself a risk factor.
Desk note. Order-flow and participant signal (signal and filter stages). Inputs are
signed trades, book snapshots and, where the venue provides it, a participant flag. A possible
output is an imbalance-based short-horizon forecast used as a filter on a slower strategy: trade
in agreement with the imbalance, or delay entry when it opposes. The clock-phase effects of Kim
and Hansen (2026) could be added as a time-of-hour interaction. What can go wrong: published
imbalance edges are measured before costs; the forecast horizon may be shorter than the desk’s
latency; flags for participant types exist only on some venues; and an edge found on one venue or
period may reflect that venue’s design, so it needs a walk-forward test by regime.


                                                81
12.5      Auctions, price pressure and behaviour
Some of the most reliable microstructure effects arise from forced or predictable demand. Wu and
Jegadeesh (2020) study closing auctions and report that the temporary price impact takes three
to five days to dissipate, and that strategies exploiting the impact and reversals are profitable in
their sample. Brown (2025) document that public retail order flow predicts reversals at the open,
again with a long–short strategy in the paper. Coval and Stafford (2005) analyse fire sales caused
by mutual fund flows and find that those who trade against constrained funds earn returns for
providing liquidity. Peress and Schmidt (2020) use sensational news that distracts noise traders
as a natural experiment, finding that trading, liquidity and volatility fall and prices reverse less.
Devadutta Indoria (2024) propose a cognitive-load account of overreaction and underreaction
using tick data across several exchanges.
    Two papers address regimes and endogeneity. Filimonov and Sornette (2012) fit a Hawkes
model to E-mini futures to measure how much of price change is endogenous, and report a
significant rise over 1998 to 2010, with implications for flash-crash prediction; this ties the chapter
to the regime material in Chapter 7. Ochędzan and Antulov-Fantulin (2026) build an agent-based
market in which market makers with different information face self-exciting order flow, to study
adverse selection against price discovery; Chapter 3 treats such simulators. Two peripheral
items belong with the practitioner’s folklore. Jan (2021) give microeconomic foundations for the
volume profile tool, and Sharma et al. (2025) apply integral transforms to market efficiency and
information asymmetry. Neither should be read as evidence of an edge without testing.
Bridge. What microstructure calls “price pressure” and “temporary impact” (the five-day
dissipation in closing auctions) is what the return-forecasting literature calls short-term reversal,
and what the factors literature files as a liquidity-provision premium. A desk that sees the
reversal as a premium paid for absorbing someone’s forced trade will size it by capacity and
inventory risk. One that sees it as a statistical factor will size it by turnover cost. Chapter 9
gives the second vocabulary.


12.6      Working the order: execution, placement and liquidation
If signals tell a desk what to trade, this strand tells it how. The classical frame is Almgren–Chriss.
Labadie and Lehalle (2012) derive recursive formulas for target-close and implementation-shortfall
strategies in it, add minimum participation constraints and propose alternative risk measures
based on self-similar processes. Schedule-based algorithms (TWAP, VWAP, percentage of volume)
are the baseline, and Maciejewski (2026) propose a passive percentage-of-volume variant that sets
its placement rate from observed order flow, without an explicit model. Barzykin et al. (2026)
model passive execution using two observables, the roughly exponential decay of fill probability
with distance from the mid-price and the linear response of price to order-flow imbalance, and
combine them into a passive impact rate. Cao et al. (2008) show on the Australian exchange
that the book affects submissions, cancellations and amendments, and Baruch (2005) analyse
who benefits when the book is visible. Mishra et al. (2025a) study expiry-day behaviour where
agency algorithms displace proprietary ones from queue priority by quoting aggressively.
     A second group casts liquidation as a game or a learning problem. Fu et al. (2025) and Chen
et al. (2024) solve mean-field liquidation games, the second with a major trader and periodic
targeting, motivated by observed periodic volume. Graf and Mastrolia (2026) compare deep
reinforcement-learning policies for liquidation that combines a limit-order book with a closing
auction, tested on synthetic and historical paths in a simulator. Li et al. (2025b) use imitation
learning with a flow-matching policy for high-frequency trading under varied market conditions.
Maeda (2026b) solve a liquidation problem where price follows a skew Brownian motion with
support and resistance levels, which is a rare formal treatment of a technical-analysis idea.


                                                  82
    The common caution is that these models are only as realistic as their impact assumptions.
The mean-field and control papers give structure and sensitivity to parameters; the learning
papers give flexible policies whose validity depends on the simulator, a point Chapter 3 returns
to.
Desk note. Execution scheduler and cost check (execution and validation). Inputs
are the parent order, an intraday volume curve, spread and depth estimates from the first section,
and an impact model. The output is a schedule or passive-placement policy with a pre-trade
cost estimate and a post-trade comparison with realised shortfall. A possible design: use a
participation schedule as the default, and let a passive policy based on fill-probability decay
replace it only where the measured spread is wide. What can go wrong: impact parameters
calibrated on one market regime, simulator-trained policies that exploit simulator artefacts,
auction and expiry-day behaviour that differs from continuous trading, and capacity limits that
make the paper-trading cost optimistic.


12.7      Do algorithms and high-frequency traders improve markets?
          Causal evidence
A desk that trades against, alongside or through high-frequency traders (HFTs) needs an answer
to a plain question: does their presence make execution cheaper or dearer? The papers in this
group share an identification problem rather than a data set. Algorithmic activity is endogenous:
it rises where liquidity is already good. Almost every study therefore leans on an exogenous shock
such as exchange co-location, a tick-size reform, a latency upgrade or a short-sale ban, and then
asks what changed in spreads, depth, volatility and price efficiency. A time-series-classification
reader will recognise the logic as an interventional label: the shock defines a “before” and “after”
class, and the question is which features separate them.
     The broad international and single-market studies mostly point the same way. Boehmer et al.
(2012) use co-location as an instrument across 42 equity markets and report better liquidity and
informational efficiency, somewhat higher short-term volatility, and lower execution shortfalls
for institutional buy-side investors. Aggarwal and Thomas (2014) use a co-location event with
matching and difference-in-differences and find lower liquidity costs, order imbalance and intraday
liquidity risk. Mestel et al. (2018) reach a similar conclusion in Austria with an instrumental-
variables design, consistent with algorithmic traders acting on average as market makers, and
Broussard et al. (2025) classify Nordic traders into algorithmic, institutional, professional and
retail groups and find narrower spreads for algorithmic and institutional traders, with retail
traders surprisingly acting as liquidity providers. Smaller markets and asset classes fit the pattern
with caveats: Hu et al. (2020) find that algorithmic quoting in agricultural futures improves
pricing efficiency and damps short-term volatility but narrows effective spreads only in the more
liquid contracts, and Riyanto et al. (2024) report a positive HFT effect on market quality in
Indonesian LQ-45 stocks together with a link from tick size to HFT activity. Survey-based work
such as Haralayya and Shetkar (2024), which draws on the experience of 70 trading experts,
offers a taxonomy of HFT strategies but not causal estimates, and should be read as vocabulary
rather than evidence.
     The counter-narrative is about heterogeneity. Hu (2026) argue that market-making and
strategic HFT differ in information processing, that one trader can switch roles, and that the
simple dichotomy should be replaced by multi-dimensional identification. Breckenfelder (2024)
find that when HFTs compete, their speculative trading increases and liquidity deteriorates
and short-term volatility rises, while Brogaard and Garriott (2019) find that spreads on a new
venue converge to the incumbent’s as HFTs enter, which fits HFTs behaving as quantity rather
than price competitors. Brogaard et al. (2017) use the 2008 short-sale ban to separate HFT
and non-HFT short selling, and find that HFT short selling adversely selects limit orders and


                                                 83
hurts liquidity. Weller (2018) adds a different cost: more algorithmic activity is associated with
less information in prices ahead of scheduled disclosures, which is a long-horizon effect that
spread-based metrics cannot see. Finally Arumugam (2023) looks at intraday profitability among
buy-side algorithms, HFTs and non-algorithmic traders and finds that the three groups win and
lose in different ways depending on whether they supply or demand liquidity.
    Two ideas make these results portable to a trading desk. The first is measurement without
labels. Hasbrouck and Saar (2013) propose a low-latency activity measure computed from widely
available message data and correlated with exchange-constructed HFT estimates, and Murray
et al. (2016) show that a latency cut on one exchange improved liquidity but did not lastingly
cut costs for institutions that already had low-latency access. The second is the structure of
rents. Yao and Ye (2018) show that a uniform tick size and time priority allocate rents to the
fastest liquidity providers, and that the message-to-trade ratio is a poor proxy for HFT liquidity
provision. Barardehi et al. (2025) refine the Tick Size Pilot reading by finding thresholds in the
number of ticks inside the spread at which a larger tick switches from helping to harming quality,
a warning against treating the pilot as a clean exogenous liquidity shock for all stocks.
Desk note. Venue and algorithm-selection filter (execution stage). Inputs are message-
level quote and trade data per symbol and venue, plus event dates for tick-size, latency or fee
changes. The output is a per-stock regime label, for example a high-speed-competition indicator
built from a low-latency activity measure, and a spread-to-tick ratio that conditions the choice
between passive and aggressive child orders. A natural use is to estimate effective and realised
spreads for the desk’s own fills on each side of a venue event, as a validation check on routing
assumptions. What can go wrong: published effects are average treatment effects on large, liquid
names and may reverse for wide-spread or small stocks; the shocks are old and the competitive
landscape has moved; and a measure calibrated on one exchange’s message format does not
transfer unchanged.


12.8      Auctions, ticks, expiries and market design
Market design determines where a systematic desk can trade without paying for immediacy.
Closing and opening auctions are the clearest case. Bogousslavsky and Muravyev (2023) document
that closing auctions carry a growing share of daily volume, that closing prices typically match
pre-close quotes, that price impact is lower than in continuous trading, and that auction volume
jumps on index additions, which they connect to indexing and ETF growth. In stress the
picture changes: Foley et al. (2022) report severely dislocated closing prices during COVID-19,
exacerbated by retail activity and reduced HFT liquidity provision. Older exchange-level evidence,
Camilleri and Green (2009) on the suspension of call auctions at the National Stock Exchange
of India, found improved volatility, efficiency and liquidity after suspension and suggests that
auctions carry liquidity externalities that help the liquid more than the illiquid. A comparative
study of the two main Indian exchanges, Goel et al. (2021), covers trading mechanisms, efficiency
tests and liquidity statistics, and is mostly useful as a reference for the institutional facts of local
venues.
    Settlement and expiry mechanics create predictable flow. Hsieh and Ma (2009) compare two
index futures on the same underlying with different settlement rules and find that average-price
settlement minimises expiration-day effects relative to opening or closing price settlement, with
foreign institutional activity increasing them. Hancock (1993), whose abstract is not in our
brief, is titled as a study of the triple witching hour, and we place it here as an expiry-effects
reference only. Together these papers say that scheduled liquidity events are not noise; they are
known-timing features that any intraday model and any execution schedule must either exploit
or avoid.



                                                  84
12.9      Market impact, optimal execution and transaction-cost
          analysis
12.9.1    Why impact has the shape it has
Several theoretical papers explain why trading large size is so costly, and they fit together. Lillo
et al. (2005) show that long memory in order signs can arise when large orders, whose sizes
follow a power law, are split and executed incrementally. Toth et al. (2011) propose a dynamical
theory in which the supply and demand profile is V-shaped and vanishes near the current price,
so that metaorders must be fragmented and small orders have a disproportionate, square-root
impact. Sato et al. (2026) embed the square-root law in the order-splitting model to reconcile
predictable order flow with approximately diffusive prices, and Zhou et al. (2026a) test three
competing predictions for the square-root exponent in a simulated order-book model and find
that none of them reproduces the measured exponents alone, concluding that order splitting
and liquidity replenishment are jointly necessary. The book-length treatment Bouchaud et al.
(2018) sits behind much of this literature (its abstract is not in our brief), as does Doyne Farmer
5 et al. (2004), whose title asks what really causes large price changes and which we cite for
the question only. At the strategic end, Evangelista et al. (2022) derive price formation from
game-theoretic and mean-field models with liquidity costs, Yang et al. (2020c) model back-runners
who infer fundamental information from past order flow so that fundamental investors randomise
their orders, and Yang et al. (2024) show that an informed institution can underperform naive
price-taking individuals when noise trading is high. Serrano-Padial (2012) supplies the related
behavioural result that prediction-market prices are mispriced when naive traders are numerous,
which we include as a reminder that noise-trader share matters for any impact model.

12.9.2    From impact models to schedules and monitoring
On the practical side, Rastegar and Eghbalreihani (2019) fit an I-star market-impact function
for Tehran Stock Exchange shares and use it to split a large order over the investor’s horizon, an
example of a simple, auditable schedule built from an estimated cost curve. Lehalle and Neuman
(2019) add a Markovian signal to the transient-impact optimal trading framework and give an
explicit strategy for a mean-reverting signal with exponentially decaying impact; the point for a
desk is that alpha and impact decay pull the trade in opposite directions. Zovko (2026) argue
that real-time slippage monitoring needs hundreds of fills to separate impact from background
volatility and does not establish causality, which makes in-flight impact detection a statistical
design problem. After the trade, Markov (2019) give a Bayesian framework for comparing broker
algorithms on implementation shortfall, volume-weighted and participation-weighted benchmarks
and short-term reversion, with fat tails and heteroscedasticity, and with hierarchical pooling
across algorithms. Equally relevant to cost modelling is Sai et al. (2026), which frames the
economics of an algorithmic platform through slippage and capital-budgeting measures such as
net present value; its claims about order-flow shares are broad, so we use it as a framing of the
business case rather than a source of parameters.
Desk note. Impact calibration and TCA loop (execution and validation stages).
Inputs are the fund’s own parent-order and fill records, arrival-price and interval benchmarks,
and daily volume and volatility. Outputs are a per-instrument impact curve (for example a
square-root form with fitted coefficient), a schedule that trades off signal decay against impact,
and a ranked list of broker algorithms with credible intervals. A natural workflow is to fit
the curve offline, use it as a cost term in the portfolio stage, and update the broker ranking
hierarchically so that thin-data algorithms borrow strength from the group. What can go wrong:
impact estimates are conditioned on the fund’s own strategy mix and are contaminated by
signal-driven selection of when to trade; a square-root exponent fitted on liquid names will be
wrong for illiquid ones; and capacity claims inherit every misspecification of the curve.

                                                85
12.10      Learning to execute and to make markets
Reinforcement learning (RL) is the natural machine-learning framing of execution: a state of
inventory, time and book features, an action of order size or price, and a reward equal to shortfall.
The papers here are mainly methodological and mostly simulation based. Zhang et al. (2023b)
model optimised trade execution as offline RL with a dynamic context and study why existing
methods overfit, which is the key concern for any deployment. Zaznov et al. (2026) propose a
deterministic actor-critic variant with pessimistic backups, policy smoothing and conservative
regularisation for liquidating large stock sell programs. Cheridito and Weiss (2026) build a
multi-level market-making agent that submits market and limit orders of varying size across
price levels while controlling inventory, with a deep-set encoder for variable-length order sets
and potential-based reward shaping, tested in three simulated environments. Koulouris and
Campajola (2026) place two deep RL agents in a shared liquidation game and examine whether
memory lets them reach lower implementation shortfall than the game-theoretic competitive
benchmark, a reminder that learning agents interact with one another. The deployment side is
addressed by Hong et al. (2026), who push order-book construction and learned inference into
programmable network switches to cut latency.
    For a desk, the shared lesson is that the hard part is not the optimiser but the simulator and
the evaluation. Each of these methods learns against an assumed impact model or a replayed
book, and results are only as good as that environment’s response to the agent’s own orders,
which replay cannot provide.


12.11      Stress, flash crashes and the fragility of liquidity
12.11.1     Who supplies liquidity when it matters
Whether electronic liquidity providers withdraw in stress is a recurring question. Brogaard
et al. (2018) find that HFTs on average absorb imbalances during extreme price movements in
single stocks, but that when several stocks move together their liquidity demand dominates their
supply, with little evidence that they cause the movements. Nawn and Banerjee (2019) find that
proprietary algorithmic traders on the National Stock Exchange of India increase limit order
supply after high volatility and extreme moves, with sub-three-millisecond revisers behaving
like their parent class. Arumugam (2025) study the two-way link between algorithmic activity
and intraday tail risk, finding that order placement and cancellation by proprietary and agency
algorithms are associated with lower tail risk, while rising tail risk reduces order activity. Panayi
et al. (2015) take the design view: designated market makers have quoting obligations that
do not track uneven liquidity demand, and the authors recommend adding requirements on
replenishment speed measured by a threshold exceedance duration. Kumar and Misra (2018)
and Cheriyan and Lazar (2019) document commonality in liquidity on Indian markets, with
individual-stock liquidity co-moving strongly with market and industry liquidity; for a portfolio
this means that liquidity shocks are correlated precisely when many positions need to be exited.

12.11.2     Toxicity indicators and cascades
Indicators built on order-flow imbalance, notably volume-synchronised probability of informed
trading (VPIN), were proposed as early warnings. The evidence here is sceptical: Andersen
and Bondarenko (2014) find VPIN a poor predictor of short-run volatility that peaked after,
not before, the flash crash, and Abad et al. (2018) find that high VPIN readings rarely precede
abnormal illiquidity or trading halts once realised volatility is controlled. The same measure is
nevertheless useful descriptively; Bhattacharya and Chakrabarti (2014) apply it to Indian IPO
aftermarkets and find adverse selection risk highest on listing day and decaying thereafter, and
Teodorovic (2011) apply a standard price-impact decomposition to stocks of different liquidity


                                                 86
on the London Stock Exchange. A systematic desk should thus treat VPIN as a state descriptor
to be validated out of sample, not as a risk trigger.
    Cascade mechanisms get a more structural treatment. Osler (2005) present evidence that
stop-loss orders can create self-reinforcing price cascades in currency markets, and Virgilio
(2022) identifies prior volatility, scarce liquidity, large quantity and stop-loss orders as drivers of
very short-time price changes. Turiel and Aste (2021) show that volume distributions during
anomalous events have tail exponents below two, hence a diverging second moment, and tie flash
crashes to self-organised criticality; Turiel and Aste (2022) report a phase transition between
small and large co-crashes. Johnson et al. (2013) argue that a sub-second all-machine regime of
extreme events emerged beyond human response time. Seuma (2026) measure branching ratios
in crypto-perpetual liquidation cascades and find the largest one to be strongly subcritical, Woo
(2026) show that scalar per-product monitoring of leveraged-ETF rebalancing feedback misses
coupled amplification, and Gao et al. (2026a) show larger price pressure from corporate bond
ETF fire sales during COVID-19. Xu et al. (2026a) train a neural detector for mechanical quote
erosion in an agent-based simulator, where ground truth exists, and O’Donovan et al. (2023)
link retail option flow to hedging-driven effects on market quality. A looser group covers flow
by investor type: Dhingra et al. (2016) study foreign institutional flows and volatility in India,
finding them to be positive-feedback traders who increase volatility when selling.
Desk note. Liquidity-stress filter (regime and risk stage). Inputs are order-book depth
and spread series, signed volume and, where available, participant-type flow. Outputs are a
liquidity-regime flag, a cross-asset commonality score, and a participation cap that tightens
when the flag trips. A natural use is to define the cap from heavy-tailed volume models rather
than Gaussian ones, in line with tail exponents below two, and to couple it with a position-level
cascade check for ETF or leveraged-product exposure. What can go wrong: indicators such as
VPIN may fail out of sample, so backtest the trigger against realised-volatility controls; simulated
ground truth (as in the crumbling-quote detector) does not guarantee real-data precision; and
regime flags that depend on a single venue can miss cross-venue stress.


12.12       Point-process and structural models of order flow
Event-time models treat orders as a self-exciting sequence. Bacry et al. (2015) review Hawkes
processes in finance, covering volatility estimation from transaction data and applications to
many high-frequency problems, and Jaisson and Rosenbaum (2015) show that nearly unstable
Hawkes processes rescale to integrated Cox–Ingersoll–Ross dynamics, a result that explains why
fitted kernels sit close to the stability boundary. The cascade papers above (branching ratios,
criticality) are the same mathematics under another name. For ecology and structure, Handa
et al. (1998) is titled as a study of the ecology of an order-driven market, and we place it as a
background reference in the agent-based tradition without further claims.
    A natural use is to estimate a branching ratio per instrument in rolling windows as a cheap
feature of endogenous activity, while treating a near-unit estimate as a modelling warning rather
than a signal.




                                                  87
12.13      Bridges out of this chapter

Bridge. Toxic-flow regimes and time-series mining (Chapter 5, Chapter 7). Mi-
crostructure speaks of “flow toxicity”, “crumbling quotes” and liquidity “regimes”. Time-series
mining speaks of anomalies, discords and change points in a multivariate stream. The crumbling-
quote detector and the VPIN critiques are, in that language, supervised and unsupervised
anomaly detection with an unreliable label; the methods of Chapter 5 for similarity search and
Chapter 7 for change points could supply label-free alternatives, while microstructure supplies
the economic meaning of a break (a market maker withdrawing) that a generic change-point
method lacks.

Bridge. Execution as decision-making under uncertainty (Chapter 13, Chapter 14).
Optimal execution and RL execution are what the decision-theory silo calls sequential stochastic
control, and the overfitting results map onto what Chapter 14 calls backtest overfitting. The
impact curve estimated here is the transaction-cost term that portfolio construction in Chapter 13
needs; conversely, portfolio people can read “capacity” as the point where the square-root curve
consumes the signal’s edge.

Bridge. Event-time clocks and generative simulation (Chapter 3, Chapter 4). Hawkes
branching, order-splitting long memory and agent-based books are generators of order flow.
The synthetic-data chapter treats such simulators as training grounds; the lesson here is that a
simulator must reproduce impact, long-memory signs and stress behaviour before an RL agent
trained on it can be trusted. Chapter 4 supplies the participant taxonomy (retail, institutional,
proprietary) that these simulators need as agent types.




                                               88
Chapter 13

Portfolio Construction and Decisions
under Uncertainty

Five communities meet in this chapter, and they rarely cite one another. Operations researchers
and “decision” machine-learning people speak of predict-then-optimize, regret, contextual op-
timization and differentiable optimization layers. Statisticians speak of random-matrix noise
bands, eigenmodes, efficient-frontier inference and conformal coverage. Applied mathematicians
speak of stochastic control, optimal stopping, rough paths and signatures. Reinforcement-
learning researchers speak of rewards, policies and distributional critics. A smaller group from
language-model research speaks of calibrated confidence and abstention. Each of these words
has a counterpart in the shared vocabulary of the atlas. A “decision loss” or “regret” is a
decision-focused learning objective. An “eigenmode outside the Marchenko–Pastur band” is a
factor-model direction in the cross-section. A “coverage guarantee” is a form of uncertainty
quantification. A “signature-linear control” is a path-signature portfolio-optimization rule.
    In the fund pipeline of the atlas (data, features, signal, regime and risk, portfolio, execution,
validation, research automation) this chapter sits in the middle. Its input is whatever the signal
and forecast stages emit: point forecasts, ranks, probabilities, or a covariance estimate. Its output
is a vector of target positions that the execution stage must turn into orders. Its central question
is how forecast quality, forecast uncertainty and cost should shape the size of a bet. Return
forecasting is treated in Chapter 8, cost-aware execution in Chapter 12, and the validation of the
whole stack in Chapter 14.
    The chapter is organised by the problem each group of papers attacks. We first clean the
covariance matrix, then size bets and choose allocation rules, then plan trades with costs, then
train forecasts through the allocator, then learn policies by reinforcement learning, then attach
uncertainty and abstention to the signal, and finally cover the theory papers that supply tools
without a trading application of their own. Where a paper is peripheral we say so and place it in
a group.


13.1      Cleaning the covariance matrix
Every mean–variance allocator inverts or otherwise consumes an estimated correlation matrix, so
the first problem is that the matrix is mostly noise. Laloux et al. (1999) compare the eigenvalue
density of empirical stock correlation matrices with random-matrix theory and conclude that
blind use of empirical correlations for risk management is doubtful. Plerou et al. (2002) extend
the test to several large panels and find that most eigenvalues fall inside the random-matrix
bounds, with deviations carrying genuine market and sector structure. These two classic papers
define the idea: a null model of uncorrelated series gives a noise band, and only modes outside it
are treated as information.
    Recent work from the same tradition reuses the idea for networks. Pawanesh et al. (2025) filter

                                                 89
the correlation matrix of NIFTY 200 and 500 stocks and report that core–periphery structure
lives in the market mode while community structure lives in the sector mode. Ansari et al. (2026)
keep only 10 to 16 structured eigenmodes of NIFTY and S&P 500 correlation matrices and
report more stable core–periphery networks than the full matrix gives. Ansari et al. (2025) then
use the core–periphery profile directly: portfolios of periphery stocks from minimum spanning
trees and planar filtered graphs are compared with centrality-based strategies on Sharpe, Sortino
and information ratios. Network people call a stock “peripheral” what a risk manager would call
weakly exposed to the dominant market factor; a natural use is as a diversification screen, not as
a stand-alone alpha.
    Other papers replace the correlation matrix or tame its use. Smyth (2025) cluster equities
with entropy-based rather than correlation-based distances to select stable minimum-variance
constituents, and note that clustering can flag stationarity changes in excess returns. Hiraki and
Sun (2022) propose OWL shrinkage, which gives similar weights to correlated stocks and, as
the abstract reports, lands near the 1/N portfolio with lower turnover. Gregnanin et al. (2024)
swap the Pearson matrix for a signature-based similarity matrix on S&P 500 networks and
report better cumulative log-returns and Sharpe ratio than the correlation baseline. Halperin
(2026) go further and drop the covariance and the expected-return vector, building the state
from a correlation distance matrix and two rank-transition matrices; its out-of-sample claims
should be read against the rest of this book’s warnings on backtests. Banner et al. (2005) offer a
stylised counterpart, rank-based Atlas models, in which growth rates depend on rank; these are
a benchmark world rather than a tradable recipe.
Desk note. Covariance hygiene gate (portfolio and risk stages). Input: a trailing
window of daily returns for the tradable universe. Output: a cleaned correlation matrix and a
count of eigenmodes above the noise band, used as an input to the optimiser and as a risk limit.
A natural use of the random-matrix papers is to compare the leading eigenvalue and the number
of retained modes each week and to freeze rebalancing when the count jumps. What can go
wrong: the noise band assumes independent observations, so fat tails and volatility clustering
make the band optimistic; window length versus universe size sets the aspect ratio and hence
the band; and a cleaned matrix that is stable in sample may still be unstable in a crisis, when
correlations rise together. Cleaning does not fix biased expected returns.


13.2     Sizing bets: Kelly, universal portfolios and Markowitz at
         scale
Once the matrix is usable, the next problem is how much to bet. Stiffelman (2026) revisit the
Kelly criterion through Cover’s universal portfolio, and argue that Kelly’s objective splits into
a money term, an entropy term and a divergence term; this gives sizing a regret benchmark
against the best constant-rebalanced portfolio. Barmish and Proskurnikov (2025) ask a practical
variant: update the account value from realised open, high, low and close bars, without assuming
geometric Brownian motion, and tailored to the order type. A related sizing idea appears
in Hubáček and Šír (2020), who argue that a predictor trained to be decorrelated from the
market price can profit even if it is less accurate than the market, by exploiting a market taker’s
advantage. We read this as a proposal about objectives rather than a rule to deploy.
    The mainstream practical answer is convex optimisation. Boyd et al. (2024) describe a
Markowitz extension that includes transaction costs and leverage limits and handles uncertainty
in return statistics, still as a convex problem. Boyd et al. (2017) give the multi-period version,
trading off return, risk, transaction and holding cost and executing only the first planned
trade. Johansson and Boyd (2025) apply the same convex receding-horizon idea to tax-lot and
account-location costs in retirement funding; it is not a trading paper, but shows how cost
terms enter a convex planner. Bodnar et al. (2009) supply the inference side: under a stationary


                                                90
Gaussian assumption, estimated efficient-frontier quantities are asymptotically normal, so claimed
improvements can come with confidence bands. Finally, Bengio (1997) is an early argument
that the model should be trained on net trading gains, including transaction costs, instead of on
squared prediction error. That idea is the seed of the next section.
Desk note. Cost-aware rebalancer (portfolio and execution stages). Input: signal
scores or forecast returns, a cleaned covariance matrix, current holdings, a cost model and borrow
rates. Output: target trades for the next bar, with only the first step of the plan sent to execution.
A natural use of the convex multi-period framework is as the production layer under any signal,
with the risk-aversion and cost-aversion parameters treated as tuned hyperparameters. What
can go wrong: forecast errors are amplified by the optimiser, so the uncertainty-aware variants
matter; cost models fitted on small trades understate impact at capacity; and tuning parameters
on the backtest is itself a source of overfitting (see Chapter 14).


13.3      Path-dependent allocation with signatures and kernels
A separate mathematical line replaces the snapshot of means and covariances with the whole
price path. Bayer et al. (2026a) extend the notion of a trading strategy to signatures, solve
a mean–variance criterion explicitly, and sketch an application to optimal execution. Bank
et al. (2025) show that controls written as functionals of the path signature are dense in the
progressively measurable controls, which licenses Monte Carlo computation of approximately
optimal controls. Alonso (2026b) cast portfolio theory in the signature coordinate and link the
gap between two lifts to the excess growth rate; the abstract reports an empirical dimensional
trade-off, which means gains depend on how many path terms must be estimated. Futter et al.
(2025) offer a kernel alternative with closed-form solutions and report that both signature and
kernel approaches beat classical Markovian methods when dynamics or signals are time dependent.
Bloch (2026a) is a more promotional paper that proposes path-geometry risk monitors; we treat
its claims as untested until validated out of sample.
    What mathematics calls a “signature feature” is, for the machine-learning side, a fixed
nonlinear embedding of a window of returns that is invariant to reparametrisation. Chapter 6
treats signatures as features; here they appear as the input to an allocator.


13.4      Training the forecast through the allocator
The idea of Bengio (1997) has since become a field of its own, called decision-focused learning or
smart predict-then-optimize. Mandi et al. (2024) survey it and benchmark eleven methods on
seven problems; Zhang and Li (2026) classify the designs into closed-loop losses, differentiable
optimisation layers and parameterised policies; Sadana et al. (2023) map the same field into
operations-research language, where it appears as contextual or prescriptive optimization. For
a trading desk the three surveys supply a translation table: what a quant calls “fitting the
alpha model, then running the optimiser” is two-stage predict-then-optimize, and what ML calls
“decision loss” is what the desk calls realised net P&L after the allocator.
    Several papers refine the method. Balghiti et al. (2023) give generalization bounds for the
smart predict-then-optimize loss, a guide to how much data a decision-aware return model
needs. Schutte et al. (2024) replace empirical regret with expected regret to account for aleatoric
and epistemic uncertainty. Wang et al. (2025c) allow feedback from the optimisation back to
prediction. Kotary et al. (2023) learn the optimal decision directly from features, which could
amortise a heavy optimiser across daily rebalances. Caunhye et al. (2026) train against worst-case
feature perturbations, a natural fit for noisy decision-time features. Wang et al. (2025a) sample
tail regions of a learned generative model of the optimisation parameters. Zhang et al. (2026h) is
an inventory paper, but its CVaR-aware allocation layer under shared capacity is structurally


                                                 91
close to a constrained portfolio problem, and we cite it as an analogy. Boyd et al. (2024) again
matters here, since a convex allocator is exactly the kind of layer through which a forecast can
be trained.
    Two papers bring the same logic to the feedback that our own trading creates. Cristian et al.
(2025) learn under endogenous uncertainty, where decisions bias the data they later generate;
in trading this is market impact. Bajpai (2025b) prove, in a stylised closed-loop model, that
high predictive power need not imply profit and can accelerate P&L erosion under convex
execution costs. We take from it a proposal: evaluate forecasts by their post-cost, post-feedback
contribution, not by their accuracy.
Desk note. End-to-end forecast training (signal to portfolio stages). Input: features,
a feature-to-return model, and the production allocator with its constraints and cost model.
Output: a forecast model whose loss is the allocator’s regret, or the net return it achieves. A
natural pilot is to fine-tune an existing return model with a decision loss on a small universe and
compare it with the same model trained on squared error, under identical costs. What can go
wrong: gradients through the optimiser are fragile when constraints bind; decision losses overfit
the in-sample optimum even more readily than squared error does; and the benefit may vanish
once feedback from our own trading is included, as the negative result above suggests.


13.5     Reinforcement-learning allocators and regimes
Reinforcement learning (RL) treats allocation as a sequential policy rather than a one-shot
optimisation. Jeon et al. (2024) feed frequency-band decompositions of stock signals to a deep
RL allocator, arguing that time-domain models miss abrupt shifts. Aminrostamkolaee et al.
(2025) compare seven reward functions across CNN, CNN+LSTM and Transformer agents and
report that minimising standard deviation worked best across architectures and horizons; the
lesson for a desk is that the reward is a design choice that deserves a validation harness. Upreti
(2026) replace hard agent selection with a hidden-Markov-model soft mixture of agents and add
a CVaR penalty to the reward, and Pan et al. (2026) use a regime detector to trigger knowledge
transfer between allocators rather than full rolling retraining. In regime language (Chapter 7) the
two papers use a posterior over states as a gate. Zerman (2022) address a neighbouring choice,
the trading frequency, with a hierarchical RL agent in an FX setting. Bouaicha et al. (2022) give
the control-theory counterpart, equilibrium allocation under Markov regime switching, which
could supply a principled action map for a regime module.
    A second group concerns where rewards and policies come from. Haußmann and Kandemir
(2026) model the joint distribution of several returns, such as return and drawdown, with Stein
particles. Ho and Ermon (2016) and Fu et al. (2018) learn policies or rewards from expert
demonstrations, and Roa-Vicens et al. (2019) test inverse RL on a simple limit-order-book
environment. Peng et al. (2019) learn from logged off-policy data with supervised regression
steps. Sokota et al. (2026) solve games of hidden information, which is a loose analogy for
counterparties with unobserved intent. All four are general RL tools with no trading result of
their own; they are peripheral here, and any desk use is our proposal.
    Stopping problems form the continuous-time cousin of RL exits. Colaneri and De Angelis
(2022) solve a recursive stopping problem for trading across two venues, Maeda and Jacka (2020)
model support and resistance as a stochastic process to decide when to exit, and Maeda (2026a)
solve the matching entry problem under a skew Brownian motion. These are stylised models
whose assumptions must be tested on real prices before any rule is adopted.




                                                92
13.6      Uncertainty, calibration and abstention
The last family asks when not to trade, and how to scale a bet to the forecaster’s own doubt.
Hutchison et al. (2004) prove that calibration does not reduce accuracy and gives decision
thresholds, which is the textbook justification for calibrating a directional classifier before
converting its probabilities into sizes. Chalkidis and Savani (2021) let a classifier abstain and
add a flat third class for small moves; Nabar and Shroff (2023) chain abstaining models so
that predictions are made only where support is high. Lahlou et al. (2021) estimate epistemic
uncertainty as excess risk and Sanderink (2026) adapt it to cross-sectional rankers, reporting that
inverse-uncertainty sizing de-levers the strongest signals and hurts, and proposing a two-level
policy that first decides whether to trade at all. That finding is a warning: uncertainty and
signal strength can be correlated, so a naive size rule can cancel the edge.
    Conformal methods give finite-sample coverage. Taheri Hosseinkhani (2026) report that
machine-learning return intervals achieve near-marginal coverage while high idiosyncratic-volatility
and market-regime cells are undercovered, and build a signal from that undercoverage. Singh
et al. (2026) show on imbalanced tabular datasets (credit scoring, fraud, healthcare, industrial
safety) that marginal conformal prediction under-covers rare classes and that class-conditional
(Mondrian) prediction restores coverage. Bach (2025) give a convex loss trading set size against
conditional coverage, and Zhang (2026) learn tail-aware ellipsoids for multivariate prediction,
which could define joint return uncertainty sets for a robust optimiser. Noarov and Roth (2026)
give online forecasts with guarantees against adaptive sequences.
    Language models enter the same stack. Torres et al. (2026) combine retrieval, conformal
calibration and abstention for financial forecasts, Damani et al. (2026) train models to report
calibrated confidence with a Brier-score reward, and Dixon (2026b) map word probabilities to
posteriors over declared states, using professional market text. Dixon (2026a) formulate how
much decision authority an AI agent should receive as evidence quality changes. Chapter 15 treats
these systems in depth. Finally, Sadeghi et al. (2024) learn causal links among nonstationary
financial series, Jung et al. (2022) attribute outcomes to causes by do-Shapley values, and Felin
and Holweg (2024) argue for theory-based causal reasoning over data-based prediction. The
three are peripheral to allocation, but together suggest a research-governance gate: require a
causal story and an attribution plan for each signal.
Desk note. Trade-or-abstain gate and exposure scaler (risk stage). Input: a model’s
score together with a calibrated probability, a conformal interval or an epistemic-uncertainty
estimate, evaluated by regime cell (volatility bucket, market state). Output: a multiplier on
target size, from zero to one, plus a kill-switch flag. A natural design follows the two-level idea:
a slow gate decides whether the strategy trades at all, and a fast rule sizes within active trades,
with the uncertainty signal first tested for correlation with signal strength. What can go wrong:
calibration data are scarce in exactly the regimes that matter; calibration windows leak if built
with future information; and a gate tuned on one stress episode may be nothing more than a fit
to that episode.


13.7      Bridges out of this chapter

Bridge. Decision loss and the backtest. What operations research calls “regret” and the
decision-focused papers call “decision loss” is what Chapter 14 calls net post-cost performance.
Conversely, the “backtest overfitting” and walk-forward language of that chapter tells decision-
focused learners that a regret estimate on one historical path is a single noisy sample. Both
silos would gain by reporting regret with the confidence bands that Bodnar et al. (2009) give for
frontier quantities, and by testing reward choices as Aminrostamkolaee et al. (2025) do.



                                                93
Bridge. Noise bands, regimes and discords. What random-matrix theory calls an
“eigenvalue outside the noise band” is a factor in Chapter 9, and a jump in the number of such
modes is, in the vocabulary of Chapter 7, a candidate change point; in time-series mining, the
same event is a “discord” in a rolling correlation profile. The regime-gated allocators of Upreti
(2026) and Pan et al. (2026) could take this count as a state input.

Bridge. Own-impact and microstructure. What Cristian et al. (2025) call “endogenous
uncertainty” and Bajpai (2025b) call “feedback” is what Chapter 12 calls market impact and
crowding. Microstructure models could supply the counterfactual that decision-focused learners
lack, while portfolio people could in turn supply the objective, net P&L, that microstructure
papers often leave implicit.

Bridge. Calibrated confidence as a shared interface. The conformal, epistemic and
LLM-calibration papers above all produce a number meaning “how much to trust this forecast”.
Chapter 8 produces the forecasts and Chapter 15 the text-derived views; a common calibrated-
confidence interface would let this chapter size both in the same units.




                                               94
      Part VI

Research Operations




         95
Chapter 14

Backtesting and Research Practice

Four communities share this chapter and rarely read one another. The time-series machine-
learning people (the tsml silo) speak of labels, targets, barriers, imbalance and classification
accuracy. The statisticians speak of multiple testing, selection bias, size and power, false discovery
and superior predictive ability. A third group, which we call the practice silo, consists of quant
practitioners, platform builders and sceptical auditors. It speaks of backtests, walk-forward,
profit factor, live versus paper and null results. A fourth, smaller group of financial economists
and control or decision scientists contributes the vocabulary of specification search, evidence
hierarchies and feedback.
    In the shared vocabulary of the atlas these become two families of concepts. Everything about
what a model is asked to predict is label design (with the triple barrier and meta-labelling as
named special cases). Everything about whether a reported result should be believed is backtest
overfitting, multiple testing, data leakage, forecast evaluation and walk-forward validation. A
third strand, transaction costs, is the reality check that cuts across both. A classification person
who says “the F1 score on the buy class improved” and a statistician who says “the Sharpe ratio
is not significant after correcting for the number of trials” are making statements about different
links in one chain, and the chapter tries to connect them.
    In the fund pipeline (data, features, signal, regime and risk, portfolio, execution, validation,
research automation) this chapter sits at both ends of the research loop. Labels belong to
the data and forecast stages: they define the supervised problem before any model is chosen.
Validation sits after the signal and portfolio stages and gates what reaches capital. The final
section of the chapter covers the research-automation stage, that is, the platforms that make
experiments reproducible and keep backtest and live code identical. The chapter is therefore less
about any single alpha than about the machinery that decides which alphas are real.


14.1      What to predict: the label as a design choice
Most machine-learning work on markets treats the target as given: the next-bar or next-month
return, or its sign. A large group of papers in this collection argues that this is the weakest
assumption in the pipeline. The cleanest evidence is cross-sectional. Guida and Coqueret (2026)
hold the universe, the features and the model fixed and decompose the label into location,
scale and shape operators, finding that reshaping the target alone changes long-short decile
performance and that the label explains more out-of-sample variation than the model does.
Cakici and Zaremba (2026) reach a compatible conclusion across 35 equity markets, where
ordinal targets beat raw-return targets, with the caveat that rank targets discard magnitude and
lose ground when dispersion or skewness is high. At the binary end of the spectrum, Balogh
et al. (2027) show that the return threshold defining a “positive” class changes class separability
sector by sector. For a desk the lesson is simple: a Gaussianised rank or a thresholded sign is a
modelling decision with its own hyperparameters, and it deserves the same ablation discipline as


                                                 96
the architecture.
    A second family replaces the next-bar target with a label that describes a trend or an event.
Wu et al. (2020) criticise short-horizon comparison labels for missing continuous trends and
propose continuous trend labelling. Han et al. (2022) label only at local extrema over N periods,
so that small price changes do not generate noisy labels, and feed an XGBoost trading system.
Saberi et al. (2024) label by the percentage move over a window with adaptive thresholds and use
LightGBM. A et al. (2022) add a third “no-action” class so that a model may abstain from small
moves. Pasterak (2026) go the other way and replace hard classes by smoothly varying buy-to-sell
values fitted by evolutionary search. Wang and Gu (2025) build the interim path into the label,
scoring an event by a probability that accounts for drawdown along the way and not only the
end point. These are variations on one idea: the label should be an actionable trade outcome,
not a price comparison. Their natural users are teams that already run a gradient-boosted signal
and want fewer, cleaner training examples; the cost is that labelling only at extrema or breakouts
shrinks the sample and makes the label depend on hindsight about the window, which must be
handled in the cross-validation design.
    How should the labelling rule be chosen? One answer is to simulate. Kovačević et al. (2023)
define the robustness of a trend-labelling algorithm through a noise model of classifier error,
so that algorithms can be compared without training a classifier each time. Another is to
search: AlRashedy and Mathkour (2025) evaluate all combinations of model type, look-ahead
window and feature-subset size per asset, and Song et al. (2026b) argue that the best supervision
signal can sit at an intermediate horizon, different from the prediction target, and select it by
bi-level optimisation. Kili et al. (2025) push adaptivity further with multi-scale, causally filtered,
meta-learned labels that output calibrated probabilities. Search over labels is, however, a form of
specification search, and every extra label option enlarges the multiple-testing burden discussed
later in this chapter.
    Two groups attack label quality rather than definition. Akşehir and Kılıç (2023) denoise
prices with a signal-processing decomposition (EEMDAN) before labelling, and Yang et al. (2023)
use a meta-learned label-correction network under a multi-task framework, borrowing from the
weak-supervision literature. Rare-event settings raise the imbalance problem directly. Zhao et al.
(2025c) treat label imbalance between profitable and unprofitable events once transaction costs
are netted out in Chinese futures, and Zaman and Sahed (2026) extend the target in a Bitcoin
limit-order-book setting to include high-volatility regimes, which enlarges the informative class.
In a derivatives setting, Vaghela (2026) label intraday option bars by Black-Scholes repriced
profit and loss, with theta, vega and costs inside the target, rather than by the direction of the
underlying. A natural reading is that the more costs and risk are pushed into the label, the less
the label and the final P&L can diverge.
Desk note. Stage: data and forecast. Input: daily or intraday bars for a cross-section of
instruments, plus a cost estimate per instrument. Output: a label-ablation table before any
model comparison. A reasonable protocol is to hold features and model fixed and vary the target
among raw return, cross-sectional rank (as studied in Guida and Coqueret, 2026), thresholded
sign and a net-of-cost event label, and to report each variant across folds. What can go wrong:
labels built from a window that extends past the training cut leak the future into the validation
set; rank and extremum labels can look good on classification metrics while the traded portfolio
is poor; and every label variant tried counts as a trial for the deflation steps below.


14.2      Barriers, meta-labels and the gap between a label and a
          P&L
The triple-barrier method labels an event by whichever is hit first: a take-profit barrier, a stop-loss
barrier or a time limit. It is the point where the label vocabulary meets the risk vocabulary, since


                                                  97
the barriers are stop and target levels. The empirical papers here mostly ask whether it helps.
Li et al. (2022) compare raw-return, fixed-horizon and triple-barrier labels on one deep model for
the Nasdaq-100 and find that each scheme wins on a different metric, with the triple barrier best
on the buy-signal F1 score. Kovačević et al. (2022) ask the more important question, namely how
classification performance maps to strategy performance, and report a statistically significant but
low correlation for fixed-horizon and triple-barrier labels. The same warning arrives in a sharper
form from Lima (2026): in a factor-investing setting, a positive top-decile lift on the take-profit
label coexisted with a negative event-return lift, and the converted mark-to-market portfolio
lost money at a 50 basis-point round-trip cost. A better classification score is not evidence of a
better portfolio, and Bach (2026) make the related point that the metric a classifier is tuned on
(precision on trades rather than raw accuracy) is tied to the expected Sharpe ratio.
    Applications of the barrier idea extend it in several directions. Fu et al. (2024) tune barrier
widths for pair trading in crypto with a genetic algorithm, targeting profit or maximum drawdown.
Youssefi et al. (2026) test triple-barrier labels with tree classifiers on four timeframes for Bitcoin
and find that the choice of timeframe matters. Grądzki et al. (2025) combine information-driven
bars (CUSUM filter, range, volume and dollar bars) with triple-barrier labels for deep models and
report that the CUSUM and triple-barrier combination performed best among their alternatives.
Event-based sampling and event-based labelling are two halves of one design, and the sampling
half is the part that microstructure researchers will recognise from Chapter 12.
    Meta-labelling separates the side of a bet from its size or its acceptance. A primary model
proposes trades, and a secondary classifier decides whether to take each one, usually by predicting
whether the primary signal will succeed. Meyer et al. (2022) organise the design space into
feature-driven and strategy-driven architectures and introduce inverse meta-labelling to improve
the side forecasts. Thumm et al. (2022) study ensembles for the meta-model and find them most
helpful when the data contain multiple regimes and are nonlinear; they point to position sizing as
a further application. The caution comes from Atluri (2026), who build a heavy pipeline of regime
features and a three-model ensemble on futures and find, on a held-out half-year, a meta-model
not separable from random, with a structural inversion of the direction-to-profit relation during a
drawdown. Their one favourable model could only be identified as best by looking at the held-out
set, which disqualifies it. A meta-label is a model of the primary strategy’s failure modes, and it
inherits the regime dependence of the primary signal.
    Regime dependence appears from a different angle in Valls and Steurer (2026), who study a
volatility-regime switching rule on three equity indices and report both substantial gains over
buy-and-hold and, on closer analysis, sensitivity to parameters and instability across sub-periods.
Hassan et al. (2010) propose monitoring a trading system as a “machine with variable outputs”
by statistical process control, which gives a production-side counterpart to these pre-deployment
tests: control charts can flag when live results leave their historical range. We read the two
together as a proposal that every regime filter should carry a monitor that tells the desk when
the filter’s own premise has failed. Regime detection itself is the subject of Chapter 7.
Desk note. Stage: signal and risk. Input: the event stream of a primary strategy (entry time,
side, realised path, cost) and a feature set that was available at entry. Output: a probability
that the trade will clear costs, used as a filter or a position-size multiplier in the manner of Meyer
et al. (2022). Checks: compare the filtered book with the unfiltered book net of costs, not on
AUC alone; require the AUC to be significant on data not used to choose the model, as Atluri
(2026) do; and chart the live acceptance rate against its historical range, in the spirit of Hassan
et al. (2010). What can go wrong: overlapping events inflate the effective sample, a regime
change can invert the relation the meta-model learned, and a filter that rejects most trades may
leave a book too small to cover its fixed costs.




                                                 98
14.3      Overfitting and multiple testing: the statistics of selection
The second half of the chapter belongs to the statistics silo and to practitioners who borrowed
from it. The common starting point is that a backtest is the winner of a search, and the winner
of a search is biased upward. Lopez De Prado (2013) states the proposition most plainly: high
simulated performance is easy to reach after trying a modest number of configurations, the more
configurations tried the higher the probability of overfitting, and, since researchers seldom report
the count of trials, investors cannot judge the degree. Bailey et al. (2014) and Bailey et al. (2015)
turn this into demonstrations, with online simulators that optimise a simple strategy over a
random walk so that the “optimal” variant is by construction overfit. These are teaching tools
that a risk committee can use as much as a research team can.
    Three formal answers follow. Bailey et al. (2013) estimate the probability of backtest
overfitting through combinatorially symmetric cross-validation, which splits the performance
matrix of tried strategies in all balanced ways and asks how often the in-sample winner ranks
poorly out of sample. Bailey and López De Prado (2019) deflate the Sharpe ratio for the number
of trials and for non-normal returns, and the earlier Bailey and López De Prado (2011) give the
probabilistic Sharpe ratio and the track-record length needed to reject a given threshold, with
non-normal moments. Witzany (2021) offer a Bayesian alternative using an MCMC posterior
over Sharpe ratios, and Koshiyama and Firoozye (2019) propose a covariance-penalty correction
that lowers a risk metric according to the number of parameters and the amount of data. Lopez
de Prado (2015) summarises the programme in the form of a practical demand: report the
number of trials and treat unadjusted backtests as exploratory.
    A parallel strand comes from the multiple-testing and selective-inference tradition. Harvey
and Liu (2019) give simple adjustments to Sharpe ratios and related statistics, suitable for real-
time evaluation of candidate strategies. Hansen (2005) proposes the test for superior predictive
ability, an improvement on the reality check for data snooping that compares a large set of
forecasts to a benchmark. Pav (2019) and Pav (2026) treat the same problem for the best-looking
asset: the first applies conditional inference to the asset with maximum sample Sharpe ratio
among correlated candidates, and the second compares shrinkage and debiasing estimators for
the true Sharpe ratio of the selected asset, finding James-Stein best in the simulations reported.
Chen (2022) gives a counterweight: using false-discovery-rate bounds, the author concludes that
most published cross-sectional predictability claims are likely true. We read this as a statement
about the prior odds of academic anomalies, not as comfort about a single team’s in-house
backtest, which was selected from a far larger search.
    A final group moves from correcting single numbers to auditing the whole protocol. Coqueret
(2023) advocate reporting results over many small variations of design choices, rather than a
single specification. Nikolopoulos (2026) test whole predictive workflows against synthetic null
environments and falsify those that generate significant walk-forward evidence from nothing.
Inglese (2026) treat the validation stack as an estimator whose size and power can be measured
with planted signals, and show that stacking two corrections against the same failure mode leaves
the false-positive rate unchanged and destroys power. Wiecki et al. (2016) supply real-world
evidence: across 888 strategies from one platform, reported backtest Sharpe ratios predicted
out-of-sample results poorly, while higher moments and portfolio-construction features carried
more information, and more backtesting by the author meant a wider gap. López De Prado
(2023) proposes a hierarchy of empirical evidence ranked by causal strength.




                                                 99
Desk note. Stage: validation, immediately before capital allocation. Input: the full matrix
of returns of every variant the team has tried for a research idea, together with a log of how
many labels, features, windows and parameters were explored (the counting step Lopez De Prado
(2013) asks for). Output: a gate with three numbers: a deflated or probabilistic Sharpe ratio, an
estimated probability of backtest overfitting, and the result of a placebo run on synthetic data, in
the manner of Nikolopoulos (2026). What can go wrong: trial counts are under-reported when
several people work on one idea; the placebo may not match the real data’s autocorrelation or fat
tails; and, as Inglese (2026) warn, adding redundant corrections costs power without removing a
new failure mode, so a stack should be justified failure mode by failure mode.


14.4      Evaluating the forecast itself
Before a forecast becomes a strategy it has to be judged as a forecast, and several papers show
that the judging is easier to get wrong than the forecasting. Diao (2026) give the sharpest
example. Five-minute gold-return models predicting a 288-bar forward return are ranked by
tiny error differences on overlapping windows; the overlap leaves about twenty independent
labels, so nominal test size overstates the independent size by orders of magnitude, and the
audited networks do no better than a zero predictor. The generic lesson is an effective-sample-size
correction: when labels overlap, the number of independent observations is nearer the sample
length divided by the label horizon than the number of rows. Bloch (2025) make a related
argument about estimation windows, showing that short look-back windows give mean, variance
and Sharpe estimates with too few effective observations, so that speed of adaptation is often a
statistical mirage.
    For directional forecasts there are tests designed for the decision at hand. Bürgi (2025)
proposes tests that penalise costly extreme misses and handle serial dependence in small samples,
and applies them to EUR/USD survey forecasts, where a simple time-series model is not beaten.
Sitthiyot and Holasut (2023) propose a joint test of directional skill across several variables,
which suits a multi-asset signal set judged as one decision. Ozimek (2026) compare strategies not
by one full-sample number but by modelling fold-level information ratios as a distribution that
depends on volatility and momentum regimes, with a GAMLSS model. Together these suggest
that a desk dashboard should show, for each signal, an effective sample size, a directional-skill
test and a regime-conditional distribution of fold results, and not only a Sharpe ratio.


14.5      Baselines, nulls and honest negative results
The most useful recent papers in this chapter report that something did not work, and report it
with enough protocol to be believed. Pandhe (2026) compare a gradient-boosted ranker with
a plain 12-1 momentum rule on the Nifty 500 under the same walk-forward and cost model;
momentum is numerically ahead and the Sharpe difference is statistically indistinguishable from
zero, with a rank correlation of predictions and outcomes near zero. Cliff and Rollins (2020)
show that some reported best-performing AI trading agents can be routinely out-performed by
extremely simple strategies involving no learning, and attribute this to minimal experimental
evaluation. Mesfin (2026b) test fourteen intraday signal families on Micro E-Mini Nasdaq futures
and find none that survives a fixed round-trip cost with a minimum t-statistic, trade count and
year-by-year consistency. Darmanin (2026) gate five retail signal families through multiplicity-
corrected, net-of-cost and bankroll-survival tests, with no family supported. Castellanos Macias
(2026) pre-register and audit a search over a very large space of configurations on crypto
perpetuals, and find that the past-performance correlation that held in-sample is near zero on
independent data and that a seemingly strong in-sample profit factor collapses below breakeven
when parameters are fixed a priori.


                                                100
    The same discipline is applied to newer model classes. Ye et al. (2026a) argue that headline
Sharpe ratios of LLM trading agents should not be treated as deployment evidence until they
pass tests for temporal integrity, frictions and counterfactual robustness, a theme taken up in
Chapter 15. Gort et al. (2022) formulate backtest-overfitting detection as a hypothesis test
for deep reinforcement-learning agents in crypto and reject the overfit ones before deployment.
One work, held as two records (Deep et al., 2025b,a), enforces information-set discipline and
rolling walk-forward testing for microstructure signals and honestly reports modest and statisti-
cally insignificant aggregate results with strong regime dependence. On the constructive side,
Mroziewicz and Ślepaczuk (2026) parameterise training and test window lengths in walk-forward
optimisation, use double out-of-sample data and run the final test once, and Sheppert (2026)
propose a composite objective, the GT-Score, that rewards consistency and penalises downside
and overfitting during optimisation. Letteri et al. (2022) go in another direction, proposing to
test candidate rules on future paths forecast by a neural network in addition to historical data;
we would treat this as a scenario generator to be validated with the tools above, and not as a
substitute for out-of-sample evidence.
Desk note. Stage: validation and forecast, as a standing gate for every new model. Input: the
candidate’s out-of-sample predictions and the predictions of three baselines: a zero or random-
walk forecast, a plain momentum rule as in Pandhe (2026), and a simple non-learning trader as
in Cliff and Rollins (2020). Output: a pass or fail on net-of-cost performance, on a significance
test of the Sharpe difference, and on the effective sample size of overlapping labels. What can
go wrong: baselines tuned less carefully than the candidate give a false pass; transaction-cost
assumptions that are too light turn a gross edge of a fraction of a point into a net loss, as Mesfin
(2026b) document for intraday futures; and a model that matches the baseline carries its own
operational cost, so equal performance should usually mean rejection.


14.6      Platforms, surveys and the research loop
The last group is the infrastructure and the map. Yang et al. (2020a) describe Qlib, a platform
that organises data, models and backtests for AI-driven quantitative research, and Yang et al.
(2026c) describe FinRL-X, a modular architecture in which backtest and broker execution share
a weight-centric interface so that research and live semantics are the same. Both address the
research-automation stage and indirectly the leakage problem, because one code path from data
to weights leaves fewer places for the backtest to differ from live trading. Shah et al. (2026a) and
Chen and Singh (2025) look at the market side: the first is a narrative review of how AI-driven
trading alters liquidity, volatility and price discovery, and the second combines a survey of
high-frequency professionals with case studies, reporting that risk management and data quality
drive algorithm choice. These two are peripheral to validation but carry a warning that matters
for it: if many agents learn from the same data, historical impact assumptions may not hold.
Barmish et al. (2024) offer a control-theoretic tutorial on order types, feedback, Kelly betting
and the limit order book, a vocabulary through which control engineers can enter the field.
    The surveys help locate this chapter in the literature. Horobet et al. (2024) map 4552
papers by science-mapping methods and note limited collaboration across scholars, which is the
silo problem of this atlas in bibliometric form. Cavalcante et al. (2016) review computational-
intelligence methods across financial applications, Dakalbab et al. (2024) systematically review
143 AI-in-trading papers and observe inconsistent estimation and performance metrics, Jukl
and Lansky (2025) follow PRISMA to review 208 algorithmic-trading papers, and Rahaman
(2026) give a graduate-level review of statistical methods. Ţiţan (2015) review the efficient
market hypothesis literature and conclude that efficiency is hard to test when conditions change,
a reminder that predictability may be transient. Finally, Li (2026) state five axioms for a
quantitative investment system, including that whatever predictability exists is slight, and argue


                                                101
that declared quantities such as a signal ceiling and an invariance defect bound what past states
can yield. Such a declaration is a formal version of the pre-registration that several empirical
papers above practise.


14.7     Bridges out of this chapter

Bridge. To time-series mining (Chapter 5) and return forecasting (Chapter 8). What
the classification community calls label noise and label correction (for example in Yang et al.,
2023) is the same problem that the forecasting chapters meet as target choice: a rank, a sign or
a horizon. Conversely, what return-forecasting calls an “out-of-sample R2 ” difference between
models is what this chapter’s statisticians treat as a hypothesis test requiring an effective sample
size (Diao, 2026; Hansen, 2005). We propose a shared checklist: every model paper states its
label, its label horizon and overlap, its baseline, and its number of trials.

Bridge. To volatility and derivatives (Chapter 11) and microstructure (Chapter 12).
A “Greeks-aware profitability label” (Vaghela, 2026) is, in derivatives language, a label built
from the position’s own scenario P&L; the same idea would translate to microstructure as a label
built from the realised cost of an execution schedule. What the label community calls an event
sampled by a CUSUM filter (Grądzki et al., 2025) is what microstructure researchers would
recognise as an information-driven clock, and the extreme-move targets of Zaman and Sahed
(2026) are what a volatility researcher would call a clustering regime. A natural use is to share
event definitions between the label pipeline and the execution model.

Bridge. To portfolio construction (Chapter 13) and strategies (Chapter 10). Meta-
labelling’s “side versus size” split (Meyer et al., 2022) is the portfolio chapter’s separation of an
alpha forecast from a sizing rule, and the finding that label gains may not survive conversion
to a portfolio (Lima, 2026) is the decision-focused learning argument in a different jargon:
train on the decision’s payoff, not on a proxy. In the other direction, the strategy chapter’s
many reported Sharpe ratios can be read as an unfiltered sample of winners; the deflation and
probability-of-overfitting tools here (Bailey and López De Prado, 2019; Bailey et al., 2013) are
how a reader or a fund converts them into evidence. Chapter 15 makes the matching request for
agent papers.




                                                102
Chapter 15

LLMs, Text and Agentic Research

Three communities share this chapter and rarely read one another. The natural-language-
processing and LLM community speaks of prompts, agents, tool use, memory, chain-of-thought,
fine-tuning, reinforcement fine-tuning and benchmarks. The quantitative-investment community,
which has produced most of the applied work here, speaks of alpha factors, information coefficients,
alpha decay and formulaic alphas. A third, smaller group comes from security and software
engineering and speaks of attack surfaces, poisoning, verification and reproducibility. Scattered
among them are finance-economics authors who test whether a language model can forecast
returns, and time-series people who ask how a numeric series should be fed to a model that reads
tokens.
    The translation into the shared vocabulary of the atlas is short. An “LLM agent” is a program
that proposes hypotheses, code or orders, calls tools such as a backtester, and reads the result. In
the atlas’s terms it is a search procedure over candidate features and signals, wrapped around a
validation oracle. “Alpha mining” is feature and signal generation under multiple testing. “Alpha
decay” is regime dependence and crowding. A “benchmark for trading agents” is a backtest
