protocol, so its failure modes are those of Chapter 14: look-ahead leakage, survivorship, data
snooping and costs ignored. “Sentiment” is a text-derived feature that must be aligned in time
with the price series it is meant to predict.
    In the pipeline (data → features → signal → regime and risk → portfolio → execution →
validation → research automation) this chapter touches every stage, but its centre of mass is at
the two ends. At the front it turns unstructured text into features. At the back it automates the
research loop itself, and then asks whether the automated loop can be trusted. The middle stages
(portfolio, execution, risk) are thinly covered and mostly appear as roles inside agent frameworks.


15.1      Text as data: from news to features
The oldest idea in the chapter is that text carries information that prices absorb with delay.
Lopez-Lira and Tang (2026) test this directly with post-knowledge-cutoff headlines. They report
that GPT-4 scores predict subsequent drift, especially for small stocks and negative news, and that
forecasting ability grows with model size. They also report that strategy returns decline as LLM
adoption rises, and they build a model with information-processing constraints, underreaction and
limits to arbitrage to rationalise this. For a desk the last finding matters most: a language-model
news signal is a crowding-sensitive signal with a visible half-life.
    Earlier and smaller-scale work establishes the same pattern with older tools. Kennis (2018)
relate Bitcoin news and online discourse to price and volume movements, and Burchi and
Regni (2026) compare BERTweet, RoBERTa and FinBERT on tweets from 25 large-cap stocks,
aggregate daily sentiment into trading signals, and compare strategies by performance rather
than classification accuracy. That shift from classifier metrics to trading metrics is the right one,
and it is where this literature meets the cost accounting of Chapter 10. Zhang et al. (2025d)


                                                103
follow a similar route with a business-oriented sentiment labelling scheme for trend prediction,
and Ntale (2026) compare five general and finance-specialised LLMs on technical market analysis.
Taken together these papers differ in the model used for scoring, not in the idea: a sentiment or
event score is computed per document, aggregated per asset and day, and lagged before use.
    A second group builds richer structure from text. Mitra et al. (2026) introduce NEIC, a
lightweight transformer that classifies Indian financial news into events, aimed at stock price
prediction with small models. This suits desks that cannot send every headline to a frontier
API. Li and Sanna Passino (2024) go further and use a fine-tuned LLM to generate a dynamic
knowledge graph, FinDKG, from financial news, with an attention-based graph network on top
for thematic trend detection. A natural use is to treat the graph’s evolving edges as a slow-moving
feature for sector rotation, and its entity links as a path for thinking about contagion, which
links to Chapter 7.
    Multimodal predictors combine text with numeric series. Du et al. (2026) combine an LLM
with transformer networks for multimodal stock movement prediction, and Wu et al. (2026b)
present TextGrad-Trader, which arbitrates among multimodal signals with attention to risk and
uncertainty. These sit at the border with the deep-learning forecasting literature of Chapter 8,
and the usual question applies: does the text channel add anything after the price channel has
been modelled? A paper that reports a gain over a text-free baseline answers that question, and
one that does not leaves it open.
    The reverse problem, getting numbers into a text model, receives less attention but has a
direct cost. Bhagat and Mudbari (2026) propose TSLN, a serialisation format using a schema-first
layout, relative timestamps and differential encoding. They report a 68–73% token reduction
against JSON on cryptocurrency, stock and IoT data while remaining lossless, and estimate
annual API savings above $18,000 for a million records a day. Whatever one thinks of that dollar
estimate, the idea is the familiar one from time-series compression, and it matters once an agent
reads price tables many thousand times a day.
Desk note. News-event feature with decay control. Stage: data and features. Input:
timestamped headlines and filings, with a vendor timestamp and an ingestion timestamp. Output:
a per-asset, per-day score (event class times model-scored polarity), lagged at least one bar and
neutralised against sector and size. A natural design, following the drift and crowding findings
above, is to monitor the score’s information coefficient by month and to cut gross exposure when
it decays. What can go wrong: the LLM may have seen the outcome during pre-training, so the
score must be tested on text after the model’s cutoff; small-cap, negative-news drift is expensive
to trade, so costs and borrow must be charged before judging the signal; and capacity shrinks as
more desks run the same model.


15.2     Zero-shot forecasting and LLM reasoning for trading
A different line uses the model itself as the forecaster. Chen and Pu (2026) deploy an LLM daily
on Russell 1000 stocks from April 2025, letting it search the web and synthesise a view without
curated text. Because predictions are collected at the live edge, they are free of look-ahead
by construction. They report that the model identifies top winners but not the rest of the
ranking. A desk should read that as a long-only tilt candidate, not a full cross-sectional ranker.
Cui et al. (2026) attack a related problem from the training side. Their Hindsight Preference
Optimization trains a language model for time-series advisory, where the quality of an answer
depends on an outcome unknown at prediction time. Xiao et al. (2025a) train Trading-R1 with
reinforcement learning to produce structured, interpretable investment reasoning. Luo et al.
(2026b) address a narrower and more verifiable task: translating a natural-language trading
intent into an executable option strategy over option-chain data, which links to Chapter 11.
    These papers share a theme with the formulaic work below: replace an opaque numeric
forecaster with a model whose output can be read by a human. They differ in what is trusted.

                                               104
In the live-nowcasting design, nothing is trusted but the timestamp. In the reinforcement-trained
designs the reward definition carries the whole burden, and reward hacking against a backtest is
the obvious failure.


15.3     Alpha mining with LLM agents
By volume this is the chapter’s core. The problem is old: search a combinatorial space of
formulas over price, volume and fundamentals for predictive factors, then keep those that survive.
Genetic programming and reinforcement learning were the incumbents. The LLM idea is to use
language-model priors as the proposal distribution, so the search is guided by economic meaning
rather than random mutation.

15.3.1    Single-loop generation and refinement
The simplest designs wrap a model in a generate–backtest–refine loop. Wang et al. (2025e) present
Alpha-GPT, in which a human steers an LLM through natural-language ideas, putting a human
inside the loop. Cao (2025) propose Chain-of-Alpha, using chained model calls for generation
and refinement. Li et al. (2024b) combine symbolic and neural elements in a neural-symbolic
factor mining agent aimed at interpretability, and Yu et al. (2026d) describe a hybrid approach
to formulaic alphas with LLM assistance. Vu et al. (2026) add a knowledge prior: a WriterAgent,
a JudgeAgent and a backtest engine in nested loops, seeded with the WorldQuant 101 formulaic
alphas and tested on Vietnamese stock data, with the stated aim of narrowing the search space
and reducing invalid code. Duan et al. (2025b) instead use multi-agent debate for interpretable
stock factors, and Weng et al. (2026) organise generation around an explicit market logic, the
mechanism meant to explain why a factor should persist.

15.3.2    Search structure: trees, evolution, schemas
A second family makes the search explicit rather than leaving it to the agent. Shi et al. (2025a)
combine an LLM with Monte Carlo tree search for formulaic factors. Luo et al. (2026a) use
the language model as a semantically informed mutation operator in an evolutionary search,
and Tang et al. (2026b) train an agent with reinforcement learning to improve how it evolves
alphas. Yi et al. (2026) argue that search needs an explicit space of trading semantics rather than
delegating navigation to the agent. Shi et al. (2026) combine LLMs with constraints for safe,
diverse and reproducible discovery, noting that unconstrained program generation is operationally
unsafe. Ye et al. (2026b) treat the evaluation budget as something to allocate, and keep a record
of how each candidate was produced. Neighbouring ideas appear in strategy-level evolution.
Sharma and Shroff (2026) evolve whole trading programs with an LLM as mutation operator,
and Yun et al. (2025) frame strategy discovery as multi-agent evolution aimed at preserving
diversity across market conditions.

15.3.3    Learning from the search history
A third family asks the agent to learn. Zhang et al. (2026c) update the generator’s policy by
reinforcement fine-tuning on evaluation feedback, and Huang et al. (2026a) align a model with
the literature for symbolic factor construction through policy learning. Wang et al. (2026g)
give an agent skills and experience memory against redundancy as the factor library grows, and
Yu et al. (2026e) store structured search-process memory while warning that naively reusing
past successes risks overfitting. Wang et al. (2026e) post-train local models, motivated by cost,
availability and confidentiality, and to avoid research path collapse onto a few mechanisms. Zhao
et al. (2026a) add multi-objective reinforcement learning for alphas that work well together, a
portfolio-aware objective.


                                               105
15.3.4    Discipline: decay, overfitting and testing
The most useful papers here treat the failure modes as design targets. Tang et al. (2025) observe
that LLM-driven mining tends to produce homogeneous factors that worsen crowding and decay,
and counter with originality enforcement via abstract-syntax-tree similarity against existing
alphas, plus hypothesis–factor alignment. Kim et al. (2026) point out that automated approaches
optimise directly for returns and rarely check whether a factor has an economic rationale, and add
empirical validation. Huang and Fan (2026) report a closed loop with out-of-sample validation
and economic-rationale requirements to mitigate data snooping, and quote a long-short Sharpe
ratio of 3.11 on U.S. equities. A skeptical reader will weigh that number against the number of
trials the loop ran, a point we return to in the validation section. Xia and Wang (2026) supply
theory: a hybrid of structured local search and LLM-generated non-local proposals helps only
under three geometric conditions, including residual signal alignment with the target, and novelty
alone does not raise yield. That is a useful guard against the assumption that more diverse
proposals mean more alpha.
    Other papers push mining toward fundamentals and whole workflows. Koa et al. (2026) use
self-evolving multi-agent symbolic discovery for financial valuation, Kou et al. (2025) describe
a risk-aware three-stage multi-agent system for strategy finding, and Li et al. (2025e) jointly
optimise data-centric factors and models in R&D-Agent-Quant. Guo et al. (2026a) study recursive
self-improvement across experiments with AQuA, and Yuan et al. (2026) carry the workflow
through cross-sectional signals toward portfolio construction in AlphaCrafter.
Desk note. Agentic factor factory with a human gate. Stage: signal generation and
research automation. Input: a panel of daily bars and fundamentals, a library of accepted
factors, and a point-in-time data layer. Output: candidate formulas with a stated hypothesis, a
similarity score to the library and out-of-sample statistics. A workable design combines ideas
above: AST-similarity and redundancy filters, a prior seeded by known formulaic alphas, and
a hard count of every candidate ever tested so a deflated Sharpe or multiple-testing correction
can be applied downstream (Chapter 14). A human approves anything that enters production.
What can go wrong: the trial count is lost when memory modules discard failures; the agent’s
pre-training exposes it to well-known factors, so “novel” alphas may be recycled; and in-sample
IC from a loop that re-reads its own evaluation is optimistic by construction.


15.4     Trading agents: roles, consensus and self-improvement
Alongside factor mining sits a literature that builds the whole desk out of agents. Xiao et al.
(2025b) present TradingAgents, a multi-agent framework with specialised roles including analysts,
researchers, a trader and risk management. Zhao et al. (2025a) apply a role-based multi-agent
design to stock selection and portfolio construction, evaluated against benchmarks under several
levels of risk tolerance. Miyazaki et al. (2026) argue that role prompts mimicking analyst and
manager are too abstract and propose fine-grained task decomposition, with a data-leakage
concern in view. Li et al. (2026a) diagnose “uniform trust”, the habit of treating all retrieved
sources as equally informative, and propose selective consensus to reduce decision uncertainty.
Chen et al. (2026) constrain self-improvement to a human-auditable rubric rather than free-form
prompt rewriting, and Mao et al. (2026a) use a verifier to guard strategy revisions against
hallucinated changes. Guo et al. (2026c) treat the evolution of market modes as a modelling
problem sitting between asset-centric and market-centric views, which relates to the regime
material in Chapter 7. Chavan (2025) come from the microstructure side and propose a high-
frequency architecture with three parallel pipelines (news LLM, order-book graph encoder with
reinforcement learning, technical strategy agent), which is worth reading for its time-based
scheduling and confidence-scaled capital allocation across pipelines rather than as evidence of
performance (its reported daily alpha comes from a backtest on NIFTY 200 minute data).

                                               106
    The system-level picture is supplied by two reviews. Hua et al. (2026) survey agentic
quantitative trading across factor mining, signal discovery, portfolio construction, execution
and risk management. Gong (2026) lay out an architecture for AI agents in financial markets
and examine its systemic implications. An engineering counterpoint is Ferrante (2026), whose
AgenTrade uses a language model only where the task is genuinely open-ended reasoning over
ambiguous input. That principle, selective use, also appears in the cost argument for compact
formats and small models earlier in the chapter, and is the most transferable design rule in this
section.
Desk note. LLM as analyst, not trader. Stage: signal and risk filter. Input: news, filings
and a structured summary of current positions. Output: a veto or a size-down flag on proposed
trades, plus a one-paragraph rationale logged for the risk team. Following the selective-use and
rubric ideas above, the model never sets size and never sends orders; deterministic code does.
What can go wrong: the model’s rationale reads as convincing but may not reflect what drove
the flag; a manipulated feed could trigger vetoes at the worst moment (see the security section);
and the filter’s value must be measured against a rule-based filter, not against no filter.


15.5      Attacks, contagion and reliability
Once agents read public feeds and talk to each other, they inherit an attack surface that numeric
models do not have. Na et al. (2026) decompose a trading pipeline into Analyst, Researcher,
Trader and Risk Manager roles and study how an adversarial signal entering through source data
and prompts propagates toward a decision and a realised loss. Sua et al. (2026) study black-box,
input-only attacks via admissible social-media feeds and how adversarial signals spread among
agents. Yan et al. (2025) propose TradeTrap to test whether trading agents remain reliable and
faithful under adversarial or faulty conditions. Wang and Saxena (2026) survey robustness and
security failures in published LLM trading schemes, and Mao et al. (2026b) widen the lens to
autonomous agents in commerce that hold and move digital assets. In the vocabulary of the
atlas these are input-validation, stress-testing and model-risk questions, and the language of
“contagion” here is the network contagion of Chapter 7 applied to message passing instead of
balance sheets.


15.6      Evaluating LLM agents without fooling ourselves
The evaluation papers are where this chapter converges with classical backtesting. The core
threat is look-ahead via memory: a model trained on data covering the backtest window can recall
prices and narratives rather than reason. Zhu et al. (2026c) address this with a data-side masking
protocol that anonymises tickers and dates, and a Barra-style attribution that separates market,
style and selection returns. Cai et al. (2026) provide an auditable point-in-time framework
in response to look-ahead leakage, optimistic execution and risk mandates that are described
but not enforced. Roy and Roy (2026) attack contamination at the signal level, combining
membership-inference features with cross-model disagreement that exploits different training
cutoffs, as a filter that costs nothing at retraining. That filter could serve as a standard validation
check on any LLM-derived signal.
    Benchmarks differ in what they stress. Chen et al. (2025b) and Yu et al. (2025) evaluate
agents on real-world or live markets, Qu and Chen (2026) propose a closed-loop, cost-aware
benchmark that diagnoses process as well as returns, Zhang et al. (2026j) stress the behavioural
instability of agents across repeated runs, and Luo et al. (2026c) extend to options with nonlinear
payoffs, linking to Chapter 11. On the code side, Khoroshilov et al. (2026) benchmark whether
LLMs write executable strategies, and Wang et al. (2026d) test whether the generated code
behaves as the described strategy, since a run can plot a clean backtest while the risk logic is


                                                 107
silently wrong. Hall et al. (2026) verify numeric claims drawn from filings, which is a data-quality
gate for any text-to-number pipeline.
    The sceptical evidence is the most informative. Li et al. (2026b) backtest LLM timing
strategies over two decades and 100+ symbols and report that previously reported advantages
deteriorate under the broader cross-section and longer period, with strategies too conservative
in bull markets and too aggressive in bear markets. Barton et al. (2026) report a six-month
production record from two fleets, finding among other things that the operating layer, such as a
risk slider, shapes behaviour more than strategy text and that sizing is blind to volatility. Both
are measurements of what agents do, which is a different object from what papers claim they
can do.
Desk note. Leakage audit for LLM signals. Stage: validation. Input: the signal history
from any LLM-based feature, the model’s documented training cutoff, and a second model with
a different cutoff. Output: a pass/fail flag per signal period, based on whether the signal’s
predictive power is concentrated before the cutoff or disagrees across models, in the spirit of
membership-inference and cross-model checks. Pair it with masked-identifier replays and factor
attribution. What can go wrong: cutoffs are only approximately known; masking removes
information the real desk would have; and passing the audit does not remove ordinary overfitting
or cost problems.


15.7     Research automation as infrastructure
The last stage of the pipeline is the research process itself. Agent Team et al. (2026) present
Mint-Agent, finance-native agentic foundation models built to be reliable on grounded evidence
and sustain auditable long-horizon research. Li et al. (2026c) replace free-form messages between
mining and evaluation agents with structured, replayable transport, a contract that makes a
hand-off verifiable. Zhang et al. (2026i) constrain agent self-evolution with finite state machines,
from the general deep-research literature, and Zimmer et al. (2026) offer a practical guide to
AI-assisted research in mathematics and machine learning. The last two are peripheral to trading
but share a message with everything above: the useful agent is the one whose steps are logged,
bounded and replayable.


15.8     Bridges out of this chapter

Bridge. Alpha mining and the multiple-testing literature (Chapter 14). The LLM
community says an agent “explores” a “search space” and “accumulates experience”. The
backtesting and statistics community says the researcher runs many trials and the best one is
biased upward. These are the same fact. Every memory module, tree search and evolutionary
loop above should report its number of trials so that selection bias can be corrected. Conversely,
statisticians can supply the stopping rule and deflation that agent builders are missing.

Bridge. Factor libraries and the factors chapter (Chapter 9). What the agents call
“redundancy” or “research path collapse” is what the factor-zoo literature calls spanning and
crowding, and what the similarity-search community calls near-duplicate retrieval. AST similarity
and orthogonality tests for a candidate formula are the same operation as asking whether a new
anomaly adds anything beyond existing factors. Economists can supply the economic-rationale
criterion that several papers here try to enforce by prompt.




                                                108
Bridge. Contagion and regimes (Chapter 7). Security researchers speak of an adversarial
signal “propagating” through agent roles. Network and systemic-risk researchers speak of shock
transmission through links. A knowledge graph of entities from news and a graph of agent
messages are both networks on which one can ask who is exposed. In the other direction, change-
point and regime detectors from that chapter could serve as an automatic circuit breaker for
agents whose behaviour was tuned in one regime, which is the failure the long-horizon evaluations
describe.

Bridge. Numeric series as tokens (Chapter 6). The serialisation and “token efficiency”
work treats a price series as text to be compressed. The representation-learning community
treats it as a sequence to be embedded. The two meet where an LLM reads a series: whether a
lossless differential encoding or a learned embedding gives the model what it needs is an open
question that neither side currently tests against the other.




                                              109
Chapter 16

General Methods and Cross-Domain
Tools

Four communities speak in this chapter, and none of them is a finance community. Statisti-
cians talk about outliers, change points, bandwidth-free inference and misspecified Bayesian
updating. Deep-learning researchers talk about noisy labels, sample weighting, domain-invariant
representations, flow-matching and tabular foundation models. Time-series-mining people talk
about pattern mining, symbolic regression and instance spaces. Language-model and agent
researchers talk about harnesses, skills, evolutionary coding agents and verifiable data. A few
papers come from further away: remote sensing, survey design, microservice operations, quantum
benchmarking and the mathematics of rough paths.
    The atlas needs a common vocabulary to make these usable. A statistician’s outlier is a
bad tick or a stress print, that is, an anomaly in the shared concept index. A change point
or distribution shift is a regime shift. A noisy label is a flaw in label design: the triple-barrier
or forward-return target never equals the quantity one wished to predict. Hyperparameter-free
inference and Bayesian updating under misspecification are two ways of asking how much a
performance number can be trusted, which is uncertainty quantification and forecast evaluation.
Symbolic regression and evolutionary coding agents are two routes to alpha mining. An agent
harness is the research-automation layer.
    Within the pipeline of data, features, signal, regime and risk, portfolio, execution, validation
and research automation, this chapter sits at the two ends and in the middle. It supplies data
hygiene and feature selection at the front, regime detectors and generic forecasters in the middle,
and validation and automation at the back. Most papers were written for other applications,
and the chapter treats them as tools to be tested. A tool is only as good as the assumptions it
makes about the data, so for each group we state what the assumption is and whether markets
are likely to honour it.


16.1     Cleaning the inputs: anomalies, noisy labels and depen-
         dence
Every desk has bad data. Li et al. (2020) propose COPOD, an outlier detector that builds
an empirical copula and reads off tail probabilities, producing something like an anomalous
p-value for each record. It has no parameters to tune and is interpretable, which suits an
unattended data-quality gate on trades and quotes. Its limit is that a genuine stress event
is also a tail event, so such a gate should flag and route to review, not delete. Kim and
Hwang (2025) address a neighbouring problem, directional dependence in sparse multivariate
longitudinal data contaminated by measurement error, using functional principal components
and copula imputation. Their setting is not market data, but irregular, noisy panels are common


                                                110
in alternative data, and the idea of imputing through a copula, not by carrying forward, is worth
testing.
    A second kind of noise lives in the targets. Several deep-learning and statistics papers treat
noisy labels as a first-class problem. Kim et al. (2021) detect noisy samples with a noise-cleansing
method, and Shu et al. (2022) learn a class-aware weighting of training samples, replacing
hand-set schemes. Hu et al. (2025) argue the opposite of the usual intuition for imbalanced data:
adding suitable label noise can adjust biased decision boundaries. The theory side is van Rooyen
et al. (2015), who revisit learning under symmetric label noise and show why an unhinged loss
remains useful where convex potentials can fail. A natural use is the following proposal. Labels
such as “next-day direction” or “barrier hit first” are noisy by construction, and event labels
are imbalanced, so sample weights and noise-robust losses are candidate tools for label design.
None of these papers tests market labels, and a desk must check that the noise model (often
class-conditional and independent of features) resembles the way trading labels go wrong, namely
through overlapping horizons and regime-dependent noise.
    Representation and selection come next. McInnes et al. (2018) give UMAP, a manifold learner
that serves both visualisation and general dimension reduction; it could be used to map the state
space of a feature set and spot clusters of days that behave alike. Lu and Osterrieder (2022)
select features with an intervened interpolative decomposition that gives each column its own
priority, and apply it to diversifying quantitative strategies; it is one of the few papers here with
a trading application in its title. Nakagawa et al. (2016) prune the search for predictive patterns
safely, so a superset of useful patterns is found without enumerating them all, which is relevant
wherever candidate rules are combinatorial.
    Several peripheral papers show how far outside finance the data stage can reach. Biswas
et al. (2026) show that off-the-shelf biomedical encoders give high cosine similarity to unrelated
concept pairs, a warning for any pipeline that links assets or news through embedding similarity.
Buehler (2024) turn a corpus of papers into an ontological knowledge graph and analyse its
degrees and communities, which is a template for a graph over the research library itself. A et al.
(2026) estimate material outflows from satellite imagery and city master plans, an example of
the alternative-data pattern in which an imagery signal is converted into a physical quantity
that might later be mapped to a sector. Schuster et al. (2024) survey best-worst scaling as a
prioritisation tool; it could be used to rank candidate research ideas by pairwise judgement. Du
et al. (2025) synthesise verifiable data for language-model training, a reminder that synthetic
data is only valuable when something can verify it (see Chapter 3).
Desk note. Data-quality gate (data stage). Input: tick, quote and bar streams plus
vendor corporate-action tables. Output: a flag and a tail p-value per record, produced by a
COPOD-style detector (Li et al., 2020) run separately per instrument and per time-of-day bucket.
Records above a flag threshold are quarantined and shown to a human, not dropped. What
can go wrong: stress periods are legitimately extreme, so a fixed threshold removes the very
observations a risk model needs; auction prints and halts need their own calendars; and a detector
refitted on contaminated history learns the contamination.


16.2      Detecting change: change points and regime shifts
The largest cluster in the chapter is change-point detection, and it is the clearest case of a
statistics idea that a trading desk already half-owns. The common model is that a series is
piecewise stationary and the task is to say when the generating parameters jump. Habibi (2022)
and Huang (2025) apply Bayesian online change-point detection (BOCPD) to financial series. The
latter builds four simulated scenarios (mean drift, variance drift, joint drift and a t-distributed
mean drift) and compares BOCPD against generalised-likelihood and Kolmogorov–Smirnov tests.
Musaev et al. (2024) survey adaptive algorithms for non-stationary, chaotic financial series,


                                                111
arguing that stationary-model methods are ill-suited. These three share a theme: the online
setting, where a run-length posterior is updated bar by bar, is the one that matters for trading,
because an offline segmentation sees the future.
    The remaining papers widen the data type. Sundararajan and Pourahmadi (2018) test
multivariate piecewise stationary series nonparametrically through differences in spectral density
matrices, which makes the method sensitive to changes in dependence structure and not only in
level. Chen and Weichi (2025) model collective anomalies and change points jointly in fine-grained
series with a recursive online algorithm, a distinction that matters because a burst of ten abnormal
bars is not a regime change. Luo et al. (2026d) treat online change detection for multivariate
inhomogeneous Poisson point processes with low-rank intensities; trades and quote updates are
point processes, so this is a candidate detector for shifts in order-arrival intensity (Chapter 12).
Pham et al. (2024) use multivariate BOCPD for root-cause analysis of microservice failures. Our
proposal is to point the same method at the research infrastructure itself: feed latency, fill rate
and data-vendor lag are multivariate series in which a change point is an incident.
    Detection is only half of a regime system, because something must act on it. Li et al. (2025d)
cluster markets into ten volatility states with k-means and evaluate equal-weight, minimum-
variance, maximum-diversification and equal-risk-contribution allocations under those states.
The theory papers say why such switching is hard. Zhao et al. (2019) analyse learning invariant
representations for domain adaptation, and an invariant representation across regimes is what
a strategy would want, but their analysis concerns when invariance alone suffices. Kalan et al.
(2025) study Neyman–Pearson classification when both the null and alternative distributions
shift, which maps naturally onto a detector whose false-alarm rate must be capped while the
world changes.
Desk note. Regime and risk filter (regime stage). Input: daily realised volatility,
cross-asset correlation and trade-arrival counts. Output: an online change-point posterior from a
BOCPD variant (Habibi, 2022; Huang, 2025) that scales down gross exposure when the probability
of a recent break exceeds a limit set from a false-alarm budget in the Neyman–Pearson spirit of
Kalan et al. (2025). What can go wrong: detection delay means the filter fires after the loss;
hazard-rate and prior choices change alarm timing and must be tuned on a window the strategy
research never touched; and turnover from flickering states can cost more than the drawdown
avoided.


16.3      Generic forecasters: tabular foundation models, trees and
          interpretable rules
The forecast stage in this chapter is populated by models built for general tabular data. Jäger
et al. (2026) introduce TabPFN-3.5, a tabular foundation model that reports state-of-the-art
results on TabArena and extends to non-i.i.d. data with temporal or grouped splits. Cho et al.
(2026) present Causilo, which reports a performance-efficiency Pareto frontier with fast inference,
and Zhang et al. (2026f) present LimiX-2, a structured-data model built around contextual
mechanism networks and masked pretraining. The shared idea is in-context learning: the model
is not fitted per task but conditions on a labelled table at inference. For a desk, a natural use is
as a fast baseline for cross-sectional return or fill-probability prediction. The traps are specific to
finance. Benchmark tables are close to i.i.d., markets are not; pretraining corpora may contain
the evaluation period; and in-context rows are a leakage route if they include future information.
Chapter 8 shows how return forecasters are judged.
    Tree ensembles remain the usual comparison. Loecher et al. (2026) study hierarchical
shrinkage, a post-hoc regularisation that shrinks node predictions toward ancestral means, and
contrast it with the implicit regularisation of random forests. Low-signal financial data punishes
variance, so shrinkage is a cheap regulariser to test. For interpretability, Lumadjeng et al. (2026)


                                                 112
start from a classifier written as a single equation and simplify it step by step into readable
rules for financial decision making, motivated by the idea that a model that cannot be explained
cannot be deployed in a regulated domain. Rezaei and Krishnan (2026) use an LLM to infer
mechanisms from model residuals on tabular data, which could serve as a hypothesis generator
for why a forecaster fails in particular regimes.
    Other forecasting papers are placed here for their idea, not for market evidence. Hwang and
Zohren (2025) fuse feature extraction and decision making in a signature-informed transformer
for asset allocation, arguing that separating forecasting from optimisation creates a mismatch
(Chapter 13). Zheng et al. (2023) add classifier-free guidance to flow-matching models for
generation and decision making; conditional generation is a possible engine for scenario simulation.
Li et al. (2024a) study when diffusion models generalise rather than memorise, which is exactly
the question for a synthetic market generator. Ma et al. (2027) reason over temporal knowledge
graphs to predict future events and Xu et al. (2026b) distil hypergraph networks into cheaper
students; both are candidates for event and relation graphs between firms, though neither is
evaluated on them.


16.4      Discovering formulas: symbolic regression and alpha mining
Symbolic regression searches for a short closed-form expression instead of a black-box fit. That
is attractive in trading because a formula can be audited, costed and monitored. Cranmer (2023)
describe PySR, a library built on a distributed evolutionary search; Cranmer et al. (2020) distil
symbolic representations from graph neural networks by encouraging sparse latent representations
and then applying symbolic regression. d’Ascoli et al. (2022) train transformers to infer the
recurrence behind integer or float sequences, and Saegert and Köthe (2026) identify a simplification
bottleneck in amortised neural symbolic regression, which is the fast-but-unscalable alternative
to genetic programming. Basiri and Nicholson (2026) generate basis functions automatically so
that sparse identification of dynamics does not depend on the researcher guessing the library in
advance.
    In the shared vocabulary this is alpha mining, and the warning from Chapter 14 applies
in full: a search over expression space is a search over a huge number of hypotheses. Each
paper here assumes noise-free or lightly noisy data from physics or mathematics, where a correct
formula exists. Prices have no such formula, so a symbolic regressor will find an expression either
way. The correct use is to treat any discovered formula as one candidate in a family whose
size is recorded and corrected for. Nezhad et al. (2025) and Liu and Li (2026) come from LLM
reasoning: the first generates verifiable code in place of prose to make mathematical reasoning
checkable, the second proposes an algebraic semantics for traceable reasoning. Both support a
design rule for automated research, that every generated signal should arrive as executable code
with a deterministic check.
Desk note. Formula-search lane (research automation and signal). Input: a feature
panel, a library of operators, and a fixed cost model. Output: candidate expressions from a
PySR-style search (Cranmer, 2023), each stored with its complexity, its search budget and its
out-of-sample record. What can go wrong: the number of trials is the multiple-testing count
and must be logged; expressions that look simple can hide high turnover; and formulas fit in one
regime may fail silently in the next, so each should pass the regime check above before capital is
assigned.


16.5      Validation: inference, attribution and testing the testers
The statistics papers in the validation stage ask how sure we can be of a number. Zhang and
Shao (2026b) test hypotheses on a functional parameter with self-normalisation, and Zhang


                                                113
and Shao (2026a) give a sample-splitting route to bandwidth-free inference. Both exist because
classical block bootstraps and HAC estimators depend on a bandwidth whose choice can change
conclusions, and serially dependent returns make that sensitivity a daily problem for Sharpe-ratio
comparisons. Shalizi (2009) analyse Bayesian updating with dependent data and misspecified
models; the lesson for a desk is that a posterior over strategy quality can be confidently wrong
when the model is wrong. Kotłowski et al. (2024) give an online algorithm for optimising complex
performance metrics such as F-measure that are functions of a confusion matrix, which matters
when a signal’s value is in rare, high-precision calls.
    Attribution has its own pitfalls. Biparva and Materassi (2024) reweight Shapley values
with a maximum-entropy approach so that information about variable ordering and causal
structure enters the attribution. Méloux et al. (2025) warn, through the dead-salmon story, that
interpretability analyses can find structure that is not there. A proposal: any feature-importance
table in a research note should be accompanied by a null run on shuffled or random inputs.
    A different question is whether a backtest suite covers enough kinds of market. Instance Space
Analysis represents test instances as feature vectors and visualises where algorithms succeed.
Smith-Miles and Muñoz (2023) give the methodology and tools, Muñoz et al. (2021) apply it
to regression problems, and Güzel et al. (2025) provide the Python package. A natural use is
to characterise each backtest window (volatility, trend, liquidity) as a feature vector, then map
which strategies win where and whether the test set was diverse or merely long. Koch et al.
(2026) do similar benchmarking for quantum optimisation on combinatorial problems, a niche
relevant to portfolio selection only if quantum hardware becomes practical. Najarro et al. (2025)
solve inverse problems in stochastic self-organising systems with invariant representations, a
pattern that could recover simulator parameters for a synthetic-market model from observed
statistics.
Desk note. Validation check (validation stage). Input: out-of-sample daily P&L of a
candidate and of its siblings in the same search. Output: a confidence interval for the Sharpe
difference from a bandwidth-free test in the style of Zhang and Shao (2026b) or Zhang and Shao
(2026a), plus an Instance-Space map of the windows where the strategy wins (Smith-Miles and
Muñoz, 2023). What can go wrong: asymptotic tests need long samples and daily strategies may
have few independent episodes; the map is only as informative as the window features chosen;
and passing the check says nothing about capacity or costs.


16.6     Automating the research loop
The last group is about agents. Novikov et al. (2025) present AlphaEvolve, an evolutionary
coding agent in which LLMs propose code changes that are scored and selected, applied to
open scientific problems and infrastructure optimisation. Replace the scientific objective with
a backtest objective and it becomes an alpha-mining loop, with the overfitting hazards above
made worse by an automated, tireless proposer. Zhang et al. (2025c) survey the combination of
metaheuristics with machine learning, giving the vocabulary for hyperparameter and portfolio
search. Foster et al. (2026) build preference models that predict which candidate experiments are
worth their GPU cost, an idea that carries over directly to ranking trading ideas before spending
compute on full backtests.
    Several papers concern the scaffolding around agents. Seong et al. (2026) study building agent
harnesses, Badhe et al. (2026) manage long-horizon agent skills with explicit state in place of an
ever-growing transcript, Liang et al. (2026) propose creating, evaluating and connecting reusable
skills, and Zhu et al. (2026b) propose a training-free multi-agent framework for recursive self-
improvement through memory. For a semi-automated fund the interest is operational: repeatable
research routines should be stored as versioned skills with tests, and every automated run should
leave an audit trail. Terwiesch et al. (2023) find that LLMs generate product ideas that score
well, yet the question of diversity remains; for idea generation, a pool of LLM-proposed alphas

                                               114
may be less diverse than it looks, which would inflate apparent breadth. Chapter 15 treats these
agents in the finance setting.
    At the mathematical edge, Hairer (2014) gives regularity structures, an algebraic framework
for describing irregular functions through local expansions, used to make sense of singular
stochastic equations. We cannot claim a desk use; it is the theoretical sibling of path-signature
features (see Hwang and Zohren, 2025) and of rough-volatility modelling (Chapter 11).


16.7     Bridges out of this chapter

Bridge. Change points ↔ regimes ↔ discords. What this chapter’s statisticians call
a “change point” (Huang, 2025; Sundararajan and Pourahmadi, 2018) is the regime shift of
Chapter 7, and what time-series mining in Chapter 5 calls a “discord” is a subsequence that fails
to match any other. A discord is a candidate change point, but a change point persists, whereas
a discord can be a single event. Joint detection of both, as in Chen and Weichi (2025), is the
bridge. Order-flow researchers speak of an “intensity regime”, which Luo et al. (2026d) would
call a change in a Poisson intensity matrix (Chapter 12).

Bridge. Label noise ↔ label design. Deep-learning researchers (Kim et al., 2021; Shu et al.,
2022; van Rooyen et al., 2015) speak of corrupted labels and class-conditional noise; backtesting
researchers in Chapter 14 speak of overlapping labels, triple-barrier targets and purging. These
are two views of one fact, that the training target is a noisy proxy. The first group offers
estimators for the noise, the second offers the temporal structure of the noise, and neither has
used the other. Representation people in Chapter 6 meet the same issue as “what the encoder
should be invariant to” (Zhao et al., 2019).

Bridge. Symbolic regression ↔ alpha mining ↔ multiple testing. Where the physics-
inspired community says “discover the governing equation” (Basiri and Nicholson, 2026; Cranmer,
2023), the factor literature of Chapter 9 says “data-mined anomaly” and the strategy literature
of Chapter 10 says “rule search”. The same expression-space search sits under all three names,
and the validation papers above (Zhang and Shao, 2026b; Shalizi, 2009) are the vocabulary for
pricing the search. In the other direction, the LLM agents of Chapter 15 are search operators for
this space, and Novikov et al. (2025) is the closest template.




                                              115
 Part VII

Synthesis




    116
Chapter 17

Bridges Across Silos

17.1      Why bridges, and how to read this chapter
The topical chapters of this atlas were written one silo at a time. Their closing notes keep making
the same observation: a concept that one community studies under one name is being studied by
another under a different name, and, as far as the abstracts show, neither refers to the other.
This chapter collects those observations and turns the strongest of them into proposals. Every
bridge below has the same four parts: the communities and the mathematical object they share;
what each side already holds, with citations; a research programme that exists only once the two
sides are combined; and the stage of a semi-automated fund where it would land, the data it
needs, and the way it most likely fails.
    Two cautions apply throughout. First, the silo and pipeline-stage labels quoted in this chapter
come from an automated classification of titles and abstracts, with at most three concept tags per
paper. A count of zero papers carrying two tags therefore means that no paper was tagged with
both, not that no such paper exists; we use such zeros as pointers, and they were the starting
point for each bridge, not the end of an argument. Second, every research programme is our
proposal. Where a cited paper supplies a component, we say which component; we do not claim
that any cited paper has tested the combination.
    Concept counts give the selection rule. We looked for concepts that are studied by three or
more silos, and for concepts that are concentrated in one silo while being obviously useful to
another. In the collection of 1065 papers, motif is tagged on 38 papers, 33 of them in time-series
mining; implied volatility on 40, of which 30 are in the quantitative-derivatives silo; order flow on
63, of which 41 are in microstructure; path signature on 23, of which 17 are in mathematics; and
conformal on only 5, four of them in statistics. Meanwhile regime shift (145 papers) and forecast
evaluation (123) are spread over four or more silos each. The first group produces bridges that
carry a tool across a wall; the second produces bridges that unify vocabulary. Table 17.1 lists all
twelve, and the silo-by-stage matrix of Table 2.1 (Chapter 2) shows where the walls are.

17.2     B1. Motifs and discords on implied-volatility paths
Communities and shared object. Time-series mining and quantitative derivatives. The
intraday implied-volatility surface, or a handful of its coordinates (at-the-money level at several
maturities, a skew measure, a butterfly), is a multivariate series. The mining community calls its
recurring stretches motifs and its never-repeating stretches discords; the derivatives community


                                               118
calls them smile regimes and surface dislocations. In the automated tagging no paper carries
both an implied-volatility tag and a motif or similarity-search tag, although 38 papers carry the
motif tag, 50 the similarity-search tag and 40 the implied-volatility tag.

What each side has. The mining side has exact all-pairs similarity joins (Yeh et al., 2016b),
variable-length motif discovery that removes the need to guess a window (Linardi et al., 2018),
joint motif and discord discovery across lengths (Linardi et al., 2020a), multidimensional motifs
restricted to informative dimensions (Yeh et al., 2017b), and admissible search with missing
data (Zhu et al., 2021). Two papers already apply the machinery to financial series (Cartwright
et al., 2021; Rodrigues et al., 2019). The derivatives side has descriptive work on smile and
term-structure patterns in an emerging market (Shaikh and Padhi, 2014), evidence that implied
volatility is largely explained by its own past (Wen et al., 2024) and by past returns with
power-law weights (Guyon and Lekeufack, 2023), a diffusion generator of dynamic surfaces (Han
et al., 2026), and a warning that the shape of at-the-money skew is hard to reconcile with
rough-volatility models (Abi Jaber and Li, 2025).

Our proposal. Compute a multidimensional matrix profile over rolling five-minute vectors of
surface coordinates for an index option chain. Two hypotheses follow. H1: bars whose nearest
neighbour in the surface history is unusually far (discords) precede realised-volatility jumps
or dealer repricing by more than the level of at-the-money volatility alone predicts. H2: the
next-interval change in term-structure slope, conditional on the motif cluster of the current
window, has a sign that survives costs. The control is the linear path-dependent model of Guyon
and Lekeufack (2023): the question is whether shape adds anything to a linear lag model.

Landing, data, failure. Stage: features, then risk. Data: timestamped option quotes mapped
to a fixed moneyness–maturity grid. Main failure: interpolation artefacts and stale quotes create
motifs that are properties of the grid; searching several lengths multiplies trials, so significance
must be assessed as Rodrigues et al. (2019) stress.


17.3     B2. Signatures of order-flow paths
Communities and shared object. Rough-path mathematics and market microstructure. An
order-placement and cancellation stream is, to the microstructure economist, a sequence of events
with signed sizes; to the rough-path theorist it is a multidimensional path whose signature collects
its iterated integrals. Depth-two terms record ordered co-movement, such as whether imbalance
at one level preceded a trade, which is the question microstructure asks. Of 23 path-signature
papers, 17 are in the mathematics silo and one is tagged with order flow.

What each side has. The mathematics side has the feature construction for streams (Lyons
et al., 2014), lead–lag embeddings for sequential learning (Fermanian, 2019) and a recent primer
covering truncation choice (Chevyrev and Kormilitzin, 2026). Two papers meet microstructure
directly: Gyurkó et al. (2014) extract information from the signature of order placement and
cancellation streams, and Sidogi et al. (2023) add truncated signatures of limit-order-book paths
to short-horizon price prediction and compare them with autoregression on prediction error. The
microstructure side has deep models of price formation from the book (Sirignano and Cont, 2018)
and interpretable neural models of price–trade dynamics (Naviglio and Lillo, 2026), which supply
benchmarks.

Our proposal. Define the path in event time as cumulative signed volume, mid-price, depth
imbalance at the first few levels and an event counter; take depth-two and depth-three signatures
over a rolling window of events; predict the sign of the mid-price move over the next few seconds

                                                119
net of half the spread. H: signature terms add predictive power beyond order-flow imbalance and
lagged returns, and beat a feature set of equal dimension built from random projections, which
removes the trivial explanation that more features fit better.

Landing, data, failure. Stage: features, feeding signal and execution timing. Data: level-two
or level-three tick data with exchange timestamps. Main failure: dimension grows quickly with
depth, timestamp jitter changes the path, and the reported gain in Sidogi et al. (2023) is a
prediction-error gain, not a net P&L result.


17.4      B3. Conformal intervals feeding position sizing
Communities and shared object. Distribution-free statistics, decision theory and the deep-
learning forecasters. All three produce, or consume, a number meaning how much to trust a
forecast: a conformal interval, a Kelly fraction, an abstention flag. Only five papers carry the
conformal tag, four in statistics, and none is also tagged with portfolio optimisation.

What each side has. Statistics offers conformalised quantile forecasts on a panel of stock
characteristics with near-marginal coverage but undercoverage in high idiosyncratic-volatility
and market-regime cells (Taheri Hosseinkhani, 2026), multivariate conformal ellipsoids (Zhang,
2026), a loss that trades set size against conditional coverage (Bach, 2025), and cost-sensitive
conformal abstention for rare costly classes, benchmarked on high-stakes tabular decisions such
as credit and fraud (Singh et al., 2026). A language-model paper abstains when evidence is sparse
(Torres et al., 2026). Decision-side tools include sizing that does not assume Brownian dynamics
(Barmish and Proskurnikov, 2025), robustness to corrupted features at decision time (Caunhye
et al., 2026) and confidence bands for frontier quantities (Bodnar et al., 2009); Hutchison et al.
(2004) give the older case for calibrating classifier probabilities before acting on them.

Our proposal. Scale each position by the lower end of a conformal interval for the signal’s next-
period return, set to zero when the interval straddles zero, with regime-conditional calibration
cells because Taheri Hosseinkhani (2026) show that pooled calibration fails exactly in the states
where risk is taken. H: at equal turnover, interval-gated sizing lowers drawdown and raises net
Sharpe against sizing by the point forecast.

Landing, data, failure. Stage: portfolio and risk. Data: several years of out-of-sample forecast
residuals per signal. Main failure: exchangeability is false for autocorrelated, non-stationary
returns, so coverage is only approximate; wide intervals produce no trades at all.


17.5      B4. Change-point detectors as regime gates for factor
          sleeves
Communities and shared object. Statisticians, time-series miners and factor economists.
A detected break is a regime label; the factor economist wants to know when a premium has
decayed or a crowded sleeve is about to crash. No paper in the collection is tagged with both
change-point and factor-model.

What each side has. Detection: online Bayesian change-point methods applied to financial
series (Habibi, 2022; Huang, 2025), multivariate detection of covariance-structure breaks (Sun-
dararajan and Pourahmadi, 2018), precision-matrix change points in heavy-tailed series (Cribben,
2019), a particle filter producing a live crisis probability (Plümer, 2026), and online matrix-profile
segmentation (Gharghabi et al., 2017). Factors: risk of momentum is predictable and managing


                                                120
it nearly doubles its Sharpe ratio (Barroso and Santa-Clara, 2015); data mining and crowding
inflate expectations of factor premia (Arnott et al., 2019), and passive inflows compress the
realised returns of long–short anomalies (Deng and Sammon, 2026); an alpha half-life shortens
with AI adoption (Meng and Chen, 2026a). Regime-conditioned allocators exist (Upreti, 2026;
Pan et al., 2026), and Boettiger and Hastings (2012) observe that early-warning indicators rarely
report their error rates.

Our proposal. Run Bayesian online change-point detection on each sleeve’s return and on the
cross-factor covariance, and scale exposure by one minus the posterior probability of a recent
break, with the threshold fixed by a false-alarm budget in flags per year. H: gating cuts drawdown
at no more cost in net Sharpe than the volatility scaling of Barroso and Santa-Clara (2015).
That benchmark is the honest one, since a break detector may only rediscover volatility.

Landing, data, failure. Stage: regime, feeding portfolio. Data: twenty or more years of daily
factor returns including several crashes. Main failure: detection delay exceeds the length of the
damage, a lag already noted in the factor chapter’s crowding monitor.


17.6     B5. Alpha-mining agents under trial accounting
Communities and shared object. Language-model agent builders, selection-bias statisticians
and backtesting practitioners. An agent that “explores a search space” and “accumulates
experience” is a researcher running many trials, and the best of them is biased upward. The
object is the set of candidate formulas evaluated, including those discarded. Of 28 alpha-mining
papers in the language-model silo, only 6 also carry a multiple-testing or backtest-overfitting tag.

What each side has. Agents: structured search memory that records edit motifs (Yu et al.,
2026e), regularised exploration against alpha decay (Tang et al., 2025), a typed operator language
in a sandbox with family-aware selection (Shi et al., 2026), out-of-sample validation and economic-
rationale requirements against data snooping (Huang and Fan, 2026), and closed-loop research
systems (Guo et al., 2026a); Xia and Wang (2026) give a geometric account of why a search
saturates when hypotheses become redundant. Statistics and practice: the deflated Sharpe ratio
(Bailey and López De Prado, 2019), the probability of backtest overfitting (Bailey et al., 2013),
adjustment for the number of strategies tried (Harvey and Liu, 2019), a false-discovery bound
for cross-sectional predictors (Chen, 2022), a falsification audit that runs a whole workflow on
zero-predictability synthetic data (Nikolopoulos, 2026), and Monte Carlo measurement of how
reliable a validation protocol is (Inglese, 2026).

Our proposal. Three requirements on any mining agent in the fund. (i) Log every candidate
evaluated, accepted or not, and estimate the effective number of independent trials from the
correlation of candidate return series. (ii) Report the selected factor only through a deflated
statistic using that number. (iii) Run the entire agent loop on placebo data in the manner
of Nikolopoulos (2026) and record how often it returns a significant alpha. H: the placebo
false-discovery rate of an unconstrained agent is far from the nominal level and falls when
trial-aware stopping is imposed.

Landing, data, failure. Stage: validation and automation. Data: the agent’s full evaluation
log, plus synthetic nulls. Main failure: LLM-proposed candidates are strongly correlated, so
the effective trial count is hard to estimate, and a model that has read the sample period leaks
information that no deflation formula sees.




                                               121
17.7      B6. Generators as test benches for execution agents
Communities and shared object. Generative-model researchers, microstructure economists
and reinforcement-learning (RL) execution researchers. The shared object is a simulated order
book that reproduces the stylised facts and, crucially, reacts to the agent’s own orders. In the
tagging, 35 papers concern synthetic data and 50 execution, yet none carries both tags.

What each side has. Generators: flow-matching generation of controllable book trajectories
(Wang et al., 2026a), a state-event foundation model for counterfactual order-flow trajectories
(Zhang et al., 2026e), diffusion-based book simulation (Berti et al., 2025), a learned world agent
that needs no agent calibration (Coletta et al., 2022), and the open agent-based simulator
(Byrd et al., 2020). Impact checks: synthetic order flow built from superposed metaorders with
square-root impact, used to support a theory linking impact, order imbalance and volatility
(Maitrier et al., 2025). Execution agents: liquidation with closing auctions trained on synthetic
rough-Heston prices (Graf and Mastrolia, 2026), a twin-critic actor for selling large programmes
(Zaznov et al., 2026), flow-matching imitation for high-frequency quoting (Li et al., 2025b), and
RL trained on adversarial synthetic data for robustness (Xia et al., 2026). Cliff and Rollins (2020)
warn that agents with no intelligence can beat published AI traders when methods are loose.

Our proposal. An execution gauntlet. Train each execution agent on one generator; evaluate
implementation shortfall against TWAP and VWAP on two other generators and on a reactive
replay of real data; admit a generator only if it passes the impact and sign-memory checks. H:
agent rankings on non-reactive replay differ from rankings on reactive generators, and agents
that win in their own training simulator lose their edge when transferred.

Landing, data, failure. Stage: execution and validation. Data: level-two history for calibra-
tion and held-out replay. Main failure: co-adaptation, where the agent learns generator artefacts;
cross-generator transfer is the guard.


17.8      B7. Contagion measures as cross-sectional risk features
Communities and shared object. Systemic-risk economists, graph-learning researchers
and statisticians. The shared object is a time-varying dependence network over assets. The
economists measure spillover on it; the deep-learning silo learns it end to end to rank stocks;
the statisticians detect when it breaks. No paper carries both a network-contagion tag and a
portfolio-optimisation tag.

What each side has. Directional volatility spillover measures (Diebold and Yilmaz, 2012);
clustering of stocks by spillover profile (Cerqueti et al., 2023); change-point analysis of cross-
market network statistics (Banerjee and Guhathakurta, 2020); graphical-lasso change points in
cross-asset precision matrices (Cribben, 2019). On the learning side, attention-based dynamic
graphs for asset pricing (Uddin et al., 2021) and a spatio-temporal graph network for stock
ranking (Yao et al., 2026); tensor-based forecasts of future stock distances as a tipping-point
indicator (Cherifi et al., 2017). From microstructure, a co-crash network of high-frequency flash
events (Turiel and Aste, 2022), a measured branching ratio of a liquidation cascade (Seuma,
2026), and a loop-gain matrix whose spectral radius exceeds what scalar monitoring shows (Woo,
2026).

Our proposal. For each stock and day compute in- and out-strength in a directional spillover
network and its centrality in a learned graph. Use them (a) as predictors of next-month left-tail
loss, controlling for beta and own volatility, and (b) as inputs to a covariance estimator, testing

                                                122
whether a learned-graph covariance predicts next-quarter covariance better than a shrunk sample
estimate. H: network exposure explains tail contribution beyond market beta.

Landing, data, failure. Stage: risk, then portfolio. Data: daily returns for a broad universe,
plus intraday data for the high-frequency variant. Main failure: spillover estimates are window-
dependent and may proxy for beta; most learned-graph papers are scored on ranking, not on
risk.


17.9      B8. Geometric stress indices with detection metrics
Communities and shared object. Geometers (persistent homology, path signatures), change-
point statisticians and financial economists. The object is a shape statistic of a rolling window
whose level shift marks a break. Geometric papers mostly show case-study plots; the statisticians
supply detection delay and false-alarm rates.

What each side has. Geometry: persistence-landscape norms rising near crashes (Gidea and
Katz, 2017), persistence norms above a threshold during extreme events (Rai et al., 2024), a
signature fragility index correlated with VIX (Mounir, 2025), an online signature-kernel two-
sample test for regimes (Issa and Horvath, 2023), signature-based regime clustering (Bilokon
et al., 2021), and persistent homology inside a Markov-switching filter reported to flag onset
earlier than classical filters (Faris, 2026). Statistics: Bayesian change-point detection compared
with classical tests on simulated drifts (Huang, 2025), Neyman–Pearson classification that caps
false alarms (Kalan et al., 2025), and the observation that early-warning indicators seldom report
error rates (Boettiger and Hastings, 2012).

Our proposal. A common bake-off. Compute the signature index, persistence norm, topological
switching filter, signature-kernel test, Bayesian change-point probability and a plain realised-
volatility threshold on the same dates; define the event as a drawdown beyond a stated size
within twenty days; compare detection delay at a fixed alarm budget in flags per year, then
compare the net result of a de-risking rule. H: at matched false-alarm rates, geometric indices
lead plain volatility by days, not by zero.

Landing, data, failure. Stage: regime and risk. Data: multi-decade index and sector returns.
Main failure: few independent crises, so any ranking is fragile, and thresholds tuned on the same
crises used for evaluation.


17.10      B9. Self-excitation and roughness
Communities and shared object. Point-process microstructure, derivatives and statistics. A
Hawkes kernel whose norm is close to one produces long memory in order flow; a rough-volatility
model has a small Hurst exponent and power-law memory in variance. Theory links the two, but
empirical work stays on opposite sides. Only five papers carry the Hawkes tag and none is also
tagged with implied volatility.

What each side has. Hawkes-based microstructural foundations for rough Heston-type models
(Wang et al., 2026f); limit theorems for nearly unstable Hawkes processes, motivated by fits that
sit near the stability boundary (Jaisson and Rosenbaum, 2015); a survey by task (Bacry et al.,
2015); an endogeneity ratio from Hawkes fits to index futures as a flash-crash warning (Filimonov
and Sornette, 2012); and a Hawkes–order-flow-imbalance engine backtested on NIFTY50 stocks
(Mishra, 2026). On the volatility side, realised volatility is rough across asset classes while the


                                               123
option-implied measure identifies roughness only in some cases (Mouti, 2026), and rough models
struggle with the global shape of SPX smiles (Abi Jaber and Li, 2025).

Our proposal. Per stock-day, estimate a branching ratio from trade arrivals, a roughness
exponent from intraday realised variance, and, where options exist, the steepness of the short-
maturity skew. H1: a branching ratio near criticality predicts next-day realised-variance spikes
beyond what lagged realised variance predicts. H2: the cross-sectional ordering of branching
ratios predicts the ordering of roughness estimates, which would link two literatures empirically.
The change-point machinery of Luo et al. (2026d) can flag when the intensity matrix itself shifts.

Landing, data, failure. Stage: risk, with theory as a by-product. Data: trade-level timestamps
and option chains for the same names. Main failure: near-critical fits can be artefacts of intraday
seasonality or of clock choice, and kernel estimates are noisy for illiquid names.


17.11      B10. Labels that carry cost and size
Communities and shared object. Label engineers in time-series learning, noisy-label the-
orists in deep learning and statistics, and derivatives practitioners. The shared object is the
training target as a noisy proxy for what the fund earns. The backtesting chapter treats triple-
barrier and rank labels; the noisy-label theorists treat corrupted classes; in the papers we read,
the two literatures do not refer to each other.

What each side has. Labels: information-driven bars with triple-barrier labelling (Grądzki
et al., 2025); evidence that the labelling algorithm changes financial performance (Kovačević
et al., 2022); a warning that a good label score need not mean good portfolio returns (Lima,
2026); separation of side and size in meta-labelling (Meyer et al., 2022); ordinal and rank targets
that raise accuracy and portfolio returns in cross-sectional stock selection (Cakici and Zaremba,
2026; Guida and Coqueret, 2026); and a label built from repriced option P&L with theta, vega
and delta-scaled costs (Vaghela, 2026). Noise theory: sample selection for noisy labels (Kim
et al., 2021), class-aware reweighting (Shu et al., 2022) and a convex loss robust to symmetric
label noise (van Rooyen et al., 2015).

Our proposal. Compare four label families on one model and one dataset: direction, triple-
barrier, rank and cost-aware (realised after-cost P&L of a stated execution or hedging rule).
Train each with and without noise-robust losses, with sample weights that account for label
overlap. H: the ranking of label families by classification score differs from the ranking by net
P&L, and the cost-aware label wins after costs on the option-trading and execution tasks.

Landing, data, failure. Stage: signal and validation. Data: any tick or minute dataset with
a cost model. Main failure: a cost-aware label bakes in one execution assumption, and overlap
between labels shrinks the effective sample.


17.12      B11. Chart patterns as testable motifs
Communities and shared object. Practitioners of chart analysis, time-series miners and
multiple-testing statisticians. A named pattern is a template plus an implicit similarity rule; a
motif is a template plus an explicit distance threshold; a rule zoo is a hypothesis family.




                                               124
What each side has. Miners: image-similarity retrieval of candlestick patterns in futures
(Jang et al., 2026), functional motifs of bubble surges (Cremona et al., 2023), and chart-image
networks that learn price patterns largely distinct from standard trend signals (Jiang et al., 2021).
Practitioners: a flag-pattern rule with filtering (Arévalo et al., 2017) and a backtest of intraday
chart patterns (Montes-Gómez et al., 2024). Testers: stepwise error-rate control over tens of
thousands of rules (Chuang et al., 2024), a superior-predictive-ability test over thousands of rules
in dozens of markets (Rink, 2023), a pre-registered test with held-out data (Brown, 2026), and a
three-gate test of significance, cost and survival (Darmanin, 2026).

Our proposal. A pattern registry. Each pattern, hand-drawn or discovered, is registered before
testing with its length, normalisation, distance threshold and holding rule. All entries, including
motifs found by variable-length search, enter one family-wise or false-discovery correction and
one cost model. H: after correction, learned and discovered patterns retain an edge only in a
small subset of markets and horizons, and discovered motifs do no better than hand patterns
once the search is charged.

Landing, data, failure. Stage: signal and validation. Data: minute bars across many markets.
Main failure: forking paths in normalisation and threshold choice, which are themselves a search.


17.13      B12. Own-impact in decision-focused learning
Communities and shared object. Decision-focused learning in operations research and
portfolio theory, and the microstructure literature on market impact. When the fund trades size,
its orders change the data it will learn from. No paper tagged with decision-focused learning is
also tagged with execution or transaction costs, and only one with market impact.

What each side has. Decision side: training a model aware of decisions that bias its own
observations (Cristian et al., 2025); a proof that predictive accuracy does not imply control under
feedback and competition (Bajpai, 2025b); robust losses (Schutte et al., 2024); generalisation
bounds for predict-then-optimise (Balghiti et al., 2023); and robustness to corrupted features
(Caunhye et al., 2026). Microstructure side: synthetic order flow that reproduces square-root
impact (Maitrier et al., 2025), price impact of inelastic passive demand on anomaly returns
(Deng and Sammon, 2026), and learned liquidation schedules (Graf and Mastrolia, 2026).

Our proposal. Train return forecasts through an allocator whose decision layer includes a
concave impact-cost term in trade size, evaluated on a reactive simulator of the kind in B6. H: at
small size the impact-aware and mean-squared-error-trained forecasts perform alike, but beyond
a size threshold the impact-aware version has lower turnover and higher net Sharpe; the crossing
size is an empirical estimate of capacity.

Landing, data, failure. Stage: portfolio and execution. Data: signal history and a calibrated
impact model. Main failure: impact parameters are noisy and a mis-specified curve is trained
into the forecasts.


17.14      Bridges the collection cannot yet build
Table 17.2 lists cells where the literature in this collection is empty or nearly so. Each row is
a place where a bridge would be useful but no source exists on one side. Counts come from
the automated tagging and stage labels and mean only that the collection, not the literature,
is silent. In the silo-stage matrix of Table 2.1, the quantitative-derivatives silo has no paper in


                                                125
the features, portfolio, execution or automation stages; the language-model silo has none in the
regime or execution stages; financial economics has none in execution; and time-series mining has
one. Only 76 of 1065 papers carry an execution stage, and 39 of those are from microstructure.

    These gaps are partly an artefact of how the collection was assembled, and partly the usual
one: communities do not read each other. The next two chapters take the bridges in different
directions. Chapter 18 is organised by the ten stages of a working fund rather than by bridge,
but several bridges have natural homes there: B10 (cost-aware labels) in signal generation,
B3 and B4 (calibrated sizing, regime gates) in the risk stage, B6 and B12 (simulators, own
impact) in execution and portfolio construction, and B5 (trial accounting) in validation and
research automation. Chapter 19 restates B3, B4, B5, B6, B10 and B12 as open problems for
mathematicians and machine-learning researchers, and uses the thin cells of Table 2.1 to steer
future additions to the collection. The empty cells of Table 17.2 remain open; neither later
chapter fills them.




                                              126
Chapter 18

Blueprint of a Semi-Automated
Systematic Fund

The fourteen topical chapters of this atlas are organised by the vocabulary of the research
communities that wrote the papers. A fund is organised differently: it is a pipeline in which data
become signals, signals become positions, positions become orders, and every stage is watched.
This chapter re-reads the collection along that pipeline. It describes the architecture of a realistic
small-to-mid semi-automated systematic fund, one with a handful of researchers, one or two
engineers, a person who owns risk and operations, and a portfolio manager who signs off on
capital. Its ten operational stages are cut differently from the eleven classification stages of
Chapter 2: data and features map to Stages 1 and 2, signal and forecast to Stage 3, portfolio
to Stage 4, regime and risk to Stage 5, execution to Stage 6, validation to Stages 7 and 8, and
automation to Stage 9; governance (Stage 10) has no classification counterpart, and theory has
no operational stage. For each stage we say which chapters and papers supply components,
where a human must stay in the loop, and, most importantly, what a real fund needs that the
collected literature does not provide.
    Two conventions apply throughout. First, statements attributed to papers are limited to what
their abstracts or the earlier chapters report. Second, everything labelled practitioner guidance
or our synthesis is ours: it is standard industry practice or our own proposal, it is not drawn
from the collection, and it should be read as a starting point for a fund’s own design and not as
a tested recipe. The silo and stage labels used when we count papers come from an automated
classification of titles and abstracts, and are approximate.


18.1      The architecture at a glance
Table 18.1 gives the whole chapter in one place: for each stage, its main components in the atlas
(with chapters), the human gate, and the gap. The stages are numbered as in the sections that
follow. The vertical arrows of a real system (a monitoring alert that halts execution, a validation
failure that sends a signal back to research) are described in the text of the stages concerned.
