18.2       Stage 1: data and reference data
Components
The microstructure chapter supplies the building blocks of the tape-level layer. Trade-sign
classification is not a solved problem: Lee and Ready (1991) document timing problems with
quote-based classification, Chakrabarty et al. (2012) find the conventional rules more accurate
than bulk volume classification, and Bessembinder (2003) show that the trade–quote time
adjustment changes estimated execution costs. We read these together as a requirement that
signed-flow series carry a data-quality metric. Intraday seasonality in volume and spreads on
the NSE (Krishnan and Mishra, 2013) means that features must be conditioned on the time of
day before they enter a model. For outlier screening, the general-methods chapter offers the
copula-based detector of Li et al. (2020), and the time-series chapter offers discord scores in the
matrix-profile family (Yeh et al., 2024), which can flag a window that is unlike anything seen
before.
    Reference data of a different kind come from the market-structure chapter. Exchange rule
changes (lot sizes, expiry days, auction sessions) are structural breaks in the data. Gunnam et al.
(2024) review regulatory changes in derivatives trading, Khatwani (2026) evaluate the closing
auction session, and Hussain and Olekar (2026) use a regulator-mandated expiry change as a
falsification test of a volatility claim. A regulatory calendar belongs in the reference-data layer
next to the holiday calendar.
    Point-in-time discipline has surprisingly few papers behind it. Cai et al. (2026) build an
auditable point-in-time evaluation for portfolio agents, and Darmanin (2026) use point-in-time
index membership and delisting corrections in cross-sectional tests; Castellanos Macias (2026)
state survivorship as an explicit ceiling on their evidence. Synthetic data enter at this stage as
augmentation and as a sandbox: the controlled environments of Sun et al. (2026), the simulation
harness of Galas (2014) and the order-flow generator of Berti et al. (2025), together with the
large-tick simulator of Noble et al. (2026), are the nearest things to a standard test bench
(Chapter 3).



                                                         128
Human in the loop
A person approves each new vendor feed, reviews the quarantine queue, and signs off on every
change to the exchange-calendar table. Automated outlier gates should quarantine and never
silently delete, because the extreme observations that a gate flags are sometimes the stress
episodes a risk model most needs. A person decides which they are.

Gaps
Practitioner guidance. The collection says almost nothing about the following, all of which cause
real losses. (i) Corporate actions and identifiers. Splits, dividends, mergers, symbol changes and
contract rolls must be applied from a reference table that is versioned, so that a price series can
be reconstructed as it looked on any past date. (ii) As-of storage. Store both the event time and
the time the fund learned of it, so that restated fundamentals and late vendor corrections do not
leak into past backtests. (iii) Cross-vendor reconciliation. Compare two sources daily and alert
on disagreement above a tolerance. (iv) Feed reliability. Treat the market-data connection as
a production service: heartbeat checks, sequence-gap detection, a documented fallback, and a
rule for what the trading system does when data are stale (the safe default is to stop opening
new risk). (v) Clock discipline. Timestamp every record at ingestion with a synchronised clock,
because the latency and leakage problems that the microstructure chapter describes reappear
in the fund’s own plumbing. In the atlas, the data stage holds 122 records by our automated
classification, and a reading of the data-stage chapters suggests that most concern similarity
search, series generation or cleaning, not the operation of a data pipeline.


18.3      Stage 2: research environment and feature store
Components
Yang et al. (2020a) describe Qlib, an AI-oriented platform that organises data, models and
backtests, and Yang et al. (2026c) describe FinRL-X, in which backtest and broker execution
share a weight-centric interface; the second is the closest the collection comes to the principle
that research and live code should be the same code (Chapter 14). For feature construction, the
time-series chapter offers feature banks such as the descriptor library of Fulcher and Jones (2017)
and fractional differencing (Hosking, 1981), and the representation chapter offers signatures,
topological summaries, learned embeddings and pretrained forecasters as candidate features
(Chapter 6). The recurring warning in those chapters is that any transform fitted on the full
history, whether a decomposition, an encoder or a clustering, leaks the future, so each must be
refitted inside each fold.

Human in the loop
A reviewer approves every feature definition before it can be used in production, with a short
note on its economic rationale and its information timestamp. The review is about meaning and
timing, not about performance; a feature is rejected if nobody can say when its value becomes
known.

Gaps
Practitioner guidance. A feature store for a small fund needs: immutable, versioned feature
definitions; point-in-time joins that can reproduce the feature matrix as of any past date; lineage
from raw data to model input; a rule that research and production read features through the
same code path; and a cost accounting of compute, since retraining frequency is often limited by
compute and not by accuracy. None of the platform papers in the collection addresses lineage,


                                               129
versioning or the cost of recomputation at scale. A second gap is the shared feature registry
across sleeves: two researchers who each build a volatility feature produce two numbers that
differ in a decimal place, and the portfolio then trades a disagreement.


18.4      Stage 3: signal and alpha generation
Components
Three families of signal feed the fund, and the atlas supplies different amounts of evidence for
each.
    Classical factors and rules are the best-documented. Time-series momentum has a known
failure mode, and Barroso and Santa-Clara (2015) show that the risk of momentum is predictable
and that managing it nearly doubles the Sharpe ratio while virtually eliminating crashes. Kurth
et al. (2026) document the demise of short-term trend following and its dependence on signal
speed and asset class. The collection of formulaic alphas of Kakushadze (2016) is a standard
library of cross-sectional price–volume expressions, and Arnott et al. (2019) list three blunders of
factor investing, starting with expectations inflated by data mining, crowding and optimistic
cost assumptions (Chapter 9). Volatility and option-derived signals are covered in Chapter 11:
implied volatility is largely past-dependent (Wen et al., 2024), so a feature built from option data
may repackage what returns already contain, and Duarte and Jones (2008) estimate a volatility
risk premium that rises with market volatility.
    ML forecasters are the largest group (126 records in the return-forecasting chapter alone).
The most useful of them for a fund are about the target and the benchmark. Guida and Coqueret
(2026) show that the label alone changes long–short decile performance and explains more
out-of-sample variation than the model; Cakici and Zaremba (2026) reach a compatible result
across 35 equity markets; and graph overlays in the style of Liu et al. (2026a) or Yao et al. (2026)
are candidate residual corrections to a tabular baseline. On the sceptical side, Pandhe (2026)
find a gradient-boosted ranker not distinguishable from plain 12-1 momentum on the Nifty 500
after costs, and Bandi et al. (2026) argue that the zero forecast is too weak a benchmark in
stock-return forecasting.
    Pattern and regime features come from Chapters 5, 6 and 7. Online change-point detectors
(Habibi, 2022; Huang, 2025), particle-filter regime detection (Plümer, 2026), and discord scores
(Yeh et al., 2024) are used as conditioning variables or filters, seldom as stand-alone alphas.
Microstructure signals (order-flow imbalance, participant mix) enter as short-horizon filters on
slower strategies (Chapter 12).

Human in the loop
A person approves the hypothesis before a test is run: what the signal is, why it should exist, its
intended horizon, and which market participant is on the other side. This is a defence against
the multiple-testing problem rather than a bureaucratic step, because a hypothesis registered in
advance fixes the number of trials. Humans also decide when a signal sits in the wrong family: a
regime detector promoted to an alpha, or a risk filter promoted to a return source.

Gaps
Practitioner guidance. (i) Cost-aware labels. Chapter 14 documents that classification gains
may not survive conversion to a portfolio (Lima, 2026); Vaghela (2026) label option bars by
repriced profit and loss with theta, vega and costs inside the target. These are exceptions. We
recommend that every label be built from the intended instrument and execution assumption:
label by net-of-cost outcome of the trade that would actually be placed, with the fund’s own cost
curve, and report the gross label as a diagnostic. (ii) Signal half-life and horizon matching. Each


                                                130
signal needs a measured information-coefficient decay profile by horizon, so that holding period,
rebalance frequency and execution urgency are chosen from it. (iii) Per-signal capacity. See the
next stage and the monitoring stage; capacity should be a field in the signal’s registry entry
from the first day. (iv) Signal correlation. A candidate signal should be judged by its marginal
contribution to the existing book, not stand-alone, which the synergy-oriented mining papers
argue (Yu et al., 2023).


18.5     Stage 4: combination and portfolio construction
Components
Chapter 13 supplies most of the layer. Covariance hygiene starts from the classical random-matrix
finding that empirical correlation matrices are mostly noise (Laloux et al., 1999; Plerou et al.,
2002); OWL shrinkage (Hiraki and Sun, 2022) gives similar weights to correlated stocks. Boyd
et al. (2024) describe a Markowitz extension with transaction costs, leverage limits and parameter
uncertainty that remains a convex problem, and Boyd et al. (2017) give the multi-period version
that executes only the first planned trade. Sizing rules are reviewed in the same chapter, with
Kelly-type rules revisited by Stiffelman (2026). For training forecasts through the allocator,
decision-focused learning is surveyed and benchmarked by Mandi et al. (2024), and Bajpai
(2025b) prove, in a stylised model, that high predictive power need not imply profit once convex
execution costs and feedback are present. Signal gating by predictability state (Farmer et al.,
2018; Lin et al., 2021) and regime-conditional allocators (Upreti, 2026) sit between forecasting
and sizing.

Human in the loop
The portfolio manager owns the objective and the constraint set: the risk-aversion and cost-
aversion parameters, the exposure and concentration limits, the turnover budget, and the allowed
instruments. The optimiser proposes, and a person reviews the proposed trade list against a few
sanity rules (gross and net change, largest single order, share of daily volume) before the first
live rebalance of any new configuration. After that review the process can run unattended inside
limits.

Gaps
Practitioner guidance. (i) Combining signals. The collection has little on how to weight many
weak signals with unstable correlations. A sound default is a simple, shrunk combination (equal-
risk weights over signals orthogonalised to the existing book), with weights updated slowly. (ii)
Capital allocation across sleeves. Allocating between a daily equity sleeve and an intraday options
sleeve depends on capacity, drawdown tolerance and correlation in stress, none of which the
portfolio papers address at the fund level. (iii) Turnover budgets. Make turnover an explicit
constraint tied to the cost curve of the execution stage, not a penalty tuned on the backtest. (iv)
Financing, borrow and margin. Short-borrow availability and margin requirements are constraints
of the optimiser and rarely appear in the research papers; for options they dominate.


18.6     Stage 5: risk
Components
The risk layer is the stage where the atlas is most helpful, because many silos converge on it.
Regime gates: Musaev et al. (2024), Habibi (2022) and Huang (2025) offer change-point detectors
for financial series, and Plümer (2026) a real-time regime detector calibrated to the distributional


                                                131
shape of stress. Volatility targeting: the risk-managed momentum result of Barroso and Santa-
Clara (2015) is the template, with a volatility forecast maintained as a shared service as described
in Chapter 11, in which GARCH-type baselines are challenged under a superior-predictive-ability
test (Reinhard Hansen and Lunde, 2003). Drawdown control: Mendes and Lavrado (2017)
implement and test Maximum Drawdown at Risk, and Harding et al. (2003) discuss the strengths
and weaknesses of drawdown as a risk statistic. Uncertainty-aware sizing: Sanderink (2026)
separate a slow decision about whether to trade from a fast sizing rule and report that naive
inverse-uncertainty sizing hurts because uncertainty and signal strength are correlated; Taheri
Hosseinkhani (2026) find conformal intervals undercover in high idiosyncratic-volatility and
regime cells; Hutchison et al. (2004) give the case for calibrating a classifier before turning
probabilities into sizes. Tail and structural risk: the flash-crash taxonomy of Ng (2026) and the
dealer-gamma caveat of Sahu (2026) mark how far vendor-quoted levels can be trusted.

Human in the loop
Limits are set by people and enforced by code. The risk owner sets gross, net, per-name,
per-sleeve, loss and liquidity limits, and decides the response to a regime flag (scale down, flatten,
escalate). Regime gates should be able to reduce risk automatically but should require a person
to restore it, because the cost of a false alarm is asymmetric to the cost of a false all-clear.

Gaps
Practitioner guidance. (i) Margin and gap stress. Run stress scenarios against the exchange
margin schedule and overnight gaps (the first-bar gap effect documented in Zielkowski, 2026
is a reminder that stops do not protect against gaps). (ii) Liquidity-adjusted limits. Express
positions in days-to-liquidate at a stated participation rate. (iii) Concentration of gates. If
several sleeves share one regime detector, one false alarm de-risks the whole fund at once, and
the de-risking itself costs spread and impact. (iv) Short-option risk. A short-volatility book
needs limits on short-gamma exposure and on the loss from a strike-level gap at expiry, with
settlement mechanics modelled explicitly.


18.7      Stage 6: execution
Components
Chapter 12 is the supply. Impact theory explains why large orders are costly: order splitting and
liquidity replenishment are jointly needed to reproduce the square-root exponent (Zhou et al.,
2026a). For schedules, Rastegar and Eghbalreihani (2019) fit an I-star impact function to split a
large order, and Lehalle and Neuman (2019) add a Markovian signal to the transient-impact
framework so that alpha decay and impact decay can be traded off. Barzykin et al. (2026) treat
optimal execution with passive impact, and Maciejewski (2026) propose a model-free passive
execution method. After the trade, Markov (2019) compare broker algorithms on implementation
shortfall with hierarchical pooling, and Zovko (2026) point out that real-time slippage detection
needs hundreds of fills to separate impact from volatility. Learned executors such as Zhang et al.
(2023b) are evaluated in simulators, which is why the simulator of Noble et al. (2026) and the
closing-auction work of Graf and Mastrolia (2026) matter for a desk that trades the close.

Human in the loop
A trader or risk owner reviews the cost model each quarter against realised slippage, picks brokers
and algorithms from the TCA ranking, and holds the manual override: the ability to pause,
cancel or flatten with one action, which must work even when the research stack is down. Large


                                                132
or unusual orders (above a threshold share of volume, in a thin contract, near an expiry) require
approval before release.

Gaps
Practitioner guidance. (i) Order state machine and reconciliation. Orders, acknowledgements,
partial fills and cancels must be tracked in a state machine, and positions reconciled against
broker and clearing records at least daily and after every restart. (ii) Pre-trade checks. Fat-finger,
price-collar, maximum-order-size, rate-limit and duplicate-order checks must sit between the
strategy and the venue. (iii) Multi-leg option execution. The collection treats execution mostly
for equities and futures; spreads and multi-leg options require leg-risk handling and expiry-day
rules. (iv) Cost-model feedback. The impact curve fitted from the fund’s own fills feeds the
portfolio stage and the capacity estimate, closing the loop that the TCA papers leave open.
Capacity itself is not estimated by any paper that we found; Kurth et al. (2026) test capacity
constraints as an explanation of trend-following’s decline, which is a use of the word and not an
estimation method.


18.8      Stage 7: validation and promotion gates
Components
This is the stage where the collection is strongest, and where Chapter 14 should be read in full.
The gate has five layers. Splits: walk-forward evaluation with purging and embargo around
overlapping labels (standard practice; the effective-sample-size problem is shown vividly by Diao,
2026). Selection correction: the number of trials must be counted (Lopez De Prado, 2013); the
probability of backtest overfitting (Bailey et al., 2013), the deflated Sharpe ratio (Bailey and
López De Prado, 2019) and the probabilistic Sharpe ratio (Bailey and López De Prado, 2011)
turn the count into a number; the test for superior predictive ability (Hansen, 2005) compares
a family of forecasts with a benchmark. Placebo and null environments: Nikolopoulos (2026)
falsify workflows that produce significant walk-forward results from nothing, and Inglese (2026)
show that stacking redundant corrections costs power. Baselines: Pandhe (2026) and Cliff and
Rollins (2020) are models of the comparison to run. Cost realism: Mesfin (2026b) test fourteen
intraday signal families against a fixed round-trip cost and find none surviving, and Wiecki et al.
(2016) report that reported backtest Sharpe ratios predicted out-of-sample results poorly across
888 strategies.

Human in the loop
A promotion committee (at least the portfolio manager, the risk owner and one researcher who
did not build the signal) reads a fixed one-page promotion pack and may only promote, return
or reject. The committee exists because the researcher who built a signal is the person least able
to count the alternatives that were tried and dropped.

Gaps
Practitioner guidance. The collection stops at the backtest; the following steps belong to a fund.
(i) Paper trading with a protocol. Run the production code path against live data with simulated
fills for a pre-set number of weeks, with exit criteria written before it starts. (ii) Staged capital.
Promote in steps, for example 10, 25 and 50 percent of target size, each step requiring that
realised cost and slippage match the model. (iii) Live-versus-backtest reconciliation. Re-run the
backtest on the live period with the live data and the realised fills, and decompose the difference
into data, signal, sizing and execution components.


                                                 133
Desk note. Promotion pack and reconciliation (validation stage; our protocol).
Inputs: the hypothesis note; the trial ledger with the number of labels, features, windows and
parameters tried; walk-forward results net of the fund’s own cost curve; the deflated Sharpe ratio
and the probability of backtest overfitting (Bailey and López De Prado, 2019; Bailey et al., 2013);
the placebo result in the manner of Nikolopoulos (2026); and the baseline comparison of Pandhe
(2026). Output: promote, return or reject, plus a stage-one capital figure. After promotion, each
week compute a four-way reconciliation: (a) data, the difference between the research and live
feature values for the same timestamps; (b) signal, the difference between backtest and live
scores on identical inputs; (c) sizing, the difference between intended and actual target positions;
(d) execution, realised minus modelled cost. Any component outside its tolerance halts further
capital steps. What can go wrong: the ledger is incomplete, the placebo does not match the real
data’s dependence, and the tolerances are set after seeing a disappointing week.


18.9      Stage 8: monitoring and decay detection
Components
Monitoring is thinly covered but not empty. Hassan et al. (2010) propose statistical process
control for trading systems, treating them as machines with variable outputs, which supplies
the control-chart vocabulary. Atluri (2026) motivate monitoring from the failure side: a meta-
labelling filter showed a structural inversion of the direction-to-profit relation during a drawdown.
On decay, Meng and Chen (2026a) formalise an alpha half-life that shortens as AI adoption rises,
Deng and Sammon (2026) and Unterberg (2026) link passive flows to weaker anomaly returns
and active-fund alpha, and Arnott et al. (2019) list crowding among the reasons for inflated
factor expectations. Break detectors (Habibi, 2022; Kalan et al., 2025) can monitor a signal’s own
error stream, and Ozimek (2026) compare strategies through regime-conditional distributions
of fold-level results, a useful reference distribution for live performance. Zovko (2026) give the
statistical lesson for in-flight cost monitoring: individual fills are too few to decide anything.

Human in the loop
A daily monitoring page is read by a person who has the authority to pause a sleeve. Alerts are
tiered, with the highest tier reserved for events that stop trading automatically. Reinstatement
after a halt always needs a human decision and a written reason.

Gaps
The central gap is that a decaying signal and an ordinary drawdown look the same over short
windows, and the collection offers no test that separates them. Practitioner guidance. Compare
live information coefficients, hit rates and cost-adjusted returns with the walk-forward distribution
from the promotion pack, using a sequential test (a CUSUM on standardised live residuals is
a simple choice) with a false-alarm budget set in advance. Track the signal’s crowding proxies
separately. Review capacity by comparing the fund’s share of volume in each name with the cost
curve of Stage 6, re-estimated as the book grows.




                                                134
Desk note. Kill-switch ladder (monitoring and risk; our design). Level 0, observe: an
alert is logged. Level 1, throttle: a sleeve’s risk multiplier falls (for example to half) when its
live drawdown, cost overrun or rejection rate leaves the promotion-pack range; this is automatic.
Level 2, stop opening: no new positions, exits only, triggered by sleeve loss limits, stale data,
failed reconciliation or a regime flag from the detectors of Stage 5; this is automatic. Level 3,
flatten: a human, or a fund-level loss limit, flattens the book through execution with pre-approved
algorithms. Level 4, disconnect: the broker or exchange kill facility, tested on a schedule. Design
rules: the switch lives outside the research stack; each level has a named owner; restoring risk
costs more approvals than removing it; every trigger is tested in the paper environment. What
can go wrong: thresholds too tight turn noise into turnover, de-risking by several sleeves at once
adds to impact, and an untested switch fails when needed. Among the papers, only a small set
discuss halts or circuit breakers, mainly at the exchange level (Abad et al., 2018; Jain et al.,
2016).


18.10      Stage 9: research automation with LLM agents
Components
Chapter 15 describes agents that mine formulaic alphas, read text, and in some cases trade.
For a fund the useful pattern is the narrow one. Shi et al. (2026) combine an LLM with a
domain-specific operator language and an AST execution sandbox, so that generated programs
cannot do arbitrary things; Tang et al. (2025) add regularised exploration to counteract alpha
decay in mining; Li et al. (2026c) replace free-form agent messages by structured, replayable
transport so that a hand-off is verifiable; Wang et al. (2026d) test whether generated code
behaves as the described strategy. The evaluation papers supply the audit layer: Roy and Roy
(2026) filter memorisation-contaminated signals by cross-model disagreement, Zhu et al. (2026c)
propose a memory-controlled benchmark with masking, and Ye et al. (2026a) argue headline
Sharpe ratios of LLM agents are not deployment evidence. Security is addressed by Na et al.
(2026), Sua et al. (2026) and Yan et al. (2025), and the measurement of agents in production by
Barton et al. (2026).

Human in the loop
Agents propose; people dispose. Our rule is that an agent may read data, write code in a sandbox
and submit candidates with a stated hypothesis, but may not modify the production feature
store, change a limit, or send an order. A person approves every artefact that enters production,
and an LLM used inside the live loop is an analyst whose output is a veto or a size-down flag at
most, never a size or an order.

Gaps
Practitioner guidance. (i) Trial ledger. Every candidate an agent generates is a trial; the ledger
must be written by the harness, not by the agent, so that memory modules that discard failures
cannot hide them from the deflation step. (ii) Permissions and secrets. Agents get read-only
credentials to research data and none for brokers. (iii) Reproducibility. Pin model versions and
record prompts, because an agent-built signal that cannot be regenerated cannot be audited.
(iv) Cost and latency. Inference cost and API availability are operating risks that the benchmark
papers do not price.




                                               135
18.11      Stage 10: governance
Components
Governance receives the least direct attention. Raschner (2021) examine, from a legal perspective,
the testing and simulation requirements for trading algorithms under MiFID II, and Hansen
(2025), drawing on 182 practitioner interviews, argue for attending to the whole technical stack of
automated trading systems rather than to algorithms alone. Jain et al. (2016) supply a framework
for assessing whether high-frequency quoting increases systemic risk, and Dixon (2026a) formulate
how much decision authority an AI agent should receive as evidence quality changes, which is a
governance policy in the literal sense. Pre-registration practices (Castellanos Macias, 2026) and
axioms stated before testing (Li, 2026) show how a fund can bind itself before it sees results.

Human in the loop
The governance body (in a small fund, the principals plus an independent reviewer) owns the
model inventory, approves exceptions, and reviews incidents. It is the only body that can raise a
limit.

Gaps
Practitioner guidance. (i) Model inventory and change control: a register of every live model with
owner, version, data dependencies, validation record, limits and retirement criteria; every change
goes through the promotion gate or a documented fast path. (ii) Incident process: a blameless
post-mortem for every breach or near miss, and a log that feeds back into the monitoring
thresholds. (iii) Segregation: the person who builds a signal does not promote it. (iv) Regulatory
compliance: venue and regulator rules on algorithmic trading (in India, those of SEBI and the
exchanges) must be checked by the fund’s compliance function; Gunnam et al. (2024) review
regulatory changes in derivatives trading, but none of the atlas papers is a compliance manual,
and nothing here is legal advice.


18.12      Worked examples
The two traces below follow a hypothetical strategy through every stage. They are illustrative
designs, not results, and every performance question in them is open until the gates above have
been passed.




                                               136
Desk note. Intraday index-options strategy (hypothetical). Idea: trade short-dated
Nifty option structures in the direction of an intraday forecast, with a volatility filter. 1 Data:
option chains, India VIX and spot bars, timestamped at ingestion; expiry calendar and rule
changes (Chhimwal et al., 2025; Gunnam et al., 2024); stale-strike checks as in Gupta (2018);
a simulated stress set from Noble et al. (2026) and Sun et al. (2026). 2 Features: model-free
implied volatility, smile level and slope, put–call ratio, residualised against a past-return baseline
(Wen et al., 2024; Guyon and Lekeufack, 2023); time-to-expiry flags, with intraday seasonality
removed (Krishnan and Mishra, 2013). 3 Signal: a direction forecast gated by regime state in the
manner of Sharma et al. (2026), labelled by repriced option profit and loss (Vaghela, 2026), with
a net-of-cost label in our recommended form. 4 Portfolio: fixed premium-at-risk per structure,
no optimiser; a cap on net vega. 5 Risk: short-gamma and expiry-gap limits informed by Duarte
and Jones (2008) and Sahu (2026); a volatility forecast challenged under Reinhard Hansen and
Lunde (2003); a change-point flag (Habibi, 2022). 6 Execution: limit-order placement for legs
with leg-risk rules (our guidance); cost model from realised fills and TCA (Markov, 2019). 7
Validation: placebo and trial count (Nikolopoulos, 2026; Bailey and López De Prado, 2019); a
simulated-null test like that of Pillai (2026). 8 Monitoring: control charts (Hassan et al., 2010);
the kill-switch ladder. 9 Automation: an agent may propose feature variants through a sandbox
(Shi et al., 2026); a person approves. Gaps that bite here: capacity in far strikes, margin, leg
execution, and the fact that the cited Indian studies are mostly descriptive.

Desk note. Daily cross-sectional equity strategy (hypothetical). Idea: a long–short book
on a liquid universe ranked by a blend of price and fundamental features. 1 Data: adjusted prices
with point-in-time index membership and delistings as in Darmanin (2026); corporate-action
table (our guidance). 2 Features: window-safe transforms (Hosking, 1981), a graph-relation
feature in the style of Liu et al. (2026a), features logged in the platform of Yang et al. (2020a).
3 Signal: labels chosen by ablation (Guida and Coqueret, 2026; Cakici and Zaremba, 2026); a
gradient-boosted ranker that must beat 12-1 momentum (Pandhe, 2026) and the mean benchmark
of Bandi et al. (2026); marginal contribution to the alpha pool (Yu et al., 2023). 4 Portfolio:
multi-period convex rebalancing (Boyd et al., 2017, 2024) with a cleaned covariance (Laloux et al.,
1999; Hiraki and Sun, 2022). 5 Risk: volatility scaling of the momentum sleeve (Barroso and
Santa-Clara, 2015), a two-level trade-or-abstain gate (Sanderink, 2026), drawdown limit (Mendes
and Lavrado, 2017). 6 Execution: participation schedule from a fitted impact curve (Rastegar
and Eghbalreihani, 2019; Zhou et al., 2026a); broker ranking (Markov, 2019). 7 Validation:
deflated Sharpe, effective sample size (Diao, 2026), superior-predictive-ability test (Hansen,
2005), then staged capital. 8 Monitoring: live information coefficient against the walk-forward
range, crowding proxies (Deng and Sammon, 2026), break detector on errors (Habibi, 2022). 9
Automation: synergy-oriented formula search (Yu et al., 2023) screened with a multi-dimensional,
backtest-free evaluation (Ding et al., 2026), confined to a quarantined window with a trial ledger
(our guidance). Gaps that bite here: borrow cost on the short leg, capacity of small-cap names,
and the live-versus-backtest reconciliation of Stage 7.


18.13       What the blueprint does not claim
Three limits deserve plain statement. The pipeline above is a synthesis, and no paper in the
collection tests an end-to-end fund. Several stage assignments are ours: papers were written for
one community and we have placed them where a fund might use them. And the gaps are gaps of
this collection, which reflects what the Zotero library holds; some of the practices we call missing
(corporate-action handling, reconciliation, incident management) are well known to practitioners
and simply do not appear in research papers. The main contribution of the blueprint is to
show where a mathematician, a machine-learning researcher or a trader can pick up a part of


                                                 137
the pipeline and know what it connects to (Chapter 17), and where the open problems that
Chapter 19 lists begin.




                                          138
Chapter 19

Gaps, Open Problems and Conclusion

The atlas was built to let people who do not read one another find one another. This closing
chapter does three things. It states a set of open problems, framed so that a mathematician or
machine-learning researcher can see the well-posed question inside the market vocabulary. It
measures what the collection over- and under-represents, using the records themselves. And it
says how the atlas should be maintained as the library grows. A short conclusion restates the
contribution.


19.1      Open problems at the silo boundaries
Each problem below sits where two or more silos meet, and each is stated as a question the
markets pose, with the part that is mathematically clean separated from the part that is empirical.
They are our selection, drawn from the gaps of Chapter 18 and the bridges of Chapter 17; the
papers cited show that the pieces exist, not that the problems are solved.
    1. Labels that close the gap between classification and profit. Market side: a
model with a better F1 score on a trade label can lose money after costs (Lima, 2026), and
the correlation between classification metrics and strategy performance is low (Kovačević et al.,
2022). Question: given a cost function and a decision rule, characterise the family of labels whose
Bayes-optimal classifier maximises expected net P&L, and bound the loss when the label is only
an approximation. The decision-focused learning literature (Mandi et al., 2024) is the natural
toolkit; the triple-barrier labels of the time-series community are special cases to be analysed.
    2. Inference with few independent observations. Overlapping labels leave about
twenty independent observations in one audited setting (Diao, 2026), and a handful of regime
episodes leaves fewer. Question: give finite-sample, assumption-light tests for the difference of two
strategies’ Sharpe ratios under overlapping labels, heavy tails and regime switching, with power
curves that practitioners can read. The deflation and probability-of-overfitting tools (Bailey
and López De Prado, 2019; Bailey et al., 2013) are asymptotic or simulation-based; sharper
small-sample theory would be a direct contribution.
    3. Detection delay against the cost of acting. Change-point detectors (Habibi, 2022;
Huang, 2025) and the Neyman–Pearson framing of Kalan et al. (2025) give false-alarm budgets,
yet a trading action costs turnover and impact. Question: formulate regime response as an
optimal-stopping or sequential-decision problem with an explicit cost of switching, and find the
detector threshold that is optimal for net P&L, not for detection accuracy. The stopping-problem
papers in Chapter 13 (for example Colaneri and De Angelis, 2022) suggest the continuous-time
setting.
    4. Learning with feedback from one’s own trades. Cristian et al. (2025) study learning
when decisions bias the data they later generate, and Bajpai (2025b) show that predictability need
not imply profit under feedback and convex costs. Question: what is the minimal experimental
design (randomised participation, small probes) that identifies the fund’s own impact curve


                                                139
without paying for the identification in cost, and how should a forecaster be trained on data it
has itself moved? Impact theory (Zhou et al., 2026a) supplies the structural model.
    5. Scoring generators by the decisions they support. The authors of Berti et al.
(2025) and Kwon and Lee (2024) both note the absence of agreed metrics for market generators.
Question: define a proper scoring rule, or a family of them, for synthetic paths and order flow so
that a lower score provably bounds the error in a downstream quantity (execution cost, drawdown,
or fill rate). The controlled benchmark of Sun et al. (2026) provides ground truth against which
candidates can be tested.
    6. Separating decay from drawdown. Meng and Chen (2026a) derive an alpha half-life;
Hassan et al. (2010) propose control charts. Question: design a sequential test that, given a
walk-forward distribution of a signal’s performance, detects a drop in its mean as early as possible
at a fixed false-alarm rate when returns are autocorrelated and volatility is time varying, and
quantify the expected delay.
    7. Calibrated confidence without exchangeability. Conformal intervals under-cover
precisely in high-volatility and regime cells (Taheri Hosseinkhani, 2026), and uncertainty-based
sizing can cancel an edge when uncertainty and signal strength are correlated (Sanderink, 2026).
Question: give coverage guarantees conditional on a volatility or regime state, under distribution
shift, that remain useful for position sizing. Online and adaptive conformal results (Noarov and
Roth, 2026; Zhang, 2026) are the starting point.
    8. Trial-counting for automated search. Agentic miners generate and discard many
candidates (Tang et al., 2025; Shi et al., 2026). Question: define the effective number of trials
of an adaptive search with memory, so that the deflated Sharpe ratio of Bailey and López De
Prado (2019) can be applied to its output, and detect contamination of signals by a language
model’s training data (Roy and Roy, 2026).


19.2      What the collection over- and under-represents
All counts in this section are computed from the 1065 records of the atlas, and the silo, stage and
relevance labels come from an automated classification of titles and abstracts. Market counts
come from keyword matches in titles and abstracts and are approximate, since a paper may
mention several markets or none.

By silo, year and relevance
The two largest silos, time-series machine learning (180 records, 16.9 percent) and deep learning
(176, 16.5 percent), together account for a third of the collection. Empirical finance and
econometrics holds 136 records (12.8 percent), microstructure 115 (10.8 percent), decision theory
and control 104 (9.8 percent), statistics 95 (8.9 percent), language models and agents 88 (8.3
percent), practitioner writing 55 (5.2 percent), mathematics 49 (4.6 percent), quantitative
derivatives 43 (4.0 percent) and other domains 24 (2.3 percent). The mathematicians and the
derivatives specialists, whose tools Chapters 6, 13 and 11 rely on most, are among the smallest
groups.
    The collection is young. Records dated 2026 number 330 (31.0 percent), and 500 (46.9
percent) are from 2025 or later, against 145 (13.6 percent) from 2015 or earlier and only 15 from
before 2000. The language-model silo is almost entirely recent: 83 of its 88 records date from
2025 or later, while microstructure and financial economics lean older, with 35 of 115 and 37 of
136 records respectively dated 2015 or earlier. A reader should therefore expect the fashionable
methods to be over-sampled relative to the slow-moving foundations, and should treat the many
2026 preprints as claims awaiting replication, as Chapter 14 urges.
    On relevance, 753 records (70.7 percent) were rated 3, 261 (24.5 percent) rated 2 and 51 (4.8
percent) rated 1, the peripheral class. Peripheral records concentrate in time-series methods (23)


                                                140
and general methods (15) by chapter. By silo, the language-model silo has the most in absolute
terms (13 of 88), but the other-domain silo has by far the highest share (11 of 24 records), which
is to be expected of imports from outside finance.

By pipeline stage and market
Table 2.1 in Chapter 2 shows how silos distribute over the pipeline stages. A record can carry
more than one stage label. The stage counts across the collection are: signal 288, forecast 206,
validation 167, risk 131, data 122, theory 115, features 79, execution 76, portfolio 68, regime
59 and automation 46. The pipeline is thus top-heavy: signal and forecast papers outnumber
execution and portfolio papers by roughly three to one. Execution is thin in the deep-learning
and time-series columns (one record each), where most of the new modelling talent sits, while
risk is led by financial economics (38) and microstructure (24), and validation by statistics (39)
and practice (30). Automation has only 46 records, 14 of them from the language-model silo.
    By market, a keyword scan of titles and abstracts finds United States references in 167
records (15.7 percent), India in 96 (9.0 percent), China in 70 (6.6 percent), cryptocurrencies
in 41 (3.8 percent), futures in 49 (4.6 percent), options in 78 (7.3 percent), commodities in 27
(2.5 percent), foreign exchange in 23 (2.2 percent) and bonds or rates in 13 (1.2 percent). Only
415 records match any of these keywords, so for most records the market is not stated in the
abstract. The Indian share is high for a global literature, reflecting the origin of the library, and
the Indian options and expiry studies of Chapter 11 are largely descriptive. Fixed income and
foreign exchange are nearly absent, as are practices that a fund needs but papers rarely discuss:
in the same scan only 7 records mention point-in-time data, 2 mention survivorship, and none
that we found estimates a strategy’s capacity. These are the gaps catalogued in Chapter 18.


19.3      Maintaining the atlas
The atlas is a snapshot of a Zotero collection, and the collection will keep growing. We propose
the following routine, all of it implementable with the scripts used to build this volume. (i)
Incremental classification: new items receive silo, stage, concept and relevance labels from the
same automated classifier, and a sample is checked by hand to estimate the label error rate that
this edition has not measured. (ii) Concept index refresh: counts per concept and per chapter are
recomputed, and a concept that crosses a size threshold or changes its silo mix triggers a review
of the relevant chapter. (iii) Bridge audit: each new item is checked against the bridge statements
of the chapter it joins, and an item that contradicts one is flagged for the author, since the
sceptical papers are the most informative. (iv) Gap tracking: the blueprint’s gap list is kept as a
table, and each addition that fills a gap (a capacity paper, a reconciliation protocol) is recorded
against it. (v) Retire and merge: duplicate records, such as the two versions of a walk-forward
study reported in Chapter 14, are merged, and preprints are updated when a published version
appears. (vi) Rebalance the intake: since the collection over-represents recent model papers,
searches should aim at the thin cells of Table 2.1: execution, portfolio and automation outside
the language-model silo, and the older econometric foundations.


19.4      Conclusion
First, a restatement of what the atlas can and cannot claim. Every statement about a paper rests
on its title and abstract, not on its full text, so methods, costs, universes and test periods are often
less certain than a reader of the full paper would know them to be. The silo, stage, concept and
relevance labels come from an automated classification of those abstracts and were not audited
by hand; every count in this volume is an estimate on a convenience sample. The reference list
carries authors’ surnames only, as recorded in the library. And the bridges, desk notes, blueprint


                                                  141
and open problems are our proposals: where a cited paper supplies a component, it supplies only
what its abstract reports, and no cited paper has tested the combinations proposed here.
    Research that a systematic fund can use exists in shards. A time-series-classification group
and an implied-volatility group can work for years on the same decision problem without sharing
a word. This atlas is a contribution in bridge-building: it reads 1065 papers into one vocabulary
of silos, pipeline stages and concepts; it shows chapter by chapter where a term in one community
is a term in another; and it re-reads the whole as the architecture of a semi-automated fund,
with humans where judgement is needed and with an explicit account of what the literature does
not yet supply.
    Three practical messages follow. For traders, the largest gains are likely to come from
borrowing validated components across silos (a label design from one chapter, a deflation test
from another) and not from a new architecture. For mathematicians and machine-learning
researchers, the markets offer well-posed problems with real data, real costs and unforgiving
evaluation, several of them listed above. For everyone, the evidence of Chapter 14 is that a result
is worth only what its protocol is worth, so the shared checklist (label, horizon, baseline, trial
count, cost) is the cheapest common language we can offer. The atlas is a map, and it is meant
to be redrawn as the territory is explored.
